# Mīmāṃsā — Complete Learning Session

> **Catalogue identity:** Philosophy Optional · Philosophy Paper I — Indian Philosophy · Syllabus item 18.
> **Official syllabus wording (verbatim, printed spelling retained):** `Mimamsa: Theory of Knowlegde.`
> **Terminology rule:** the English concept leads; the accurate Sanskrit term in IAST follows immediately in brackets.
> **Evidence discipline:** doctrine, textual attribution and previous-year wording follow the repository's canonical owners. Facts are separated from interpretation, and genuinely contested matters are reported as contested. Model answers are independent learner practice and are never presented as official keys.

## BASIC LEARNING SESSION

### How this Basic block is organised

The nineteen sessions below run in teaching order, not in source order. Each session opens with a
definition, gives a gateway idea in plain language, shows the concept before it argues it, then
supplies the named evidence, the answer-grabbing line, the must-write keywords, a worked
answer-paragraph model, the exam route, the traps and a rapid revision strip.

Everything needed for a complete 10-, 15- or 20-mark answer on any of the fourteen previous-year
questions owned by this syllabus item is inside this Basic block. The Optional Advanced block near
the end of this document is enrichment; **no core answer depends on it.**

The printed syllabus clause for this item is three words long — `Mimamsa: Theory of Knowlegde.` —
and that brevity is a trap rather than a mercy. Mīmāṃsā's theory of knowledge is not a free-standing
epistemology bolted onto a ritual manual. It is an epistemology **built to secure supersensible duty
without a God**, and every one of its famous doctrines exists because that project needed it. The
sessions therefore follow the system's own load-bearing order: first the project, then the
instruments, then the theory of error and self-awareness that the instruments force, then the
philosophy of language that the scripture forces, then the authorlessness and injunction-theory that
the duty forces, and only then the metaphysics of self, liberation and the rejected creator.

| Session | What it settles | Why it comes here |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | The project: inquiry into duty (**dharma-jijñāsā**) and why epistemology serves it | Fixes the purpose that explains every later doctrine |
| 2 | Textual lineage and the Pūrva/Uttara ownership boundary | Separates what Mīmāṃsā owns from what Vedānta owns |
| 3 | The two sub-schools — Bhāṭṭa and Prābhākara — and why the split matters | UPSC almost never asks generic Mīmāṃsā |
| 4 | Intrinsic validity (**svataḥ-prāmāṇya**): thesis, fourfold formulation, argument | The system's foundation stone |
| 5 | The extrinsic rival (**parataḥ-prāmāṇya**), the dream objection, Sāṃkhya and Buddhist positions | The 2025 comparison and the standard critical paragraph |
| 6 | The map of knowledge-sources: why six for Bhāṭṭa and five for Prābhākara | The irreducibility principle that governs the whole list |
| 7 | Perception, inference, comparison and testimony as Mīmāṃsā states them | The four shared instruments, with the Nyāya contrasts |
| 8 | Why memory is not a means of valid knowledge | The 2020 and 2024 questions, and the novelty condition |
| 9 | Postulation (**arthāpatti**): structure, the stout Devadatta case, irreducibility | The 2022 and 2024 questions |
| 10 | Non-cognition (**anupalabdhi**) and the reality of absence (**abhāva**) | The 2021, 2023 and 2025 questions |
| 11 | Theories of error: the four rival accounts, stated precisely | The 2022 twenty-marker |
| 12 | How cognition is known: threefold awareness, knownness, after-cognition | The 2024 twenty-marker |
| 13 | Does self-luminosity entail intrinsic validity? | The 2018 twenty-marker, settled by an intra-Mīmāṃsā counterexample |
| 14 | Sentence-unity and the two rival semantics | The 2024 ten-marker |
| 15 | Universals or particulars, exclusion (**apoha**), and the rejected sound-burst (**sphoṭa**) | The 2023 fifteen-marker |
| 16 | The authorless Veda (**apauruṣeyatva**) and the circularity charge | The 2019 ten-marker |
| 17 | Injunction, impelling force (**bhāvanā**), obligation (**niyoga**), unseen potency (**apūrva**), duty and the hermeneutic canons | Where the epistemology becomes an ethics |
| 18 | Mīmāṃsā without God (**nirīśvaravāda**) | The school's most counter-intuitive and most examinable position |
| 19 | Self, liberation, inter-school debates, criticisms and the graded verdict | The evaluative close every high-mark answer needs |

> **One-line map of the whole topic.** Mīmāṃsā secures a supersensible duty by making cognition
> innocent until proven guilty, by admitting every irreducible way of knowing, by explaining error
> without surrendering that innocence, and by grounding scripture in the absence of an author rather
> than in the perfection of one.

---
### SESSION 1 — THE PROJECT: INQUIRY INTO DUTY, AND WHY EPISTEMOLOGY SERVES IT

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition.** Earlier Mīmāṃsā (**pūrva-mīmāṃsā**) is the school that asks one
question and builds an entire theory of knowledge to answer it: *how can a human being know what he
ought to do, when what he ought to do cannot be seen, touched or inferred from anything visible?*

**Technical definition.** Pūrva-Mīmāṃsā is **inquiry into duty (dharma-jijñāsā)**. Its controlling
formula is **codanā-lakṣaṇo'rtho dharmaḥ** — duty (**dharma**) is that good which has Vedic
injunction (**codanā**) as its distinguishing mark. Because duty so defined is supersensible, the
school's epistemology is inseparable from its hermeneutics: a means of valid knowledge
(**pramāṇa**) is never discussed in the abstract alone, but always in relation to the *kind of
object* it can disclose.

#### SIMPLE START — THE GATEWAY IDEA

Think of a legal system with no legislature and no judge, only a statute book that nobody wrote and
nobody can amend. Everything that matters — what is obligatory, what is forbidden, what counts as
correct performance — must be read out of the text itself. There is no author to consult about
intention, no sovereign to appeal to for clarification, and no divine enforcer to guarantee that the
text is true.

If that is your situation, two things become urgent at once. First, you must show that the text
carries authority *by itself*, since there is nobody behind it to lend authority to it. Second, you
must show that reading and knowing are generally reliable, since if cognition in general needed
external certification, the reading of that statute book would need it too — and there is nothing
outside to supply it.

That is the whole of Mīmāṃsā in one image. Its famous doctrines — intrinsic validity, the six
knowledge-sources, the theories of error, the eternal word, the impersonal injunction, the unseen
potency, the rejection of a creator — are not a shopping list. They are the load-bearing walls of a
single building whose purpose is to make an authorless command knowable and binding.

#### VISUAL — WHY EACH DOCTRINE EXISTS

```text
   THE PROBLEM ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   Duty (dharma) is supersensible: no perception reaches it, no inference has a sign     │
   for it, and no human speaker can be trusted about it.                                 │
   └──────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                      │
                     "Then only verbal testimony (śabda) can reach it."
                                      │
        ┌─────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┐
        │                                                           │
   BUT: whose word?                                          BUT: why trust
   ── if a person's, he can err, deceive, forget                  any cognition at all?
        │                                                           │
        ▼                                                           ▼
   AUTHORLESSNESS (apauruṣeyatva)                        INTRINSIC VALIDITY (svataḥ-prāmāṇya)
   remove the author, remove the defect-locus            cognition is authoritative on arrival;
        │                                                falsity comes from outside, from defect
        │                                                           │
        └─────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┘
                                  ▼
                    A TEXT THAT BINDS WITHOUT A COMMANDER
                    injunction (vidhi) · impelling force (bhāvanā) / obligation (niyoga)
                                  ▼
                    AN ACT THAT PAYS WITHOUT A PAYMASTER
                    unseen ritual potency (apūrva) carries the perishing act to its later fruit
                                  ▼
                    ⇒ NO CREATOR GOD IS NEEDED ANYWHERE IN THE CHAIN (nirīśvaravāda)
```

*What the diagram shows: every celebrated Mīmāṃsā doctrine is a repair to a specific hole that opens
the moment you decide that duty is supersensible and that no person may be trusted with it. Read the
column downwards and the school stops looking like a ritual curiosity and starts looking like a
tightly argued anti-sceptical system.*

#### CORE UPSC — THE ARGUMENT IN FULL

1. **The school begins from duty, not from being.** Jaimini's opening inquiry is into what ought to
   be done, not into what metaphysically is the case. The orientation is action-guiding rather than
   speculative-theological, and this single fact explains why Mīmāṃsā develops a philosophy of
   language and a theory of injunction that no purely metaphysical school needs.

2. **Instruments are matched to objects.** Perception (**pratyakṣa**) reveals what is presently
   sensible. Inference (**anumāna**) extends beyond the present where a reliable relation is already
   established. Neither reaches duty, ritual efficacy, or a fruit that will mature after death.

3. **Therefore verbal testimony (śabda) becomes central, and specifically Vedic testimony.** In
   Mīmāṃsā, testimony is *not* a fallback used when perception fails. It is the **only** route to
   supersensible duty, which is why the school gives it a philosophical dignity that Nyāya, for all
   its acceptance of testimony, never quite gives it.

4. **Defending the Veda therefore requires defending cognition itself.** If a cognition were
   suspect until certified from outside, Vedic cognition would hang on some further authority — and
   that authority would need certifying in turn. Mīmāṃsā refuses the dependence at the root by
   holding that validity is intrinsic (**svataḥ-prāmāṇya**) and invalidity extrinsic.

5. **The minimum metaphysics is deliberately sparse but strong enough to carry the epistemology.**
   The school is broadly **realist**: enduring objects stable enough to be known, named and acted
   upon; a **plurality of selves**, each a knower, agent and enjoyer; moral and ritual
   responsibility that therefore makes sense; and a moral order regulated by action and unseen
   potency rather than by a divine dispenser.

6. **Three enabling propositions do the work.** Knowledge is possible because objects are real.
   Ritual obligation is possible because injunction is meaningful. Future fruit is possible because
   action leaves an unseen potency. Remove any one and the system loses a wall.

7. **Error must be exceptional, not constitutive.** A school that lets falsity into the normal
   structure of cognition cannot leave the ground floor; the ritual instruction it exists to protect
   would collapse into permanent uncertainty. Hence Mīmāṃsā's theories of error are engineered to
   quarantine error rather than to generalise it — a point Session 11 develops.

8. **The result is a general epistemic realism, not a ritualist's convenience.** ⚠️ Read narrowly,
   the school looks like ritual apologetics. Read philosophically, **svataḥ-prāmāṇya** is a fully
   general theory of default epistemic entitlement that happens also to secure scripture. Both
   readings have textual support; the second is the one that earns marks.

#### NAMED EVIDENCE YOU CAN CITE

- ✅ **Jaimini, *Mīmāṃsā-sūtra*** — organises the inquiry into duty and Vedic interpretation; the
  source of the formula **codanā-lakṣaṇo'rtho dharmaḥ**.
- ✅ **Śabara, *Śābara-bhāṣya*** — the foundational surviving commentary through which the aphorisms
  are classically read.
- ✅ **Kumārila Bhaṭṭa, *Ślokavārttika* and *Tantravārttika*** — the Bhāṭṭa line's epistemology,
  semantics and polemic.
- ✅ **Prabhākara Miśra, *Bṛhatī*** — the Prābhākara line, with **Śālikanātha** as a major later
  expositor.
- ⚠️ Standard modern presentations — Chatterjee and Datta, C. D. Sharma, Radhakrishnan — supply the
  survey vocabulary in which UPSC questions are usually phrased.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Mīmāṃsā's theory of knowledge is not an epistemology that happens to defend a scripture; it is a
> scripture-defence that had to become a general epistemology, because a text with no author can
> borrow no authority from anywhere outside cognition itself.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

| Keyword | Why the examiner wants it |
|---|---|
| **inquiry into duty (dharma-jijñāsā)** | Names the school's project in its own vocabulary |
| **codanā-lakṣaṇo'rtho dharmaḥ** | The defining formula; one Sanskrit clause worth several sentences |
| **Vedic injunction (codanā / vidhi)** | Marks duty as *commanded*, not discovered |
| **supersensible (atīndriya) object** | Explains why testimony is not a fallback here |
| **intrinsic validity (svataḥ-prāmāṇya)** | The load-bearing epistemic thesis |
| **realism about objects and selves** | The presupposition without which the thesis is subjectivism |
| **unseen potency (apūrva)** | The bridge from perishing act to later fruit |

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — CLAIM -> NAMED EVIDENCE -> ANALYSIS -> QUALIFICATION

> **Claim.** Mīmāṃsā's epistemology is best understood as instrumental to its practical project:
> the school theorises knowledge because it must secure a duty that nothing visible can disclose.
>
> **Named evidence.** Jaimini opens the *Mīmāṃsā-sūtra* with the inquiry into duty and defines duty
> by its distinguishing mark, Vedic injunction — **codanā-lakṣaṇo'rtho dharmaḥ** — and Śabara's
> commentary reads the whole system through that clause; Kumārila and Prabhākara then divide over
> *how* the injunction binds without dividing over *that* it binds.
>
> **Analysis.** Because duty is supersensible, perception and inference are structurally
> disqualified, testimony becomes the sole competent instrument, and the reliability of testimony
> cannot be borrowed from a speaker who does not exist. The school is therefore forced upward from
> hermeneutics into general epistemology, and it arrives at intrinsic validity not as a pious
> preference but as the only position that does not generate an infinite regress of certifiers.
>
> **Qualification.** ⚠️ The dependence runs both ways, and that is also the school's exposure: if
> the general theory of cognition fails, the defence of scripture fails with it. Mīmāṃsā's
> epistemology and its scriptural authority rise and fall together, which is exactly why Nyāya
> attacks the epistemology rather than the scripture.

#### EXAM APPLICATION — WHERE THIS HAS BEEN ASKED

- **2019, Q5(e), 10 marks** — "How does Mīmāṃsā establish the authority of Vedic knowledge?" The
  framing paragraph of that answer is this session; the machinery is Sessions 4 and 16.
- Every 15- and 20-mark Mīmāṃsā answer can open from here, because stating the project in one
  sentence immediately explains why the doctrine under examination exists at all.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Writing that Mīmāṃsā "is only ritualism." The ritual orientation is real; the epistemology is
   general. Say both.
2. ⚠️ Treating **śabda** as a secondary or emergency source. In Mīmāṃsā it is the *only* competent
   source for its own proper object.
3. ⚠️ Confusing **pūrva-mīmāṃsā** with **uttara-mīmāṃsā** (Vedānta). Session 2 fixes the boundary.
4. ⚠️ Saying the school "has no metaphysics." It has a deliberately minimal but load-bearing realism
   about objects, selves and moral order.
5. ⚠️ Importing God anywhere into the chain. Session 18 shows the school removes God *on purpose*.

#### RAPID REVISION

- Project = **dharma-jijñāsā**; formula = **codanā-lakṣaṇo'rtho dharmaḥ**.
- Duty is supersensible ⇒ testimony is the only competent instrument ⇒ scripture must be self-valid
  ⇒ cognition in general must be self-valid.
- Minimum metaphysics: real objects, plural enduring selves, agent-enjoyer identity, unseen potency,
  beginningless moral order.
- Error must remain exceptional or the whole instructional project collapses.
- Lineage: Jaimini → Śabara → Kumārila / Prabhākara.

---

### SESSION 2 — TEXTUAL LINEAGE AND THE PŪRVA / UTTARA OWNERSHIP BOUNDARY

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition.** "Mīmāṃsā" means reverential investigation. The word names two
different investigations of the same corpus: the **earlier** one (**pūrva-mīmāṃsā**) investigates
the action-guiding, ritual portion and asks what must be done; the **later** one
(**uttara-mīmāṃsā**, that is, **Vedānta**) investigates the Upaniṣadic portion and asks what
ultimately is.

**Technical definition.** Pūrva-Mīmāṃsā owns injunction, duty, authorless verbal authority and
ritual interpretation; Uttara-Mīmāṃsā owns the Brahman-centred interpretation of the Upaniṣads. The
traditions are historically related and share the premise of Vedic authority, but ⚠️ Vedānta must
not be allowed to absorb Mīmāṃsā's independent ownership of the four items just listed.

#### SIMPLE START — THE GATEWAY IDEA

Two commentators are handed the same library. One turns to the volumes that tell people what to do
and asks, "What exactly is being commanded, by what rule do I read it, and how does an act performed
now pay out later?" The other turns to the volumes that describe what is, and asks, "What is the one
reality these sentences are pointing at?"

They are not rivals about the library's authority — both accept it absolutely. They are rivals about
what the library is *for*. That single disagreement generates almost every Mīmāṃsā–Vedānta clash you
will ever be asked about, including whether a sentence like "you are that" (**tat tvam asi**) needs
to be reinterpreted as supporting an injunction.

#### VISUAL — THE LINEAGE AND THE BOUNDARY

```text
                    THE VEDIC CORPUS
                            │
     ┌──────────────────────┴───────────────────────┐
     │                                              │
  KARMA-KĀṆḌA (action portion)              JÑĀNA-KĀṆḌA (knowledge portion)
     │                                              │
  PŪRVA-MĪMĀṂSĀ                              UTTARA-MĪMĀṂSĀ = VEDĀNTA
  "what ought to be done"                    "what ultimately is"
     │                                              │
  ┌──┴──────────────────────────────────┐        (owned by syllabus item 19)
  │  JAIMINI — Mīmāṃsā-sūtra            │
  │     inquiry into duty; interpretation│
  │            ↓                         │
  │  ŚABARA — Śābara-bhāṣya              │
  │     foundational surviving commentary│
  │            ↓                         │
  │      ┌─────┴──────┐                  │
  │  KUMĀRILA        PRABHĀKARA          │
  │  Ślokavārttika   Bṛhatī              │
  │  Tantravārttika    ↓                 │
  │      │           ŚĀLIKANĀTHA         │
  │  = BHĀṬṬA line   = PRĀBHĀKARA line   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┘

  OWNED HERE, NOT BY VEDĀNTA:  injunction · duty · authorless verbal authority · ritual exegesis
  OWNED THERE, NOT HERE:       Brahman · māyā · superimposition · identity-sentences as final
```

*What the diagram shows: a single trunk with two branches of inquiry, and a two-generation
commentarial ladder inside the earlier branch that ends in the two rival sub-schools whose quarrel
supplies most examinable Mīmāṃsā content.*

#### CORE UPSC — THE ARGUMENT IN FULL

1. **Jaimini's *Mīmāṃsā-sūtra* is the base text.** It organises the inquiry into duty and into the
   rules by which Vedic sentences are interpreted. It is a manual of *how to read a command*, and
   its epistemology is embedded in that task.

2. **Śabara's *Śābara-bhāṣya* is the foundational surviving commentary.** ⚠️ This matters for source
   control: much of what a candidate calls "Jaimini's view" is in fact reached through Śabara. Say
   "as read through Śabara" when the point is commentarial.

3. **Kumārila Bhaṭṭa establishes the Bhāṭṭa line** through the *Ślokavārttika*, the *Tantravārttika*
   and related works — six knowledge-sources, the reality of absence, contrary-apprehension in
   error, knownness as the route to knowing that one knows, and word-priority in semantics.

4. **Prabhākara Miśra establishes the Prābhākara line** through the *Bṛhatī*, with **Śālikanātha**
   as a major later expositor — five knowledge-sources, no separate instrument for absence,
   non-discrimination in error, threefold self-luminous awareness, and sentence-priority in
   semantics.

5. **Textual chronology is debatable; doctrinal ownership is not.** ❓ Dates, the relative priority
   of Kumārila and Prabhākara, and the internal layering of the commentaries are genuinely disputed
   in scholarship. What the examination requires is the *doctrinal* map: Jaimini–Śabara supply the
   shared frame; Kumārila and Prabhākara build rival epistemic and semantic architectures on it.

6. **The ownership firewall has a practical payoff.** When a question mentions "the Mīmāṃsakas"
   alongside "the Advaita Vedāntins" — as the 2018 twenty-marker does — you are being asked to keep
   two Vedic schools apart, not to blend them. When a question says only "Mīmāṃsā," you must still
   name the sub-school whose doctrine you are stating.

7. **Where the boundary is genuinely contested.** ⚠️ Pūrva- and Uttara-Mīmāṃsā share vocabulary
   (**vidhi**, **arthavāda**, **ekavākyatā**, **apauruṣeyatva**) and the later Vedāntic use of
   Mīmāṃsā hermeneutics is real. Report the sharing; do not let it dissolve the difference in
   purpose, which is what actually gets tested.

#### NAMED EVIDENCE YOU CAN CITE

- ✅ Jaimini, *Mīmāṃsā-sūtra*; Śabara, *Śābara-bhāṣya*.
- ✅ Kumārila Bhaṭṭa, *Ślokavārttika*, *Tantravārttika*; Prabhākara Miśra, *Bṛhatī*; Śālikanātha as
  later Prābhākara expositor.
- ✅ The doctrinal boundary statement: Pūrva-Mīmāṃsā owns injunction, duty, authorless verbal
  authority and ritual interpretation; Uttara-Mīmāṃsā owns the Brahman-centred reading of the
  Upaniṣads.
- ⚠️ Chronology and attribution details are reported by the tradition and by modern scholarship with
  varying confidence; keep them as background, not as assertions.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Pūrva- and Uttara-Mīmāṃsā are not two levels of one doctrine but two answers to the question of
> what a scripture is for; the earlier school reads the Veda as a command and the later reads it as
> a disclosure, and almost every quarrel between them is a consequence of that one difference.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

| Keyword | Why the examiner wants it |
|---|---|
| **earlier inquiry (pūrva-mīmāṃsā)** | Correct self-designation of the school |
| **later inquiry (uttara-mīmāṃsā) / Vedānta** | Names the boundary partner precisely |
| **action portion (karma-kāṇḍa)** | Locates the school's textual base |
| **Jaimini · Śabara · Kumārila · Prabhākara · Śālikanātha** | Attribution precision |
| **Bhāṭṭa line / Prābhākara line** | The two architectures, not two moods |
| **ownership firewall** | Prevents Vedāntising the answer |

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — CLAIM -> NAMED EVIDENCE -> ANALYSIS -> QUALIFICATION

> **Claim.** Mīmāṃsā's doctrinal identity is fixed by a lineage and by a boundary: a two-stage base
> that both sub-schools share, and a firm separation from the Vedānta that reads the same corpus for
> a different purpose.
>
> **Named evidence.** Jaimini's *Mīmāṃsā-sūtra*, read through Śabara's *Śābara-bhāṣya*, supplies the
> shared frame; Kumārila's *Ślokavārttika* and Prabhākara's *Bṛhatī*, extended by Śālikanātha, build
> the two rival systems on it; and the traditional division of labour assigns injunction, duty,
> authorless verbal authority and ritual exegesis to the earlier inquiry.
>
> **Analysis.** The lineage explains the shape of the doctrine. Because the base text is a manual of
> interpretation rather than a metaphysical treatise, both sub-schools inherit an obligation to
> explain sentence-meaning, and because both inherit the same defence of the text, their differences
> concentrate in epistemology and semantics rather than in scriptural loyalty.
>
> **Qualification.** ❓ The relative chronology of Kumārila and Prabhākara and the internal layering
> of the commentaries remain scholarly disputes; an answer should therefore assert doctrinal
> ownership confidently and historical sequence cautiously.

#### EXAM APPLICATION — WHERE THIS HAS BEEN ASKED

- **2018, Q8(a), 20 marks** — the question expressly ranges over Naiyāyikas, Mīmāṃsakas and Advaita
  Vedāntins; the boundary taught here is what keeps the three columns from bleeding into one another.
- Any question that says "according to Mīmāṃsakas" is implicitly asking you to name **which**
  Mīmāṃsakas.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Calling Kumārila's school "Jayarāśi Bhaṭṭa's" school. **Jayarāśi Bhaṭṭa** is the sceptical
   Cārvāka-adjacent author; **Bhāṭṭa** names the Kumārila line. The similarity of names is a genuine
   examination hazard.
2. ⚠️ Attributing to Jaimini what is available only through Śabara.
3. ⚠️ Letting "Mīmāṃsā" silently mean "Vedānta" because both accept the Veda.
4. ⚠️ Presenting contested dates as settled facts.
5. ⚠️ Writing "the Mīmāṃsā view" on error, absence or semantics without naming a sub-school — on
   every one of those three, there is no single Mīmāṃsā view.

#### RAPID REVISION

- Jaimini → Śabara → (Kumārila = Bhāṭṭa) and (Prabhākara → Śālikanātha = Prābhākara).
- Earlier inquiry = action portion, duty, injunction, exegesis. Later inquiry = Upaniṣads, Brahman.
- Firewall: injunction · duty · authorless verbal authority · ritual interpretation stay here.
- Bhāṭṭa ≠ Jayarāśi Bhaṭṭa.
- Assert doctrine confidently, chronology cautiously.

---

### SESSION 3 — THE TWO SUB-SCHOOLS, AND WHY THE SPLIT MATTERS

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition.** Mīmāṃsā comes in two working versions. Kumārila's version
(**Bhāṭṭa**) is willing to admit extra entities and extra instruments whenever a phenomenon refuses
to be reduced. Prabhākara's version (**Prābhākara**) is economical, and prefers to explain the same
phenomenon with machinery it already has.

**Technical definition.** The Bhāṭṭa sub-school accepts six means of valid knowledge including
non-cognition (**anupalabdhi**), defends contrary-apprehension (**viparīta-khyāti**) in error, denies
self-luminosity of cognition and explains awareness of cognition through the object's acquired
knownness (**jñātatā**), and holds connection-of-the-denoted (**abhihitānvayavāda**) in semantics.
The Prābhākara sub-school accepts five means, rejects a separate instrument for absence, defends
non-discrimination (**akhyāti / vivekākhyāti**), affirms self-luminosity through threefold awareness
(**tripuṭī-saṃvit**), and holds denotation-of-the-connected (**anvitābhidhānavāda**).

#### SIMPLE START — THE GATEWAY IDEA

Two engineers are given the same brief. The first says: "If a phenomenon behaves differently from
everything else on my list, it gets its own line on the list, even if the list grows." The second
says: "Adding a line is a confession of failure; find the phenomenon a home in what you already
have."

Both are good engineers, and both are building the same bridge — Vedic authority, realism, ritual
efficacy, intrinsic validity. But the first ends up with six instruments, a real category of absence
and a genuinely false cognition in illusion; the second ends up with five instruments, no separate
absence and no false cognition anywhere. ⚠️ Notice that the disagreements are not independent
eccentricities. Each follows from the same instinct applied to a different case.

#### VISUAL — ONE INSTINCT, SIX CONSEQUENCES

```text
                        SHARED MĪMĀṂSĀ COMMITMENTS (both sub-schools)
      Vedic authority · realism about objects and selves · ritual efficacy through unseen potency
      intrinsic validity of cognition · irreducibility as the test for admitting an instrument
      ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
                    │                                                   │
      BHĀṬṬA (Kumārila): admit what resists            PRĀBHĀKARA (Prabhākara): economise
      reduction, and pay the ontological cost          and avoid positing extra faculties
      ───────────────────────────────────────          ───────────────────────────────────────
  1.  SIX instruments of knowledge                     FIVE instruments of knowledge
  2.  absence (abhāva) is REAL, known by               absence is the bare locus, PERCEIVED;
      non-cognition (anupalabdhi)                      no separate instrument needed
  3.  error = contrary apprehension                    error = failure to DISCRIMINATE two
      (viparīta-khyāti): a real false cognition        valid cognitions; NO false cognition
  4.  cognition NOT self-luminous; inferred from       cognition IS self-luminous; knower,
      the object's knownness (jñātatā)                 known and knowing revealed together
  5.  semantics: words denote first, then connect      semantics: words denote only as connected
      (abhihitānvayavāda)                              (anvitābhidhānavāda)
  6.  motivation: the desired fruit moves the          motivation: the command itself binds
      qualified agent (bhāvanā-vāda)                   (niyoga-vāda) — duty for duty's sake
      ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
      ⇒ UNITY IN DIFFERENCE: both remain Mīmāṃsā because the shared row above never breaks.
```

*What the diagram shows: a single shared foundation, two opposite methodological instincts, and six
paired consequences that a good answer can reel off in order. Learning the table as a table is worth
more than learning six isolated facts.*

#### CORE UPSC — THE ARGUMENT IN FULL

1. **UPSC almost never asks for "generic Mīmāṃsā."** Of the fourteen previous-year parts owned by
   this syllabus item, six name Bhāṭṭa or Prabhākara explicitly, and several more are unanswerable
   without the distinction. Writing an unattributed "Mīmāṃsā view" of error, absence or sentence
   meaning is a factual error, not merely a stylistic weakness.

2. **The split is architectural, not superficial.** It reaches the accepted list of instruments, the
   explanation of absence, the explanation of illusion, the self-luminosity of cognition, the theory
   of sentence meaning, and the source of ritual motivation. Six different fault lines, one
   underlying tectonic difference.

3. **Kumārila's instinct: multiply where the phenomenon demands.** Kumārila is willing to admit an
   extra instrument (non-cognition), an extra ontological category (absence with its
   counterpositive), and an extra property (knownness) whenever collapsing them would distort what
   is actually cognised.

4. **Prabhākara's instinct: economise where a simpler explanation is available.** He explains
   absence by perception of the bare locus, error by non-discrimination, and knowledge of cognition
   by self-luminosity — in each case avoiding a new posit.

5. **Both keep the roof on.** Both accept the Veda's authorless authority; both are realists; both
   accept ritual efficacy through unseen potency; both accept intrinsic validity; both apply
   irreducibility as the admission test for an instrument. ⚠️ A high-scoring answer says this
   explicitly: it is **unity in difference**, and the unity is what makes the differences
   *intra*-school disputes rather than a schism.

6. **A warning about "economy."** ⚠️ It is tempting to score Prabhākara as the more rigorous thinker
   because his system is leaner. That is an evaluative judgement, not a doctrinal fact. Kumārila's
   reply is that economy purchased by distorting the phenomenon is false economy — precisely the
   argument he makes about absence in Session 10.

#### NAMED EVIDENCE YOU CAN CITE

- ✅ Kumārila Bhaṭṭa, *Ślokavārttika* — six instruments, reality of absence, contrary apprehension,
  knownness.
- ✅ Prabhākara Miśra, *Bṛhatī* — five instruments, bare-locus perception, non-discrimination,
  threefold awareness.
- ✅ The paired-consequence grid is the canonical Bhāṭṭa-versus-Prābhākara master table used across
  the previous-year corpus.
- ⚠️ Both sub-schools' accounts of liberation are late and comparatively thin; report this rather
  than manufacturing symmetry (Session 19).

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The Bhāṭṭa–Prābhākara dispute is not six quarrels but one: whether a philosopher should enlarge
> his inventory to fit the phenomenon or trim the phenomenon to fit his inventory — and every
> examinable difference between them is that single instinct applied to a fresh case.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

| Keyword | Why the examiner wants it |
|---|---|
| **Bhāṭṭa (Kumārila) / Prābhākara (Prabhākara)** | Correct attribution is half the mark |
| **irreducibility test** | The shared admission rule for instruments |
| **six versus five instruments** | The single most-tested numerical fact here |
| **unity in difference** | The evaluative sentence examiners reward |
| **economy versus fidelity to the phenomenon** | Frames the dispute philosophically |

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — CLAIM -> NAMED EVIDENCE -> ANALYSIS -> QUALIFICATION

> **Claim.** The internal division of Mīmāṃsā is systematic: a single methodological difference
> between admitting and economising generates the whole grid of Bhāṭṭa–Prābhākara disagreements.
>
> **Named evidence.** Kumārila's *Ślokavārttika* admits non-cognition as a sixth instrument, treats
> absence as a real content with a counterpositive, and posits knownness as a property of the
> cognised object; Prabhākara's *Bṛhatī* declines each posit, explaining absence by perception of
> the bare locus and cognition by its own self-luminosity in the threefold awareness.
>
> **Analysis.** Read together, the two systems show that the disputes are not independent: admitting
> non-cognition and admitting a genuinely false cognition are the same instinct, and declining both
> is the same economy. This is why the sub-school label predicts the position on every other
> question, and why examiners can safely pair "Bhāṭṭa" with "anupalabdhi" or "Prabhākara" with
> "akhyāti" in a single clause and expect the rest to follow.
>
> **Qualification.** ⚠️ The instinct-level explanation is an analytical reconstruction, not a
> statement either thinker makes about himself; and leanness alone does not establish superiority,
> since Kumārila's standing reply is that a system must be as large as the phenomena it explains.

#### EXAM APPLICATION — WHERE THIS HAS BEEN ASKED

- **2022, Q7(a), 20 marks** — why Prabhākara and Kumārila differ on erroneous cognition *despite*
  sharing intrinsic validity. The "unity in difference" frame is the question's actual demand.
- **2024, Q5(d), 10 marks** and **2025, Q6(c), 15 marks** — both are explicitly Bhāṭṭa-versus-
  Prābhākara questions.
- **2023, Q6(b)** and **2021, Q6(c)** — both require you to know which sub-school owns the doctrine.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ **Prabhākara accepts five, not six.** This is the single most repeated Mīmāṃsā error.
2. ⚠️ Assigning threefold awareness to Bhāṭṭa or knownness to Prabhākara. They are exactly reversed.
3. ⚠️ Assuming that because Prabhākara affirms self-luminosity he must also be the one who admits
   more instruments. He affirms self-luminosity *because* it is the economical move.
4. ⚠️ Presenting the split as a sectarian schism. Both remain orthodox Mīmāṃsakas.
5. ⚠️ Treating "Bhāṭṭa" as a personal name. It names the school of Kumārila **Bhaṭṭa**.

#### RAPID REVISION

- One instinct, six consequences: instruments · absence · error · self-luminosity · semantics ·
  motivation.
- Bhāṭṭa = 6, real absence, contrary apprehension, knownness, word-priority, fruit-motivation.
- Prābhākara = 5, bare locus, non-discrimination, threefold awareness, sentence-priority, command-
  motivation.
- Shared: Vedic authority, realism, unseen potency, intrinsic validity, irreducibility test.
- Say "unity in difference" once, explicitly.

---
### SESSION 4 — INTRINSIC VALIDITY: THESIS, FOURFOLD FORMULATION AND THE ARGUMENT

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition.** A cognition does not arrive neutral and then wait for a certificate.
It arrives already claiming to be true, and it keeps that claim unless something later defeats it.
Truth is the default setting; falsity is a fault imported from outside.

**Technical definition.** **Intrinsic validity (svataḥ-prāmāṇyavāda)** holds that a cognition is
valid by its own very occurrence under normal causal conditions: the same conditions that generate
the cognition generate its validity, and the cognition presents itself as trustworthy without a
further validating cognition. **Invalidity (aprāmāṇya)** is extrinsic in both respects — it arises
from a defect (**doṣa**) and becomes known through a sublating cognition (**bādhaka**),
contradiction or practical failure.

#### SIMPLE START — THE GATEWAY IDEA

Compare two legal systems. In the first, every person is guilty until a court proves them innocent.
In the second, every person is innocent until a court proves them guilty. Both systems admit that
guilty people exist; they differ about where the burden of proof sits before any court has spoken.

Mīmāṃsā runs the second system for cognitions. A cognition walks in with its rights intact. If you
want to strip it of authority, *you* must produce the defect or the contradiction. And the reason
Mīmāṃsā insists on this is not generosity. It is that in the first system no trial could ever begin,
because the court's own cognitions would themselves be under suspicion, and so on without end.

#### VISUAL — THE FOURFOLD FORMULATION AND WHERE EACH SCHOOL STANDS

```text
   Every theory of validity must answer FOUR questions, not one:

   ┌──────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┐
   │                              │  ORIGIN (utpatti)          │  ASCERTAINMENT (jñapti)    │
   │                              │  "what MAKES it so?"       │  "how is it KNOWN to be?"  │
   ├──────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
   │  VALIDITY (prāmāṇya)         │  MĪMĀṂSĀ: INTRINSIC        │  MĪMĀṂSĀ: INTRINSIC        │
   │                              │  same causes make the      │  cognition presents itself │
   │                              │  cognition and its truth   │  as trustworthy, unaided   │
   ├──────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
   │  INVALIDITY (aprāmāṇya)      │  MĪMĀṂSĀ: EXTRINSIC        │  MĪMĀṂSĀ: EXTRINSIC        │
   │                              │  needs a DEFECT (doṣa):    │  needs a DEFEATER (bādhaka)│
   │                              │  bad light, distance,      │  contradiction, correction,│
   │                              │  organ-damage, dream-state │  failure, defect-awareness │
   └──────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┘

   COMPARATIVE PLACEMENT (learn as a four-row strip)
     MĪMĀṂSĀ   validity intrinsic  ·  invalidity extrinsic      ← the position under study
     NYĀYA     validity extrinsic  ·  invalidity extrinsic      ← the chief rival
     SĀṂKHYA   validity intrinsic  ·  invalidity intrinsic      ← agrees on half
     BUDDHIST  validity extrinsic  ·  invalidity intrinsic      ← the exact mirror-image
     (Sāṃkhya and Buddhist placements follow standard survey presentation.)
```

*What the diagram shows: the doctrine is a claim about a two-by-two grid, and Mīmāṃsā occupies
exactly one cell-pattern in it. Candidates who memorise only "validity is intrinsic" lose the marks
that live in the other three cells — and lose the comparative strip that the 2025 question asks for
by name.*

#### CORE UPSC — THE ARGUMENT IN FULL

1. **What the thesis is a thesis about.** It concerns the epistemic status with which a cognition
   presents itself. ⚠️ It is *not* a claim about psychological confidence, and *not* a licence for
   dogmatic refusal to revise. It describes how a true cognition (**pramā**) initially functions.

2. **The core statement.** Every cognition carries *prima facie* validity unless and until defeated.

3. **The regress argument, in the school's own steps.**
   (i) A cognition is meant to disclose an object.
   (ii) If every cognition needed another cognition to validate it, the validating cognition would
   itself need validation.
   (iii) That generates an infinite regress (**anavasthā**).
   (iv) To avoid the regress, some cognition must be accepted as valid without external validation.
   (v) The most economical view is that *each* cognition is *prima facie* valid on its own.

4. **The argument from lived practice.**
   (vi) We in fact live by trusting cognitions unless a defeating condition appears.
   (vii) Practical life therefore confirms default trust rather than default doubt.
   (viii) Hence validity is intrinsic and invalidity adventitious.
   (ix) If later confirmation were required for *every* cognition, no first action could ever be
   rationally launched.
   (x) Therefore epistemic economy favours intrinsic validity.
   (xi) The burden of proof lies on the challenger, not on every fresh cognition.

5. **The presupposition that keeps it from collapsing into subjectivism.** ✅ The argument
   presupposes **realism**: cognitions are aimed at real objects and normally succeed in disclosing
   them, and normal causes tend toward truth rather than arbitrary fabrication. ⚠️ Without that
   background realism, "self-certification" would be exactly the vice critics accuse it of being.
   Mīmāṃsā wants object-directed cognition with default warrant, not a mind congratulating itself.

6. **The distinction that decides most answers.** ⚠️ **Intrinsic validity is not infallibilism.** It
   does not say every cognition is indefeasible; it says every cognition *begins* with *prima facie*
   authority. What is intrinsic is default validity, not guaranteed incorrigibility. This one
   sentence, written explicitly, is worth more than a paragraph of exposition.

7. **The worked example.** ✅ I see a pot on the floor. I do not suspend judgement pending a
   certificate; I walk around it, pick it up, or refer to it. Only if I later discover poor light,
   hallucination or contradiction do I revoke the cognition. The ordinary structure of experience
   already *is* the doctrine.

8. **Why the doctrine is central rather than incidental.** Mīmāṃsā must secure Vedic authority
   without God. If all validity were externally conferred, the Veda too would need an external
   certifier, and that certifier would need one. By making validity intrinsic, the school blocks
   scepticism at the starting point — and it does so with a general theory in which perceptual,
   inferential and verbal knowledge all participate alike. **The Veda is secure because cognition as
   such is secure.**

9. **Standing objection and standing reply.**
   ❌ *Objection:* if cognition is intrinsically valid, error should be impossible.
   ✅ *Reply:* error occurs when an **extrinsic defect** intervenes; intrinsic validity is only
   *prima facie* validity under normal conditions.

10. **Two further objections worth carrying into an answer.**
    ❌ *Intrinsic validity licenses gullibility.* ✅ It is defeasible and welcomes correction.
    ❌ *Later correction shows the invalidity was already intrinsic.* ✅ It shows only that a
    previously unnoticed defect was operating.
    ❌ *Why call a cognition valid before checking correspondence?* ✅ Because
    correspondence-checking must itself begin from some trusted cognition.

#### NAMED EVIDENCE YOU CAN CITE

- ✅ Kumārila Bhaṭṭa, *Ślokavārttika* — the classical Bhāṭṭa defence of intrinsic validity.
- ✅ Prabhākara Miśra, *Bṛhatī* — the Prābhākara defence, which reaches the same thesis by a route
  that never admits a false cognition at all (Session 11).
- ✅ The four-cell formulation of origin and ascertainment for validity and invalidity is the
  standard scholastic frame in which the doctrine is stated and compared.
- ⚠️ The Sāṃkhya (intrinsic–intrinsic) and Buddhist (extrinsic–intrinsic) placements are reported as
  they are standardly presented in survey literature; state them as the received comparison.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Intrinsic validity is Mīmāṃsā's refusal to let scepticism set the terms of the debate: it does not
> claim that cognition cannot be wrong, only that doubt is parasitic on a trust that must already be
> in place before any doubt can be raised.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

| Keyword | Why the examiner wants it |
|---|---|
| **intrinsic validity (svataḥ-prāmāṇya)** | The thesis itself |
| **origin (utpatti) / ascertainment (jñapti)** | Shows you know it is a four-cell doctrine |
| **defect (doṣa) / sublating cognition (bādhaka)** | The two extrinsic routes to invalidity |
| **infinite regress (anavasthā)** | The argument's engine |
| **defeasible default warrant** | The precise English of the thesis |
| **not infallibilism** | The distinction that most answers omit |
| **true cognition (pramā)** | Correct technical name for the valid product |

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — CLAIM -> NAMED EVIDENCE -> ANALYSIS -> QUALIFICATION

> **Claim.** Mīmāṃsā's intrinsic validity is a thesis about the burden of proof in epistemology: a
> cognition is entitled to be believed on arrival, and it is invalidity, not validity, that must be
> earned from outside.
>
> **Named evidence.** Kumārila's *Ślokavārttika* argues that if each cognition required another for
> validation, the validator would require a validator and an infinite regress (**anavasthā**) would
> follow; the school accordingly assigns intrinsic status to both the origin and the ascertainment
> of validity, while assigning defect (**doṣa**) and defeater (**bādhaka**) to invalidity alone.
>
> **Analysis.** The argument is doubly powerful because it is simultaneously anti-sceptical and
> anti-regress: it explains why practical life can begin at all, and it removes the need for any
> external certifier — which is exactly what a school defending an authorless text requires, since
> no such certifier is available to it.
>
> **Qualification.** ⚠️ The thesis buys its strength by relocating the difficulty: everything now
> depends on being able to identify a genuine defect or defeater, and Nyāya's standing complaint is
> that Mīmāṃsā has not shown how default trust differs from credulity when no defeater happens to
> present itself. ❓ Whether the doctrine truly escapes circularity or merely moves it into the
> notions of "defect" and "defeater" remains contested.

#### EXAM APPLICATION — WHERE THIS HAS BEEN ASKED

- **2025, Q5(c), 10 marks** — the main points of difference between intrinsic and extrinsic
  validation; the four-cell grid *is* the answer's skeleton.
- **2018, Q8(a), 20 marks** — whether self-luminosity entails intrinsic validity; you must have the
  precise thesis before you can deny the entailment (Session 13).
- **2022, Q7(a), 20 marks** — how two schools that both accept intrinsic validity nonetheless split
  on error (Session 11).
- **2019, Q5(e), 10 marks** — Vedic authority; this doctrine supplies half the mechanism.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Equating intrinsic validity with infallibility. It is *defeasible* default warrant.
2. ⚠️ Stating only the validity row and omitting the invalidity row. The doctrine is a pair.
3. ⚠️ Confusing intrinsic **validity** (svataḥ-prāmāṇya) with self-**luminosity**
   (svayaṃprakāśatva). Different questions, different answers (Session 13).
4. ⚠️ Attributing extrinsic validity to Buddhism *and* extrinsic invalidity to it. The standard
   Buddhist placement is extrinsic validity with **intrinsic** invalidity.
5. ⚠️ Forgetting the realism that the doctrine presupposes, and thereby making it sound like
   self-congratulation.

#### RAPID REVISION

- Grid: validity intrinsic in origin *and* ascertainment; invalidity extrinsic in both.
- Engine: infinite regress (**anavasthā**) + lived default trust + epistemic economy.
- Presupposition: realism. Distinction: default warrant ≠ incorrigibility.
- Strip: Mīmāṃsā I/E · Nyāya E/E · Sāṃkhya I/I · Buddhist E/I.
- One-line trap-killer: "*prima facie* validity, not infallibility."

---

### SESSION 5 — THE EXTRINSIC RIVAL, THE DREAM OBJECTION, AND THE COMPARATIVE STRIP

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition.** Nyāya's answer to the same question is the opposite one: a cognition
does not certify itself; you find out that it was true by acting on it successfully, or by a later
cognition that agrees with it.

**Technical definition.** **Extrinsic validity (parataḥ-prāmāṇyavāda)** treats both the origin and
the ascertainment of validity as extrinsic relative to the mere occurrence of the cognition. Its two
classical routes of verification are **successful practical activity (pravṛtti-sāmarthya)** and
**confirming later perception (saṃvādi-pratyakṣa)**.

#### SIMPLE START — THE GATEWAY IDEA

You see water shimmering in the distance. Nyāya says: walk over, touch it, drink it. If the action
succeeds, the cognition is certified. That is a perfectly sensible policy — it is roughly how
laboratories and law courts operate.

Mīmāṃsā's reply is not that testing is bad. It is that testing cannot be what *makes* a cognition
true, because you had to trust something in order to start walking. The very act that is supposed to
confer validity presupposes validity already in place. Nyāya, says the Mīmāṃsaka, is quietly living
on credit from a bank it claims not to have.

#### VISUAL — THE ATTACK, THE COUNTER-ATTACK AND THE DECISIVE DISTINCTION

```text
   NYĀYA'S TWO ROUTES                       MĪMĀṂSĀ'S THREE COUNTER-MOVES
   ─────────────────────────────────        ───────────────────────────────────────────────
   (1) successful activity                  (A) ACTION PRESUPPOSES TRUST
       (pravṛtti-sāmarthya)          ──►        to act on a cognition is already to rely on
       "I drank it, so it was water"            it; Nyāya uses what it postpones

   (2) confirming later perception           (B) REGRESS RETURNS
       (saṃvādi-pratyakṣa)           ──►        if cognition-2 validates cognition-1, what
       "I touched it, so I saw right"           validates cognition-2? and cognition-3?

                                             (C) THE DECISIVE DISTINCTION
                                                 VALIDITY ITSELF  ≠  ASCERTAINMENT OF IT
                                                 later success can DISCLOSE or CONFIRM
                                                 validity; it cannot GENERATE it
   ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
   NYĀYA'S BEST SHOT — THE DREAM / MIRAGE OBJECTION
     dreams and mirages arise spontaneously, feel entirely normal, and are FALSE
                        │
                        ▼
   MĪMĀṂSĀ'S REPLY — "APPEARANCE OF NORMALCY IS NOT NORMALCY"
     dream        → extrinsic defect: exhaustion, sleep-state, internal disturbance
     mirage       → extrinsic defect: heated air, optical distortion
     ⇒ these cognitions do NOT arise from fully normal truth-producing conditions
     ⇒ the principle survives: cognition under genuinely normal conditions is valid
```

*What the diagram shows: the dispute is not a clash of intuitions but a clash over one distinction.
If validity and the ascertainment of validity are the same thing, Nyāya wins; if they come apart,
Mīmāṃsā wins. Write that sentence and the comparison paragraph writes itself.*

#### CORE UPSC — THE ARGUMENT IN FULL

1. **State the rival fairly first.** Nyāya is the chief opponent on validity. In the contrast as it
   is taught, Nyāya holds that a cognition becomes accepted as valid by appeal to something outside
   itself — successful activity, or a later confirming perception.

2. **The Mīmāṃsā criticism, in its seven steps.**
   (i) If action-success is needed before validity is granted, one must act before one is entitled
   to trust the cognition.
   (ii) But that action itself presupposes some trust already.
   (iii) So Nyāya secretly relies on what it officially postpones.
   (iv) If a later cognition validates the earlier one, a further cognition must validate the
   validator.
   (v) The regress returns.
   (vi) Therefore later success can at most *confirm or disclose* validity, not *generate* it.
   (vii) Mīmāṃsā accordingly separates **validity itself** from our reflective **ascertainment** of
   it — and that separation is what weakens Nyāya's challenge.

3. **The dream and mirage objection, and why it is the strongest one.**
   ❌ Dream-cognitions and mirage-cognitions arise spontaneously and feel normal to the subject, yet
   they are false. If normal-*looking* cognitions can be false, why privilege intrinsic validity?
   ✅ The Mīmāṃsā reply distinguishes *appearance* of normalcy from *actual* normalcy: in dreams
   there is a defect such as exhaustion, sleep-state or internal disturbance; in mirages there is
   heated air and optical distortion. These are abnormal cases and therefore do not falsify a thesis
   about cognition produced under genuinely normal conditions.

4. **Sāṃkhya: the ally that goes too far.** ✅ Sāṃkhya is commonly represented as taking both
   validity *and* invalidity to be intrinsic — truth and falsity are each disclosed from within
   cognition. Mīmāṃsā agrees with Sāṃkhya against Nyāya on the intrinsic side of validity, but
   refuses to make invalidity equally intrinsic: falsity does not self-announce at birth; it is
   discovered through later defeat.

5. **Buddhism: the exact mirror-image.** ✅ Buddhist epistemologists are often presented as taking
   validity to be extrinsic — tied to causal efficacy or successful function — and invalidity to be
   intrinsic, revealing itself in failure. ✅ Mīmāṃsā rejects the asymmetry with a sharp empirical
   point: **if falsity were intrinsic, illusion should announce itself as illusion immediately — but
   illusion deceives precisely because it does not.**

6. **Default trust as the structure of embodied life.** ✅ Human life would stop if every cognition
   had to wait for prior certification. Crossing a road, reading a page, recognising a friend,
   obeying an instruction — all presuppose immediate trust. Mīmāṃsā converts this practical
   inevitability into an epistemic principle: **doubt is parasitic upon a more basic trust.**

7. **The distinctions the examiner is checking.** ✅ Validity ≠ certainty. ✅ Intrinsic validity ≠
   incorrigibility. ✅ Extrinsic invalidity means falsity arises from an external defect, not that
   error is impossible. ✅ Later confirmation is not the *source* of truth but one way of detecting
   correction or stability.

8. **Where the pressure genuinely remains.** ⚠️ Nyāya can press that Mīmāṃsā has drawn the line
   between warranted default trust and mere credulity by *stipulation*: the defect is invoked
   whenever the cognition turns out false, which risks making "abnormal conditions" a label applied
   after the fact. A critical answer should record this rather than declare a knockout.

#### NAMED EVIDENCE YOU CAN CITE

- ✅ The two Nyāya verification routes, **pravṛtti-sāmarthya** and **saṃvādi-pratyakṣa**, are the
  standard pair cited in the contrast.
- ✅ Kumārila's regress objection and the validity/ascertainment distinction are the Bhāṭṭa line's
  central replies.
- ✅ The dream–mirage objection with the defect-based reply is the canonical exchange.
- ⚠️ Sāṃkhya's intrinsic–intrinsic and the Buddhist extrinsic–intrinsic placements are reported as
  standard survey presentation rather than as the last word on either school.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Nyāya's test is a test of our knowledge that a cognition was valid, not of the validity it already
> had; once that distinction is drawn, successful action turns out to be evidence of truth rather
> than its manufacture — and the regress Nyāya feared reappears on its own side of the argument.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

| Keyword | Why the examiner wants it |
|---|---|
| **extrinsic validity (parataḥ-prāmāṇya)** | Names the rival exactly |
| **successful activity (pravṛtti-sāmarthya)** | Nyāya's first route |
| **confirming perception (saṃvādi-pratyakṣa)** | Nyāya's second route |
| **validity versus ascertainment of validity** | The move that wins the argument |
| **appearance of normalcy ≠ normalcy** | The reply to dreams and mirages |
| **doubt is parasitic on trust** | The anti-sceptical thesis in one clause |

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — CLAIM -> NAMED EVIDENCE -> ANALYSIS -> QUALIFICATION

> **Claim.** The Mīmāṃsā–Nyāya dispute over validation is decided by a single distinction between
> a cognition's validity and our subsequent ascertainment of it.
>
> **Named evidence.** Nyāya certifies cognition by successful activity (**pravṛtti-sāmarthya**) and
> by confirming perception (**saṃvādi-pratyakṣa**); Kumārila replies that acting on a cognition
> already presupposes trusting it, and that a validating cognition would itself need validation,
> restoring the infinite regress (**anavasthā**) Nyāya was meant to escape.
>
> **Analysis.** The reply does not deny that testing is epistemically useful; it re-describes what
> testing does. Confirmation discloses a validity the cognition possessed from the moment of its
> occurrence, which is why we can say a belief "turned out to have been true" rather than "became
> true" — and Nyāya's own practice of provisional reliance concedes the point in action.
>
> **Qualification.** ⚠️ The Mīmāṃsā victory is local rather than total: it shows that later success
> cannot *generate* validity, but it does not by itself show how to distinguish warranted default
> trust from credulity in cases where no defeater ever appears, which is where Nyāya's pressure
> continues.

#### EXAM APPLICATION — WHERE THIS HAS BEEN ASKED

- **2025, Q5(c), 10 marks** — "Delineate the main points of difference between intrinsic and
  extrinsic validation in classical Indian philosophy." This session and Session 4 are the whole
  answer; the four-school strip is what makes it "in classical Indian philosophy" rather than merely
  Mīmāṃsā-versus-Nyāya.
- **2018, Q8(a), 20 marks** — the Naiyāyika column of that three-column answer is built here.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Presenting Nyāya as denying that cognitions are ever true. It denies only that they certify
   themselves.
2. ⚠️ Forgetting that Nyāya has **two** routes, not one.
3. ⚠️ Reversing the Sāṃkhya and Buddhist placements — a very common slip.
4. ⚠️ Writing the dream objection without the defect-based reply, which converts a strong answer
   into a summary of the opponent.
5. ⚠️ Claiming Mīmāṃsā "refutes" Nyāya outright; the residual pressure about credulity is part of a
   critical answer.

#### RAPID REVISION

- Nyāya routes: successful activity · confirming perception.
- Mīmāṃsā counters: action presupposes trust · regress returns · validity ≠ ascertainment.
- Dream/mirage: appearance of normalcy is not normalcy; defect is extrinsic.
- Sāṃkhya I/I (ally, too far); Buddhist E/I (mirror-image, refuted by non-self-announcing illusion).
- Residual pressure: how is default trust distinguished from credulity?

---
### SESSION 6 — THE MAP OF KNOWLEDGE-SOURCES: WHY SIX, WHY FIVE, AND WHAT ADMITS ONE

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition.** A list of knowledge-sources is not a list of topics. It is a list of
structurally different *ways* a fresh true cognition can be produced. Mīmāṃsā's list is longer than
its rivals' because it thinks at least two ways of knowing cannot be squeezed into the others
without distortion.

**Technical definition.** A **means of valid knowledge (pramāṇa)** is the operative source of a
fresh true cognition; the resulting cognition is **pramā** or **pramiti**; its object-guided
consequence is practical engagement. Bhāṭṭa Mīmāṃsā accepts **six**: perception (**pratyakṣa**),
inference (**anumāna**), comparison (**upamāna**), verbal testimony (**śabda**), postulation
(**arthāpatti**) and non-cognition (**anupalabdhi**). Prābhākara Mīmāṃsā accepts the first **five**.

#### SIMPLE START — THE GATEWAY IDEA

Imagine a workshop with a rule posted on the wall: *no new tool may be bought if an existing tool
can do the job without damaging the work.* That single rule explains the whole disagreement about
list-length across Indian philosophy. Sāṃkhya thinks three tools suffice. Nyāya thinks four. The
Prābhākara thinks five. The Bhāṭṭa thinks six.

Nobody is counting casually, and nobody is inflating for prestige. The question in every case is
whether a particular cognition — knowing that Devadatta eats at night, knowing that there is no pot
on the table — can be produced by an existing tool *without remainder*. **Irreducibility**, not
enthusiasm, is the admission test.

#### VISUAL — THE COMPARATIVE LIST AND THE TWO CONTESTED SEATS

```text
                 pratyakṣa  anumāna  upamāna  śabda  arthāpatti  anupalabdhi
                 perception inference compar.  testim. postulation non-cognition
   ─────────────┬──────────┬────────┬────────┬───────┬──────────┬─────────────
   SĀṂKHYA      │    ✔     │   ✔    │        │   ✔   │          │              = 3
   NYĀYA        │    ✔     │   ✔    │   ✔    │   ✔   │          │              = 4
   PRĀBHĀKARA   │    ✔     │   ✔    │   ✔    │   ✔   │    ✔     │              = 5
   BHĀṬṬA       │    ✔     │   ✔    │   ✔    │   ✔   │    ✔     │      ✔       = 6
   ─────────────┴──────────┴────────┴────────┴───────┴──────────┴─────────────
                                              └──── THE TWO SEATS BY WHICH ────┘
                                                    MĪMĀṂSĀ EXCEEDS NYĀYA

   THE ADMISSION RULE (shared by both sub-schools)
   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ Admit a new instrument ONLY where a type of knowledge cannot be reduced       │
   │ to another instrument WITHOUT REMAINDER or WITHOUT CIRCULARITY.               │
   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                    perception ......... directly given objects
                    inference .......... sign-based mediated knowledge
                    testimony .......... verbally transmitted content
                    comparison ......... resemblance-based identification
                    postulation ........ resolves EXPLANATORY TENSION among known facts
                    non-cognition ...... discloses ABSENCE under fitness conditions
                                          ▲                    ▲
                    neither of these two arises through signhood
                    or through positive direct perception ⇒ irreducible

   AND ONE FAMOUS EXCLUSION:  MEMORY (smṛti) is NOT on any list — it reproduces, and a
                              pramāṇa must produce a FRESH cognition (novelty, anadhigatatva).
```

*What the diagram shows: three facts in one frame — the comparative counts, the exact two seats that
distinguish Mīmāṃsā, and the admission rule that justifies them. The fourth fact, memory's
exclusion, sits at the bottom because it is the negative application of the same rule and is
separately examined in Session 8.*

#### CORE UPSC — THE ARGUMENT IN FULL

1. **Why enumeration matters at all.** A list of instruments is a map of irreducible
   knowledge-sources. Schools differ because they disagree about whether a type of cognition can be
   reduced to another. ⚠️ Mīmāṃsā is therefore not "generous"; it is *defending irreducibility*.

2. **The one-line definitions you must be able to give.**
   ✅ **Perception:** immediate knowledge produced by sense–object contact.
   ✅ **Inference:** mediate knowledge arising from a sign through known concomitance.
   ✅ **Comparison:** fresh knowledge that a previously known but now absent object is similar to a
   presently perceived object.
   ✅ **Verbal testimony:** valid verbal testimony, especially Vedic testimony regarding duty.
   ✅ **Postulation:** necessary postulation of an unseen fact to reconcile incompatible known facts.
   ✅ **Non-cognition:** knowledge of absence through non-apprehension of what would be apprehended
   if present.

3. **The justification argument, in eight steps.**
   (i) Perception handles directly given objects.
   (ii) Inference handles sign-based mediated knowledge.
   (iii) Testimony handles verbally transmitted content.
   (iv) Comparison handles resemblance-based identification.
   (v) Yet some cognitions arise neither through signhood nor through positive direct perception.
   (vi) Postulation resolves contradiction-like tension among known facts.
   (vii) Absence-cognition, for Bhāṭṭa, discloses what is *not* there under proper conditions.
   (viii) If these cannot be reduced without circularity, they deserve independent status.

4. **What the list is *not* a list of.** ⚠️ Mīmāṃsā is not multiplying faculties psychologically. It
   is distinguishing **logical structures of knowing**. An instrument is identified not by its topic
   but by *the way the cognition is generated*.

5. **The three-term vocabulary you should use precisely.** ✅ The **instrument (pramāṇa)** is the
   operative source; the **true cognition (pramā / pramiti)** is what it produces; **successful
   action (pravṛtti-sāmarthya)** is the object-guided consequence. Successful action may *confirm*
   correspondence but, on Mīmāṃsā's view, does not manufacture validity after the cognition has
   arisen — which is precisely how this session connects back to Session 5.

6. **The link that earns marks in "knowledge of knowledge" questions.** ⚠️ Once instrument, product
   and confirmation are separated, the sub-school split becomes intelligible: Prābhākara says the
   cognitive episode manifests knower, known and knowing together; Bhāṭṭa infers the cognition from
   the object's acquired knownness. The three-term distinction is the bridge from Session 6 to
   Session 12.

7. **The summary formula.** ✅ Bhāṭṭa = six. ✅ Prābhākara = five. ✅ The difference turns on
   **non-cognition**. ✅ The whole list is governed by **irreducibility**, not by inflation.

#### NAMED EVIDENCE YOU CAN CITE

- ✅ The comparative table Sāṃkhya 3 / Nyāya 4 / Prābhākara 5 / Bhāṭṭa 6 is the canonical grid.
- ✅ Kumārila's admission of **anupalabdhi** and both sub-schools' admission of **arthāpatti** are
  the two seats by which Mīmāṃsā exceeds Nyāya's fourfold list.
- ✅ The **pramāṇa / pramā / pravṛtti-sāmarthya** three-term distinction is owner-stated and is the
  correct technical vocabulary.
- ⚠️ Later Advaita lists also run to six; do not use that to blur the Mīmāṃsā-specific argument for
  the two extra seats.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Mīmāṃsā's longer list of knowledge-sources is not epistemic generosity but epistemic honesty: it
> refuses to call a cognition "inference" merely because inference is already on the list, and it
> pays for that refusal with two extra entries it can independently justify.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

| Keyword | Why the examiner wants it |
|---|---|
| **means of valid knowledge (pramāṇa)** | The unit being counted |
| **true cognition (pramā / pramiti)** | The product, distinguished from the means |
| **irreducibility test** | The rule that licenses the count |
| **six for Bhāṭṭa, five for Prābhākara** | The single most-tested numerical fact |
| **the two extra seats: postulation and non-cognition** | Where Mīmāṃsā exceeds Nyāya |
| **novelty (anadhigatatva)** | Why memory is excluded |

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — CLAIM -> NAMED EVIDENCE -> ANALYSIS -> QUALIFICATION

> **Claim.** Mīmāṃsā's enumeration of knowledge-sources is governed by a single admission rule, and
> the school's distinctive length follows from applying that rule honestly rather than from any
> taste for multiplicity.
>
> **Named evidence.** Against Sāṃkhya's three and Nyāya's four, the Prābhākara admits postulation
> (**arthāpatti**) as a fifth and Kumārila admits non-cognition (**anupalabdhi**) as a sixth,
> because the cognition that Devadatta eats at night arises from explanatory tension rather than
> from a sign, and the cognition that no pot is on the table arises from qualified non-apprehension
> rather than from positive perception.
>
> **Analysis.** Because instruments are individuated by the *manner* in which the cognition is
> generated and not by subject matter, reduction must be resisted wherever the manner differs; and
> the same rule that admits two extra instruments simultaneously excludes memory, which reproduces
> rather than produces. The list is thus internally consistent in both directions.
>
> **Qualification.** ⚠️ Nyāya and the Prābhākara reply that the rule is being applied too
> liberally — Nyāya to both extra seats and the Prābhākara to the sixth alone — so the count itself
> is philosophically contested and should be presented as a defended position rather than as a
> neutral fact about Indian epistemology.

#### EXAM APPLICATION — WHERE THIS HAS BEEN ASKED

- **2022, Q6(c), 15 marks** — establishing postulation as an *independent* instrument requires this
  session's admission rule before Session 9's specific arguments.
- **2023, Q6(b), 15 marks** — presenting non-cognition as a valid means requires the same frame.
- **2020, Q5(c)** and **2024, Q8(c)** — memory's exclusion is the negative use of the same rule.
- Any comparative question benefits from the four-school count strip in a single line.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ **Prābhākara accepts five.** Writing "Mīmāṃsā accepts six" without qualification is wrong for
   half the school.
2. ⚠️ Saying Mīmāṃsā "rejects inference." It rejects **reductionism about inference**, which is a
   different thesis (Session 7).
3. ⚠️ Treating the list as a psychology of faculties rather than a taxonomy of logical structures.
4. ⚠️ Putting memory on the list because it is often true and often useful.
5. ⚠️ Forgetting that comparison in Mīmāṃsā is *not* the Nyāya word-reference account (Session 7).

#### RAPID REVISION

- Counts: Sāṃkhya 3 · Nyāya 4 · Prābhākara 5 · Bhāṭṭa 6.
- Extra seats: postulation (both sub-schools) and non-cognition (Bhāṭṭa only).
- Rule: admit only where reduction fails without remainder or without circularity.
- Vocabulary: instrument → true cognition → successful action; the last confirms, never creates.
- Memory excluded by the novelty condition (**anadhigatatva**).

---

### SESSION 7 — PERCEPTION, INFERENCE, COMPARISON AND TESTIMONY AS MĪMĀṂSĀ STATES THEM

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition.** The four instruments Mīmāṃsā shares with its rivals are not shared
*identically*. Each carries a Mīmāṃsā-specific twist, and each twist is examinable: perception is
realist and two-staged; inference is accepted but not allowed to swallow everything; comparison is
defined by a *similarity* cognition rather than by learning a word; and testimony is split into a
personal kind that can fail and an impersonal kind that cannot.

**Technical definition.** Perception (**pratyakṣa**) is immediate knowledge from sense–object
contact, occurring first as **indeterminate perception (nirvikalpaka-pratyakṣa / ālocana-jñāna)**
and then as **determinate perception (savikalpaka-pratyakṣa)**. Inference (**anumāna**) is standard
sign-based cognition through known concomitance (**vyāpti**). Comparison (**upamāna**) yields the
new cognition that a remembered, presently absent object is similar to a perceived one. Testimony
(**śabda**) divides into **personal (pauruṣeya)** and **impersonal (apauruṣeya)**, and by function
into **fact-stating (siddhārtha-vākya)** and **action-enjoining (vidhāyaka-vākya)**.

#### SIMPLE START — THE GATEWAY IDEA

Four familiar tools, four small but decisive modifications.

The first tool, perception, gets a two-stage handle: you first register *that something is there*,
and only then register *what it is*. Mīmāṃsā insists the second stage unpacks what the first already
held — it does not invent a false label.

The second tool, inference, is left almost untouched, which is itself the point: Mīmāṃsā has no
quarrel with inference, only with the claim that all mediate knowledge *is* inference.

The third tool, comparison, is re-aimed. Nyāya uses it to learn what a word means. Mīmāṃsā uses it to
learn that an absent remembered thing resembles a present perceived one. Getting this backwards is
one of the most reliable ways to lose marks on this topic.

The fourth tool, testimony, is split down the middle: a human speaker can be ignorant, partial or
forgetful, and an authorless text cannot. That single split is the hinge on which Session 16 turns.

#### VISUAL — THE FOUR SHARED INSTRUMENTS AND THEIR MĪMĀṂSĀ-SPECIFIC TWISTS

```text
  1. PERCEPTION (pratyakṣa) ─ realist, immediate, and TWO-STAGED
       nirvikalpaka (indeterminate) ──► savikalpaka (determinate)
       "something is present"            "this is a white moving cow"
       (non-verbal, pre-classificatory)  (qualified by class, quality, action, name)
       ⇒ the second stage INTERPRETS what was implicitly available; it does not FALSIFY it
       ⇒ this is the anti-Buddhist move: the given is not a bare unique particular
       ⚠ SUB-SCHOOL SPLIT INSIDE PERCEPTION: is the cognition itself revealed? see Session 12

  2. INFERENCE (anumāna) ─ accepted in fully standard sign-based form
       smoke seen + smoke–fire concomitance known ──► fire inferred
       ⇒ Mīmāṃsā has NO general hostility to inference
       ⇒ it opposes only REDUCTIONISM ABOUT INFERENCE (i.e. "all mediate knowing is inferential")

  3. COMPARISON (upamāna) ─ the most misreported instrument
       MĪMĀṂSĀ: saw a cow earlier ► now perceives a wild ox (gavaya) ► NEW cognition:
                "the previously seen cow is SIMILAR to this gavaya"
       not perception (the cow is absent) · not memory (the similarity was not known before)
       · not inference (no universal concomitance is used)
       NYĀYA:   prior testimony "a gavaya is like a cow" ► later perception ► learns the
                WORD–OBJECT relation
       ❗ DO NOT write the Nyāya mechanism as the Mīmāṃsā definition.
       ❓ Śabara is sometimes read as allowing a wider analogical form (e.g. reasoning from
          one's own experienced self to other selves); the similarity-cognition account is the
          safe exam default.

  4. TESTIMONY (śabda) ─ split by SOURCE and by FUNCTION
       BY SOURCE:   personal (pauruṣeya) ─ depends on a competent speaker; inherits speaker-defect
                    impersonal (apauruṣeya) ─ Vedic; intrinsically valid in the domain of duty
       BY FUNCTION: fact-stating (siddhārtha-vākya)  ·  action-enjoining (vidhāyaka-vākya)
       Kumārila recognises the fact/injunction distinction while making injunction central;
       Prabhākara more radically reads cognition and language through action-guidance.
       ⇒ Testimony here is NOT a fallback after perception fails: it is the ONLY route to duty.
```

*What the diagram shows: what is shared and what is modified, instrument by instrument, with the two
highest-frequency error sites flagged — the Nyāya comparison mechanism and the treatment of
testimony as a second-best source.*

#### CORE UPSC — THE ARGUMENT IN FULL

1. **Perception is shared, realist and immediate.** Both sub-schools accept it as the most basic
   disclosure of sensible objects. What they dispute is not the instrument but its *reflexive
   structure* — whether the cognition reveals itself along with the object (Session 12).

2. **The two stages of perception, and why they matter beyond perception.** ✅ Indeterminate
   perception is the first non-verbal awareness that an object is present, prior to explicit
   classification and predication. ✅ Determinate perception grasps the object as qualified by class,
   quality, action and name. ⚠️ Mīmāṃsā treats the determinate stage as *interpretation of what was
   implicitly available*, not as the invention of an unreal predicate — and that reading is the
   school's defence of realism against the Buddhist claim that perception delivers only a
   non-conceptual unique particular, and against any view on which classification necessarily
   falsifies the given. This anticipates Session 15's defence of real universals.

3. **Inference: accepted, and protected from its own imperialism.** ✅ Where smoke is seen and the
   smoke–fire concomitance is known, fire is inferred. The school has no general hostility to
   inference. ⚠️ Its distinctive move is only to deny that *all* mediate cognition is inferential —
   which is why an answer must never say "Mīmāṃsā opposes anumāna." It opposes reductionism about
   anumāna, and Sessions 9 and 10 are the two places where that opposition does real work.

4. **Comparison, stated correctly.** ✅ Having previously seen a cow, one later perceives a wild ox
   (**gavaya**) in the forest as similar to it; from the perceived similarity of the present wild ox
   to the remembered cow, one gains the new cognition *"the previously seen cow is similar to this
   gavaya."* ✅ It is not perception, because the cow is not presently sensed; not memory, because
   its similarity to this new animal was not previously known; not inference, because no universal
   concomitance is used. ⚠️ The Nyāya contrast — learning the word–object relation from prior
   testimony — must not be written as the Mīmāṃsā definition. ❓ A wider analogical reading of Śabara
   exists; report it as a reading, and default to the similarity account.

5. **Testimony, and the reason it is dignified here.** ✅ In ordinary matters testimony depends on a
   competent source; in matters of duty the decisive verbal source is the Veda. ⚠️ Mīmāṃsā therefore
   gives testimony a philosophical dignity it rarely receives elsewhere: **it is not a fallback after
   perception fails; it is the only route to supersensible duty.**

6. **The two classifications of testimony.** ✅ By source: personal testimony (**pauruṣeya-śabda**)
   depends on a competent human speaker and remains vulnerable to speaker-defect, whereas impersonal
   testimony (**apauruṣeya-śabda**) is Vedic and intrinsically valid in the supersensible domain of
   duty. ✅ By function: testimony may state an existent fact (**siddhārtha-vākya**) or enjoin action
   (**vidhāyaka-vākya**). ✅ Kumārila recognises the distinction while making injunction central to
   Vedic purpose; Prabhākara interprets cognition and language through action-guidance more
   radically still.

7. **Where this session hands off.** The personal/impersonal split is the entry point to Session 16
   (authorlessness); the fact/injunction split is the entry point to Session 17 (injunction theory);
