   the two-stage perception is the entry point to Session 15 (universals).

#### NAMED EVIDENCE YOU CAN CITE

- ✅ The indeterminate/determinate pair, with **ālocana-jñāna** as the alternative name for the
  first stage and *"this is a white moving cow"* as the standard illustration of the second.
- ✅ The wild-ox (**gavaya**) case, stated in the Mīmāṃsā similarity form and expressly distinguished
  from the Nyāya word-reference form.
- ✅ The fourfold testimony grid: personal/impersonal by source, fact-stating/action-enjoining by
  function, with Kumārila and Prabhākara positioned on it.
- ❓ The wider analogical reading of Śabara on comparison is reported as a possible reading, not as
  the school's settled definition.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Mīmāṃsā keeps every instrument its rivals keep, and quietly re-tunes each one: perception is
> allowed two stages so that classification does not become falsification, inference is honoured but
> forbidden to annex its neighbours, comparison is redefined around similarity rather than
> vocabulary, and testimony is promoted from a convenience to the sole competent witness of duty.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

| Keyword | Why the examiner wants it |
|---|---|
| **indeterminate (nirvikalpaka) / determinate (savikalpaka)** | The two-stage realism |
| **wild ox (gavaya) similarity-cognition** | The correct comparison mechanism |
| **personal (pauruṣeya) / impersonal (apauruṣeya) testimony** | The split that grounds Vedic authority |
| **fact-stating (siddhārtha) / action-enjoining (vidhāyaka)** | The functional split |
| **anti-reductionism about inference** | Prevents the "Mīmāṃsā rejects inference" error |
| **concomitance (vyāpti)** | Needed to state what inference requires and postulation lacks |

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — CLAIM -> NAMED EVIDENCE -> ANALYSIS -> QUALIFICATION

> **Claim.** The four instruments Mīmāṃsā shares with its rivals are each modified in ways that
> serve the school's realism and its defence of Vedic authority.
>
> **Named evidence.** Perception is analysed into an indeterminate stage (**nirvikalpaka**) and a
> determinate stage (**savikalpaka**) in which the object appears as qualified by class, quality,
> action and name; comparison is defined by the new cognition that a remembered cow is similar to a
> perceived wild ox (**gavaya**) rather than by learning a word's reference as in Nyāya; and
> testimony is divided into personal (**pauruṣeya**) and impersonal (**apauruṣeya**) sources.
>
> **Analysis.** Each modification is doing work elsewhere in the system: the two-stage account of
> perception blocks the inference from "classification is conceptual" to "classification is false";
> the similarity account of comparison keeps a genuinely non-inferential form of knowing on the
> list; and the personal/impersonal split isolates exactly the defect-locus that authorlessness will
> later be used to eliminate.
>
> **Qualification.** ⚠️ The modifications are not free: the realist reading of determinate
> perception is contested by Buddhist epistemologists who deny real universals, and ❓ the wider
> analogical reading of Śabara's comparison shows that even the school's own textual base is not
> uniform on the point.

#### EXAM APPLICATION — WHERE THIS HAS BEEN ASKED

- **2023, Q8(c), 15 marks** — universals or particulars; the two-stage perception is the
  epistemological support for Session 15's semantic answer.
- **2019, Q5(e), 10 marks** — Vedic authority; the personal/impersonal split is the first move.
- Comparison and inference appear as supporting content in any question that asks why postulation or
  non-cognition cannot be reduced.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Writing the **Nyāya** comparison mechanism as Mīmāṃsā's. The single highest-yield trap in this
   session.
2. ⚠️ Saying Mīmāṃsā rejects or distrusts inference.
3. ⚠️ Treating determinate perception as a falsifying overlay on a pure given.
4. ⚠️ Treating testimony as an inferior, last-resort instrument.
5. ⚠️ Collapsing the source-split and the function-split into one classification; they are two
   independent cuts across testimony.

#### RAPID REVISION

- Perception: two stages; determinate interprets, does not invent.
- Inference: fully accepted; only reductionism about it is refused.
- Comparison: remembered cow ↔ perceived wild ox; **similarity** cognition, not word-learning.
- Testimony: personal vs impersonal (source); fact-stating vs action-enjoining (function).
- Testimony is the *only* competent instrument for duty, not a fallback.

---

### SESSION 8 — WHY MEMORY IS NOT A MEANS OF VALID KNOWLEDGE

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition.** Remembering is not knowing-afresh. When you recall yesterday's temple
visit, no new object is being disclosed to you; an old disclosure is being replayed. Mīmāṃsā, and
Nyāya with it, therefore refuse memory a place among the instruments of valid knowledge — not
because memory is unreliable, but because it is not *productive*.

**Technical definition.** **Memory (smṛti)** depends on a prior cognition and its latent impression
(**saṃskāra**) and reproduces an already-cognised content. It therefore fails the **novelty
condition (anadhigatatva)** that a means of valid knowledge must satisfy: a **pramāṇa** must generate
a *fresh* true cognition of an object not already known through that route.

#### SIMPLE START — THE GATEWAY IDEA

Two people bring you information about a room down the corridor. The first has just walked in,
looked around, and come back. The second has never been today, but remembers what the room looked
like last week and repeats it.

Both may be telling you the truth. Both may be useful. But only the first has *found something out*.
The second has retrieved a finding. Mīmāṃsā's rule is that the title "means of valid knowledge"
belongs to finding-out, not to retrieval — and it applies that rule even when the retrieval is
perfectly accurate and even when it successfully guides action.

⚠️ Notice the shape of the argument. It is not "memory often errs." That would make memory a
*defective* instrument. The claim is stronger and cleaner: memory is not an instrument of that kind
at all, because it does not do the job by which the kind is defined.

#### VISUAL — THE NOVELTY CONDITION AND WHO AGREES ABOUT IT

```text
   WHAT A MEANS OF VALID KNOWLEDGE MUST DO
   ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ (a) operate as the source of a cognition                                       │
   │ (b) disclose an OBJECT                                                         │
   │ (c) do so FRESHLY — novelty (anadhigatatva)                                    │
   └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                        │
                        MEMORY IS TESTED AGAINST (a) (b) (c)
                                        │
     ┌──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┐
     │                                  │                                  │
   (a) ✔ it does operate         (b) ✔ it does have an object      (c) ✘ IT FAILS HERE
                                                                    it depends on an earlier
                                                                    cognition + impression
                                                                    (saṃskāra) and re-presents
                                                                    already-known content
                                        │
                                        ▼
                       ⇒ MEMORY IS NOT A PRAMĀṆA (both sub-schools; Nyāya agrees)

   PRĀBHĀKARA'S FIVE STEPS                       NYĀYA'S CONCURRENCE
   1. a pramāṇa must generate NEW cognition      • memory presents what was already known
   2. memory depends on an earlier cognition     • it is re-presentation, not fresh disclosure
      and a latent impression                    • hence not a pramāṇa
   3. it reproduces old content                  ⇒ THIS IS WHY THE 2024 PAPER PAIRS
   4. it does not reveal a new object as            "the Prābhākaras AND the Naiyāyikas"
      presently known                               IN ONE QUESTION
   5. ∴ it is not a pramāṇa

   ⚠ WHAT THE EXCLUSION DOES NOT SAY
     memory may be TRUE · memory may be USEFUL · memory may GUIDE ACTION successfully
     — but usefulness is not enough for the title; NOVEL VALID DISCLOSURE is required.

   ⚠ AND WHERE MEMORY STILL DOES REAL WORK IN THE SYSTEM
     comparison (upamāna) uses a remembered cow · non-discrimination error (akhyāti) uses a
     remembered silver · sentence-comprehension uses memory of the ordered series of sounds
     ⇒ memory is a working COMPONENT of cognitive life; it is simply not an INSTRUMENT.
```

*What the diagram shows: the exclusion is a precise verdict on one of three conditions, not a
character assassination — and the bottom panel prevents the common over-correction in which a
candidate, having learned that memory is not a pramāṇa, then writes memory out of the system
altogether.*

#### CORE UPSC — THE ARGUMENT IN FULL

1. **The thesis.** ✅ Memory is not a means of valid knowledge because it does not produce fresh
   object-knowledge.

2. **The Prābhākara argument, in the owner's five steps.**
   (i) A means of valid knowledge must generate a new cognition of an object.
   (ii) Memory depends on an earlier cognition and a latent impression.
   (iii) It reproduces old content.
   (iv) It does not reveal a new object as presently known.
   (v) Therefore it is not a means of valid knowledge.

3. **Nyāya agrees, and this agreement is examinable.** ✅ Memory presents what was already known; it
   is re-presentation rather than fresh disclosure. ⚠️ This convergence is why the 2024 paper could
   legitimately pair "the Prābhākaras and the Naiyāyikas" in a single question: on *this* issue the
   two rivals stand together, even though they are opposed on intrinsic validity, on error and on
   how cognition is known.

4. **The distinction that must be stated explicitly.** ✅ Memory may be true. ✅ It may be useful. ✅
   It may guide action. ⚠️ But usefulness is not enough for the title of instrument; **novel valid
   disclosure is required.**

5. **The worked example.** ✅ Seeing a temple now is perception. ✅ Recalling yesterday's visit is
   memory. ✅ The latter is not new knowledge *of the temple*.

6. **Standing objection and reply.**
   ❌ *Objection:* memory can be the basis of successful conduct; why deny it the title?
   ✅ *Reply:* because an instrument is defined by the production of fresh knowledge, not by
   practical utility.

7. **The technical name for the condition.** ✅ The requirement is **novelty (anadhigatatva)** —
   literally, not-already-ascertained. Writing this term converts a good answer into a precise one.

8. **Where memory nevertheless remains indispensable.** ⚠️ Comparison requires a *remembered* absent
   cow; the Prābhākara analysis of error requires a *remembered* silver; sentence-comprehension on
   the Bhāṭṭa account requires memory of the ordered series of phonemes. The exclusion is a verdict
   about instrument-status, not about cognitive irrelevance — and an answer that shows this
   awareness reads as controlled rather than mechanical.

9. **A qualification worth one sentence in a critical answer.** ⚠️ Because the exclusion is
   definitional, it can be accused of settling by stipulation a question that other traditions treat
   substantively; the Mīmāṃsā reply is that the definition is not arbitrary but tracks a real
   difference between disclosing and retrieving.

#### NAMED EVIDENCE YOU CAN CITE

- ✅ Prabhākara Miśra's five-step argument, as it is standardly presented.
- ✅ The Nyāya concurrence on memory as re-presentation, which the 2024 paper presupposes.
- ✅ The novelty condition, **anadhigatatva**, as the technical name of the failed requirement.
- ✅ The temple example, and the general rule that utility does not confer instrument-status.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Memory is excluded from the instruments of knowledge not because it is unreliable but because it
> is unoriginal: it can be perfectly true and still fail the one condition that makes a cognition an
> instrument's product rather than its echo.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

| Keyword | Why the examiner wants it |
|---|---|
| **memory (smṛti)** | The subject of the verdict |
| **latent impression (saṃskāra)** | What memory depends on |
| **novelty (anadhigatatva)** | The failed condition, named |
| **re-presentation, not fresh disclosure** | The precise English formula |
| **Prābhākara–Nyāya convergence** | What the 2024 question is built on |
| **utility ≠ instrument-status** | The distinction most answers omit |

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — CLAIM -> NAMED EVIDENCE -> ANALYSIS -> QUALIFICATION

> **Claim.** Mīmāṃsā denies memory the status of a means of valid knowledge on structural grounds:
> memory reproduces a content already cognised and therefore fails the novelty condition.
>
> **Named evidence.** Prabhākara argues that an instrument must generate a new cognition of an
> object, that memory depends on an earlier cognition together with its latent impression
> (**saṃskāra**), and that it therefore only re-presents; Nyāya reaches the same verdict by the same
> route, which is why the 2024 paper could ask about the Prābhākaras and the Naiyāyikas together.
>
> **Analysis.** The verdict is definitional rather than evaluative, and this is its strength: it
> allows Mīmāṃsā to concede everything a defender of memory might want to say — that memory is
> often true, frequently useful and regularly action-guiding — while still denying it the title,
> because the title tracks the production of fresh disclosure and nothing else.
>
> **Qualification.** ⚠️ The move is not costless: it makes instrument-status turn on a definition
> that a rival could contest, and it sits somewhat awkwardly with the fact that memory remains an
> indispensable *component* of comparison, of the Prābhākara analysis of error, and of ordinary
> sentence-comprehension.

#### EXAM APPLICATION — WHERE THIS HAS BEEN ASKED

- **2020, Q5(c), 10 marks** — "Why is memory not a valid knowledge according to Mīmāṃsā?" This
  session is the whole answer.
- **2024, Q8(c), 15 marks** — "On what grounds do the Prābhākaras and the Naiyāyikas reject memory
  as a source of knowledge?" The convergence paragraph is the question's actual demand.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Answering that memory is excluded because it is *unreliable*. That is the wrong reason and
   loses the point of the argument.
2. ⚠️ Omitting **anadhigatatva**, the term the question is quietly testing.
3. ⚠️ Failing to state the Nyāya agreement in the 2024 question, which explicitly names both schools.
4. ⚠️ Over-correcting by claiming memory plays no role in Mīmāṃsā cognition.
5. ⚠️ Confusing the exclusion of memory with the exclusion of *testimony about the past*; testimony
   remains an instrument.

#### RAPID REVISION

- Rule: instrument = fresh disclosure. Memory = retrieval. ⇒ excluded.
- Term: **anadhigatatva** (novelty / not-already-ascertained).
- Five Prābhākara steps; Nyāya concurs ⇒ 2024 pairing.
- True + useful + action-guiding ≠ instrument.
- Memory still works inside comparison, error and sentence-comprehension.

---
### SESSION 9 — POSTULATION: EXPLANATORY NECESSITY AS A SOURCE OF KNOWLEDGE

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition.** Two things you already know refuse to sit together. Something must be
added to make them consistent, and the addition is not a guess — it is forced. That forced addition
is knowledge, and Mīmāṃsā gives it its own name.

**Technical definition.** **Postulation (arthāpatti)** is the necessary postulation of an
unperceived fact in order to remove an incompatibility (**anupapatti**) between facts already known.
It is compelled explanatory supplementation: neither arbitrary conjecture nor standard inference. It
comes in two forms — **postulation from perceived facts (dṛṣṭārthāpatti)** and **postulation from
heard words (śrutārthāpatti)**.

#### SIMPLE START — THE GATEWAY IDEA

A friend is visibly putting on weight. You also know, from repeated observation, that he never eats
during the day. Now hold both facts in your mind at once. They will not settle. Bodies do not
thicken on nothing.

Notice what happens next in your own mind: you do not *deduce* that he eats at night from some rule
you already possessed. You had no rule "all stout day-fasters eat at night." What actually happens is
that the two facts press against each other, and the pressure itself produces the third fact. The
knowledge arrives as the *only way to let the first two stand*.

⚠️ That is the exact phenomenon Mīmāṃsā is naming. It is not "the best available guess." It is the
supplement without which what you already accept becomes untenable.

#### VISUAL — THE STRUCTURE, THE CASE, AND THE THREE ARGUMENTS AGAINST REDUCTION

```text
   THE BARE STRUCTURE                            THE CANONICAL CASE (Devadatta)
   ────────────────────────────                  ─────────────────────────────────────────
   Fact A is known                               1. Devadatta is stout        (perception)
   Fact B is known                               2. He does not eat by day    (testimony /
   A and B CANNOT stand together                    repeated observation)
        unless C is admitted                     3. stoutness ⟂ total absence of nourishment
   ∴ C is postulated                             4. ⇒ explanatory TENSION
   necessity = explanatory reconciliation        5. resolve it: he eats at ANOTHER time
                                                 6. minimal sufficient postulate: AT NIGHT
                                                 7. night-eating is NOT perceived here
                                                 8. NOT supplied by memory either
                                                 9. ∴ POSTULATED — otherwise the data are
                                                    inexplicable

   TWO FORMS
     dṛṣṭārthāpatti  (from perceived facts)  ─ the Devadatta case above
     śrutārthāpatti  (from heard words)      ─ supplies what an utterance needs to be coherent:
                                               ellipsis, contextual completion
     ⇒ both are EXPLANATORY NECESSITY; the difference is only where the tension starts.

   ══════════ WHY IT IS NOT INFERENCE (anumāna) — three independent arguments ══════════
   (1) NO INDEPENDENTLY ESTABLISHED CONCOMITANCE
       inference needs vyāpti known BEFOREHAND. We possess no prior universal rule
       "all stout non-day-eaters eat at night." The engine is not vyāpti.
   (2) THE DIRECTION OF REASONING DIFFERS
       inference:  observed SIGN ──► unobserved SIGNIFIED   ("this sign indicates that fact")
       postulation: explanatory TENSION ──► reconciling POSTULATE
                    ("without that postulate, THESE facts are inexplicable")
   (3) NO SUBJECT / REASON / PROBANDUM STRUCTURE
       inference parses into pakṣa · hetu · sādhya. Postulation has no hetu sitting in a pakṣa;
       it has a CLASH of accepted facts and a demanded supplementation.

   ══════════ THE NYĀYA REDUCTION AND THE CIRCULARITY REPLY ══════════
   NYĀYA:  hetu = fatness, sādhya = eating; vyāpti = "whoever is fat eats"
   MĪMĀṂSĀ: that vyāpti is not INDEPENDENTLY known. If you say it is known, it was learned
            from lived cases by exactly the tension-driven move under dispute.
            ⇒ THE REDUCTION USES AS ITS PREMISE WHAT POSTULATION ALONE SUPPLIES ⇒ CIRCULAR.
```

*What the diagram shows: the whole of a 15-mark answer in one frame — structure, canonical case, two
forms, three arguments and the circularity reply. Reproduce the frame in the exam and the marks are
already allocated.*

#### CORE UPSC — THE ARGUMENT IN FULL

1. **The core structure, stated abstractly.** ✅ Fact A is known. Fact B is known. A and B cannot
   stand together unless C is admitted. Therefore C is postulated. **The necessity lies in
   explanatory reconciliation.**

2. **The canonical case worked in full.** ✅ I know Devadatta is stout — possibly through perception.
   ✅ I know he does not eat during the day — possibly through testimony or repeated observation. ✅
   Stoutness is incompatible with the complete absence of nourishment. ✅ The two known facts
   therefore produce explanatory tension. ✅ To resolve it I must posit that he eats at another
   time; the natural and sufficient postulate is that he eats at night. ✅ That night-eating is not
   directly perceived in this case, and is not supplied by memory; it is postulated because
   otherwise the known data remain inexplicable.

3. **The presuppositions.** ✅ Bodily conditions have intelligible causes. ✅ Stoutness normally
   requires nourishment. ✅ Rational cognition seeks coherence among accepted facts.

4. **The distinction inside the case.** ⚠️ The reasoning does not begin from a sign like smoke; it
   begins from *tension between facts*. And the output is not merely "he eats," but the **minimally
   reconciling postulate**. That word "minimally" is doing real philosophical work: it is what stops
   postulation from licensing extravagant explanations.

5. **The two forms.** ✅ **From perceived facts (dṛṣṭārthāpatti):** the Devadatta case. ✅ **From
   heard words (śrutārthāpatti):** what is required to make an utterance meaningful or coherent,
   including ellipsis and contextual completion. ⚠️ Both are forms of explanatory necessity, not
   tentative hypothesis; the distinction concerns only whether the initiating tension arises from
   perceived facts or from verbal material.

6. **The three arguments against reduction to inference.**
   ✅ *First — no independently established concomitance.* In inference the relation between reason
   (**hetu**) and probandum (**sādhya**) must be known beforehand. In postulation no independently
   established universal concomitance drives the transition; we do not already possess the rule "all
   stout non-day-eaters eat at night." The cognition arises because the facts would otherwise
   conflict. Its engine is not **vyāpti**.
   ✅ *Second — the direction of reasoning differs.* Inference moves from observed sign to unobserved
   signified. Postulation moves from explanatory tension to reconciling postulate. The logic is not
   "this sign indicates that fact" but "without that postulate, these facts are inexplicable."
   ✅ *Third — no subject/reason/probandum structure.* Standard inference parses into **pakṣa**,
   **hetu** and **sādhya**. Postulation lacks that form: there is no identified reason present in a
   subject from which a probandum is derived; there is a clash of accepted facts and a demanded
   supplementation.

7. **The Nyāya reduction and the circularity reply.** ❌ Nyāya says fatness is the reason and eating
   the probandum. ✅ Mīmāṃsā replies that the proposed concomitance "whoever is fat eats" is not
   independently known; if one claims it *is* known, one is already relying on postulation-like
   reasoning from lived cases. ✅ **The reduction therefore uses as a premise exactly what postulation
   alone supplies, and is circular.**

8. **Postulation and modern explanatory reasoning.** ⚠️ Postulation resembles what modern philosophy
   calls inference to the best explanation, but the classical formulation is **stronger**: it is not
   "best among options," it is *the postulate without which the known facts remain untenable*. That
   necessity is why Mīmāṃsā grants it instrument-status — and stating this comparison, with the
   disanalogy attached, is the single best way to show comparative control in a 15-marker.

9. **Distinctions to write out.** ✅ Postulation ≠ mere guess. ✅ ≠ probabilistic hunch. ✅ ≠ standard
   inference. ✅ = explanatory postulation compelled by otherwise unresolved incompatibility.

10. **Two objections and their replies.**
    ❌ *Any explanation could be relabelled postulation.* ✅ No — only postulations *necessary* to
    resolve an established incompatibility qualify.
    ❌ *Why not reduce the whole process to hidden universal laws?* ✅ Because those universals are
    often themselves abstracted from the very explanatory move in question.

11. **The 2024 candidate case, worked in the Bhāṭṭa manner.** ✅ The candidate tops the examination.
    ✅ The candidate is never seen studying by day. ✅ Examination success is incompatible with no
    preparation at all. ✅ Therefore some unobserved preparation must be posited. ✅ The natural
    postulate is study at night or otherwise outside observed daytime. ⚠️ The Nyāya framing — "wherever
    there is examination success there is preparation; this candidate has success; therefore this
    candidate had preparation" — misses the explanatory tension central to the original case and
    slides in a concomitance far too quickly.

12. **Where postulation reappears later in the system.** ⚠️ It is the instrument by which **unseen
    potency (apūrva)** is established (Session 17) and it supplies the *form* of the argument for
    authorlessness (Session 16). Postulation is thus not an epistemological ornament; it is
    load-bearing for the school's two most contested metaphysical posits.

#### NAMED EVIDENCE YOU CAN CITE

- ✅ The stout-Devadatta case in its nine steps — the canonical Indian illustration, to be used in
  almost every answer on this topic.
- ✅ The two forms, **dṛṣṭārthāpatti** and **śrutārthāpatti**.
- ✅ The three arguments against reduction, and the circularity reply to the Nyāya reduction.
- ✅ The 2024 candidate case as an applied Bhāṭṭa analysis, with the Nyāya inferential framing stated
  and criticised.
- ✅ Further example bank: a person never seen leaving home yet present at the office daily must
  leave at some unobserved hour. ⚠️ Use the canonical example first; modern examples only to
  illustrate.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Postulation is knowledge produced by the pressure of coherence rather than by the guidance of a
> sign: nothing points to Devadatta's night-eating, and yet nothing else will let his stoutness and
> his daytime fasting remain true together.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

| Keyword | Why the examiner wants it |
|---|---|
| **postulation (arthāpatti)** | The instrument itself |
| **incompatibility (anupapatti)** | The trigger condition, named |
| **from perceived facts (dṛṣṭārthāpatti) / from heard words (śrutārthāpatti)** | The two forms |
| **concomitance (vyāpti)** | What inference needs and postulation lacks |
| **subject (pakṣa) · reason (hetu) · probandum (sādhya)** | The structure postulation lacks |
| **circular reduction** | The reply that finishes the Nyāya attempt |
| **minimally reconciling postulate** | Prevents postulation from licensing extravagance |

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — CLAIM -> NAMED EVIDENCE -> ANALYSIS -> QUALIFICATION

> **Claim.** Postulation is an independent means of valid knowledge because the cognition it
> produces is driven by explanatory incompatibility rather than by a previously established
> sign-relation.
>
> **Named evidence.** In the canonical case, stout Devadatta's daytime fasting and his stoutness
> cannot both stand unless night-eating is admitted; and Kumārila's three arguments show that no
> prior concomitance such as "all stout non-day-eaters eat at night" is available, that the movement
> runs from tension to reconciliation rather than from sign to signified, and that no subject,
> reason and probandum can be identified in the case at all.
>
> **Analysis.** The Nyāya attempt to recover an inference by treating fatness as the reason
> therefore fails at its own first step, because the concomitance it needs — "whoever is fat eats" —
> is itself learned from lived cases by exactly the tension-driven move under dispute; the reduction
> consumes its own conclusion, and the charge of circularity is not rhetorical but structural.
>
> **Qualification.** ⚠️ Nyāya can still press that the appeal to "explanatory necessity" is doing the
> work of a suppressed general premise, so the debate is best presented as a genuine disagreement
> about whether explanatory compulsion is epistemically basic, with Mīmāṃsā's stronger-than-modern
> formulation — necessity, not best-available — as its most defensible feature.

#### EXAM APPLICATION — WHERE THIS HAS BEEN ASKED

- **2022, Q6(c), 15 marks** — how Mīmāṃsakas refute the Nyāya reduction and establish postulation as
  independent. The three arguments plus the circularity reply *are* the answer.
- **2024, Q8(b), 15 marks** — the candidate who is never seen studying; the question asks for the
  Bhāṭṭa and the Naiyāyika explanations side by side, so both framings must be written.
- Supports **2019, Q5(e)** (the postulation-form of the authorlessness argument) and any question
  touching unseen potency.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Calling postulation "inference to the best explanation" without the disanalogy. The classical
   claim is *necessity*, not comparative merit.
2. ⚠️ Giving only one of the three arguments against reduction. All three are separable and all
   three are marked.
3. ⚠️ Forgetting the circularity reply, which is the move that actually defeats Nyāya.
4. ⚠️ Presenting only the modern candidate example and omitting Devadatta.
5. ⚠️ Confusing the two forms, or treating verbal postulation as a mere sub-case of ellipsis-filling
   with no epistemic weight.
6. ⚠️ Writing that Prābhākara rejects postulation. He accepts it; it is his **fifth** instrument.

#### RAPID REVISION

- Structure: A + B incompatible without C ⇒ C postulated. Necessity = reconciliation.
- Case: stout Devadatta, no day-eating ⇒ night-eating. Minimal postulate only.
- Forms: perceived-fact and heard-word postulation.
- Three anti-reduction arguments: no prior concomitance · reversed direction · no
  subject/reason/probandum.
- Nyāya reduction ⇒ circular, because its concomitance is postulation-derived.
- Both sub-schools accept it; it is Mīmāṃsā's fifth instrument.

---

### SESSION 10 — NON-COGNITION, AND THE REALITY OF ABSENCE

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition.** You look at a well-lit table and know that the book is not there. That
is a definite piece of knowledge, it guides what you do next, and it is not the same as merely
failing to notice something. Kumārila says it needs its own instrument.

**Technical definition.** **Non-cognition (anupalabdhi)** is the independent means of knowing absence
through the non-apprehension of what *would* be apprehended if present. The limiting condition is
**fitness (yogyatā)**, so the instrument is precisely **yogyānupalabdhi** — non-apprehension of the
*perceptible*. Its object, **absence (abhāva)**, is for Bhāṭṭa a real structured content with a
**counterpositive (pratiyogin)**, the thing whose absence it is. ⚠️ The doctrine is characteristic of
**Bhāṭṭa** Mīmāṃsā; **Prābhākara and Nyāya reject it as a separate instrument.**

#### SIMPLE START — THE GATEWAY IDEA

Three situations, and only one of them is knowledge of absence.

*First:* you glance at a cluttered desk in bad light and do not see your keys. You have not learned
that the keys are absent. You have learned nothing.

*Second:* you stand in an empty room and wonder whether there are atoms in front of you. You do not
perceive any. But atoms are not the sort of thing you would perceive even if present, so your
non-perception tells you nothing about them either.

*Third:* you look at a bare, well-lit table from a normal distance with working eyes, and there is no
pot. Here — and only here — your non-apprehension is informative, because *if a pot were there, you
would have seen it.*

⚠️ That conditional is the whole doctrine. **Fitness (yogyatā)** is what converts a non-event into a
disclosure, and it is also what stops the doctrine from licensing every lazy inference from "I didn't
see it" to "it isn't there."

#### VISUAL — THE FITNESS FILTER AND THE THREE-WAY DISPUTE

```text
   ┌──────────── THE FITNESS FILTER (yogyatā) — all four must hold ────────────┐
   │  • the absent object must be the KIND of thing perceivable if present     │
   │  • the LOCUS must be suitable                                             │
   │  • the SENSE ORGAN must be functioning                                    │
   │  • light, distance and attention must be PROPER                           │
   └───────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                   │ passes  ⇒ yogyānupalabdhi ⇒ KNOWLEDGE OF ABSENCE
                                   │ fails   ⇒ mere non-noticing ⇒ NO KNOWLEDGE
        ✔  "no pot on this bare, lit table before me"
        ✘  "there are no atoms here"  (atoms are not perceptible ⇒ filter fails)

   ══════════════ KUMĀRILA'S SEVEN-STEP ARGUMENT ══════════════
   1. we DO know absences: "there is no pot on the table"
   2. this cognition is DEFINITE and ACTION-GUIDING
   3. not ordinary perception — perception gives POSITIVE content
   4. not inference — inference requires a POSITIVE sign
   5. not memory, comparison or testimony in the typical case
   6. ∴ an independent source is needed
   7. that source is NON-COGNITION (anupalabdhi)

   ══════════════ THE THREE-WAY DISPUTE ══════════════
   BHĀṬṬA        absence (abhāva) is REAL, with a counterpositive (pratiyogin);
                 known by an INDEPENDENT instrument, anupalabdhi
   PRĀBHĀKARA    what is known is simply the LOCUS as unqualified by the absent object;
                 the bare-locus cognition already carries the negative differentiation;
                 no separate instrument — an ECONOMY thesis
   NYĀYA         accepts abhāva as a real category BUT says absence is DIRECTLY PERCEIVED:
                 "pot-absence-on-the-floor" is itself a perceptual object
   ⇒ NYĀYA AND PRĀBHĀKARA CONVERGE AGAINST BHĀṬṬA ON INDEPENDENCE — for different reasons.
   ⚠ Do not therefore say "Nyāya denies abhāva." Nyāya denies the separate INSTRUMENT.

   ══════════════ WHY BHĀṬṬA REFUSES BOTH REDUCTIONS ══════════════
   to PERCEPTION: the table, its colour, shape and spread are perceptually given;
                  "absence of pot" is not a positive sensible datum of that kind.
                  Perceiving the empty table is ONE thing; cognising that the pot is
                  absent is ANOTHER.
   to INFERENCE:  inference needs a positive sign and known concomitance. If "not-seeing
                  the pot" is offered as the sign, one must explain why not-seeing has
                  evidentiary force — and that force ALREADY presupposes the relevance of
                  non-apprehension under fit conditions. ⇒ strained or circular.
```

*What the diagram shows: the fitness filter that makes the doctrine respectable, the seven-step
argument that establishes it, the exact three-way alignment of the schools, and the two refusals
that hold the position together — everything a 15-mark answer on absence needs.*

#### CORE UPSC — THE ARGUMENT IN FULL

1. **The thesis.** ✅ Non-cognition is the independent means of knowing absence through
   non-apprehension of what would be apprehended if present.

2. **Kumārila's argument, in the owner's seven steps.**
   (i) We do have knowledge of absence, such as "there is no pot on the table."
   (ii) This cognition is definite and action-guiding.
   (iii) It is not ordinary perception, because perception gives positive content.
   (iv) It is not inference, because inference requires a positive sign.
   (v) It is not memory, comparison or testimony in the typical case.
   (vi) Therefore an independent source is needed.
   (vii) That source is **anupalabdhi**.

3. **The presupposition.** ✅ Absence is a genuine *object* of cognition; negative knowledge is not a
   mere linguistic shadow of positive perception.

4. **The distinction that keeps the doctrine honest.** ⚠️ Non-cognition is **not** simple ignorance
   and **not** "I failed to notice." It is a **determinate cognition that something is absent.**

5. **The fitness condition in full.** ✅ The absent object must be the kind of thing that would be
   perceived if present; the locus must be suitable; the sense organ must be functioning;
   conditions such as light, distance and attention must be proper. ✅ Only then does non-perception
   justify absence-cognition. ✅ Hence "no pot on the table" is knowable when the table is clearly
   before me, but "there are no atoms here" cannot be reached from non-perception, because atoms are
   not perceptible objects. ⚠️ **The doctrine is therefore carefully limited, and the limitation is
   itself the answer to the charge of subjectivism.**

6. **Why Bhāṭṭa denies reduction to perception.** ✅ Perception reveals what is positively present —
   table, colour, shape, spatial spread. ✅ "Absence of pot" is not itself a positive sensible datum
   of that kind. ✅ The perception of the empty table is one thing; the cognition that the pot is
   absent is another.

7. **Why Bhāṭṭa denies reduction to inference.** ✅ Inference requires a positive sign and known
   concomitance. ✅ In absence-cognition no independent positive sign is typically identified. ✅ If
   "not-seeing the pot" is offered as the sign, one must explain why not-seeing has evidentiary
   force — and that force already presupposes the relevance of non-apprehension under fit
   conditions. ✅ The inferential reduction is therefore either strained or circular.

8. **The Prābhākara rejection, stated fairly.** ✅ What we directly know is the locus. ✅ We perceive
   the table as bare, not occupied by the pot. ✅ No special faculty for "what is not there" need be
   posited. ✅ Therefore ordinary perception suffices. ⚠️ This is an **economy thesis**, not a denial
   that we know absences. ❌ *Objection:* perception of a bare locus does not yet explain a
   determinate negative judgement. ✅ *Reply:* Prabhākara holds that the bare-locus cognition already
   carries the negative differentiation needed.

9. **The Nyāya rejection, stated precisely.** ✅ Nyāya also denies an independent non-cognition. ✅ It
   *accepts* absence (**abhāva**) as real but holds that absence is **directly perceived**: looking
   at the floor, I perceive "pot-absence-on-the-floor." ⚠️ So Nyāya and Prābhākara converge against
   Bhāṭṭa on *independence* while differing in their broader theories — and an answer that notes this
   convergence-with-divergence is doing exactly what "explain and examine" asks for.

10. **The ontological status of absence — the 2021 core.** ✅ Bhāṭṭa Mīmāṃsā accepts **abhāva as
    real**. ✅ It is not merely a feature of language. ✅ It has a **counterpositive (pratiyogin)**,
    the entity whose absence is involved. ✅ Thus "absence of pot" is a *structured ontological
    content*, and negative cognition therefore has a real object.

11. **The intra-Mīmāṃsā debate on absence — the 2025 core.** ✅ Bhāṭṭa: absence is real and known by
    an independent instrument. ✅ Prābhākara: what is known is simply the locus as unqualified by the
    absent object; no separate instrument is needed. ⚠️ The 2025 question turns on precisely this
    divergence, and it must be answered as an *internal* Mīmāṃsā dispute, not as Mīmāṃsā versus
    Nyāya.

12. **Objections and replies.**
    ❌ *If absence is real, reality becomes overcrowded with negatives.* ✅ Bhāṭṭa accepts this
    ontological cost to preserve the reality of negative cognition.
    ❌ *Non-perception is too subjective.* ✅ Only **yogyānupalabdhi** under fit conditions counts,
    never careless overlooking.

13. **The ordinary-life vindication.** ✅ Negative cognition is woven into daily life: no chalk on
    the tray, no key in the drawer, no bus at the stop. ⚠️ Philosophy must explain what everyday life
    constantly presupposes — and a school that could not account for these would be refuted by the
    tray, the drawer and the bus stop.

#### NAMED EVIDENCE YOU CAN CITE

- ✅ Kumārila Bhaṭṭa's seven-step argument and his acceptance of real **abhāva** with a
  **pratiyogin**.
- ✅ The fitness condition **yogyānupalabdhi**, with the pot-on-the-table case and the
  atoms counter-case.
- ✅ Prabhākara's bare-locus economy thesis and its reply to the determinacy objection.
- ✅ Nyāya's perceptual account of absence — "pot-absence-on-the-floor" — and the resulting
  convergence with Prābhākara on independence.
- ✅ The decisive formula: **non-perception of what would be perceived if present.**

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Kumārila's claim is not that nothing can be learned from failing to see; it is that something can
> be learned only when failing to see is *guaranteed to be informative* — and the entire doctrine of
> non-cognition is the specification of when that guarantee holds.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

| Keyword | Why the examiner wants it |
|---|---|
| **non-cognition (anupalabdhi)** | The instrument |
| **fitness (yogyatā) / yogyānupalabdhi** | The limiting condition that answers the main objection |
| **absence (abhāva)** | The object, and the 2021 question's subject |
| **counterpositive (pratiyogin)** | Shows absence is structured, not blank |
| **bare locus, unqualified** | Prabhākara's alternative, stated in his own terms |
| **"pot-absence-on-the-floor"** | Nyāya's perceptual formulation |
| **non-perception of the perceptible** | The decisive phrase |

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — CLAIM -> NAMED EVIDENCE -> ANALYSIS -> QUALIFICATION

> **Claim.** Bhāṭṭa Mīmāṃsā treats absence as a real object known by an independent instrument,
> because the determinate cognition of absence can be reduced neither to perception nor to inference
> without distortion or circularity.
>
> **Named evidence.** Kumārila argues that "there is no pot on the table" is definite and
> action-guiding, that perception delivers positive content while inference requires a positive
> sign, and that the resulting cognition therefore needs its own source — with **abhāva** accepted as
> real and structured by its counterpositive (**pratiyogin**), and with the fitness condition
> (**yogyānupalabdhi**) restricting the instrument to objects that would have been perceived if
> present.
>
> **Analysis.** The fitness condition is what converts the doctrine from a licence into a discipline:
> it explains why "no pot on this lit table" is knowledge while "no atoms here" is not, and it
> simultaneously defeats the inferential reduction, since any attempt to treat non-seeing as a sign
> must presuppose exactly the evidentiary force that fitness supplies.
>
> **Qualification.** ⚠️ The position is bought at an ontological price that Prabhākara and Nyāya both
> refuse to pay — the former by perceiving the bare locus, the latter by perceiving absence itself —
> so the honest verdict is that Bhāṭṭa preserves the phenomenology of negative cognition most
> faithfully while carrying the heaviest ontology.

#### EXAM APPLICATION — WHERE THIS HAS BEEN ASKED

- **2021, Q6(c), 15 marks** — the ontological status of absence and how it is known. Both halves are
  here: reality with a counterpositive, and non-cognition as the route.
- **2023, Q6(b), 15 marks** — Bhāṭṭa's view of non-cognition as a valid means of knowledge.
- **2025, Q6(c), 15 marks** — the Bhāṭṭa–Prābhākara debate on the nature of non-existence and its
  knowledge; answer it as an internal dispute.
- Supports **2022, Q6(c)**, since both extra instruments are defended by the same irreducibility
  logic.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Attributing non-cognition to Mīmāṃsā generally. It is **Bhāṭṭa's**; Prābhākara rejects it.
2. ⚠️ Saying Nyāya denies **abhāva**. Nyāya accepts absence and denies only the separate instrument.
3. ⚠️ Omitting the fitness condition, which is where the doctrine's respectability lives.
4. ⚠️ Describing non-cognition as "not noticing." It is a determinate positive verdict of absence.
5. ⚠️ Answering the 2025 question as Mīmāṃsā versus Nyāya rather than Bhāṭṭa versus Prābhākara.
6. ⚠️ Forgetting the **pratiyogin** in a question that asks about *ontological status*.

#### RAPID REVISION

- Formula: non-perception of what would be perceived if present.
- Filter: kind-perceptibility · suitable locus · working organ · proper light, distance, attention.
- Seven steps: we know absences → definite → not perception → not inference → not memory/comparison/
  testimony → independent source needed → non-cognition.
- Bhāṭṭa: real absence + counterpositive + independent instrument.
- Prābhākara: bare locus, economy. Nyāya: absence directly perceived.
- Convergence: Nyāya + Prābhākara against Bhāṭṭa on independence only.

---
### SESSION 11 — THEORIES OF ERROR, STATED PRECISELY

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition.** A piece of shell on the beach flashes in the sun and you reach for it,
saying "silver." Something has gone wrong. But *what* exactly has gone wrong? Indian philosophy's
four main answers to that question are four different theories of error, and Mīmāṃsā supplies two of
them — one from each sub-school.

**Technical definition.** A **theory of error (khyāti-vāda)** explains what an erroneous cognition is
and what its apparent object is. Prābhākara defends **non-apprehension of difference (akhyāti /
vivekākhyāti)**; Bhāṭṭa defends **contrary apprehension (viparīta-khyāti)**; Nyāya defends
**otherwise-apprehension (anyathā-khyāti)**; Advaita defends **indescribable-apprehension
(anirvacanīya-khyāti)**.

#### SIMPLE START — THE GATEWAY IDEA

Ask four witnesses what happened on that beach.

*Prābhākara:* "Nothing false occurred. You truly perceived *this shining thing*, and you truly
remembered *silver*. Your only failure was that you did not notice the two were different. There is
no false cognition here to explain — only a missing discrimination."

*Bhāṭṭa:* "Something false did occur. Bad light and similar lustre made you cognise the shell **as**
silver. That is one cognition, and it is wrong. But it is wrong because of a defect in the
conditions, not because cognition is naturally unreliable."

*Nyāya:* "The silver is real — it exists elsewhere, in a shop or in memory's object — and it has been
presented *here*, where it does not belong. Error is displacement."

*Advaita:* "The silver that appeared was neither real nor unreal; it was an indescribable something
projected and later cancelled."

⚠️ Note carefully: Bhāṭṭa and Nyāya are the pair most often conflated, because both admit a genuinely
false cognition. The difference is what the "silver" is. For Bhāṭṭa it is a *wrong identification* of
the shell; for Nyāya it is a *real object displaced from elsewhere*.

#### VISUAL — THE FOUR THEORIES, AND THE TWO THAT ARE ALWAYS CONFUSED

```text
   THE SHARED DATUM:  a shell (nacre) lies before the subject, who says "this is silver"
                      and reaches for it.

   ┌──────────────────────┬─────────────┬──────────────────────────────┬───────────────────┐
   │ THEORY               │ SCHOOL      │ HOW SHELL-SILVER IS EXPLAINED│ STATUS OF "SILVER"│
   ├──────────────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
   │ akhyāti /            │ PRĀBHĀKARA  │ TWO valid cognitions, not    │ NO false silver   │
   │ vivekākhyāti         │             │ discriminated: perception of │ at all            │
   │ (non-apprehension    │             │ "this shining thing" +       │                   │
   │  of difference)      │             │ memory of silver             │                   │
   ├──────────────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
   │ viparīta-khyāti      │ BHĀṬṬA      │ the shell is POSITIVELY      │ really WRONG      │
   │ (contrary            │             │ miscognised AS silver;       │ identification    │
   │  apprehension)       │             │ ONE erroneous cognition      │                   │
   ├──────────────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
   │ anyathā-khyāti       │ NYĀYA       │ REAL silver known elsewhere  │ real, but         │
   │ (otherwise-          │             │ is presented HERE, wrongly   │ DISPLACED         │
   │  apprehension)       │             │                              │                   │
   ├──────────────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
   │ anirvacanīya-khyāti  │ ADVAITA     │ an indescribable appearance  │ NEITHER real nor  │
   │ (indescribable-      │             │ is projected and sublated    │ unreal            │
   │  apprehension)       │             │                              │                   │
   └──────────────────────┴─────────────┴──────────────────────────────┴───────────────────┘
             ▲                                    ▲
             └── these two both admit a FALSE COGNITION, and are constantly conflated ──┘
                 BHĀṬṬA: the object here is MISIDENTIFIED
                 NYĀYA : the object elsewhere is MISPLACED
                 ⇒ "wrong identification" ≠ "right object, wrong location"

   ══════ HOW EACH MĪMĀṂSĀ THEORY PROTECTS INTRINSIC VALIDITY (the 2022 puzzle) ══════
      PRĀBHĀKARA: protects EVERY CONSTITUENT COGNITION — perception valid, memory valid,
                  error located only in their conflation ⇒ no cognition is ever false
      BHĀṬṬA    : protects THE PRINCIPLE GOVERNING NORMAL COGNITION — falsity is admitted
                  but quarantined in DEFECT-RIDDEN conditions and known only extrinsically
      ⇒ SAME THESIS, TWO DEFENCE STRATEGIES. That is the whole answer to "in spite of
        accepting the intrinsic validity of knowledge, why do they differ?"
```

*What the diagram shows: the four-column comparison every answer needs, the precise Bhāṭṭa–Nyāya
distinction that most answers get wrong, and the reconciliation panel that answers the 2022
twenty-marker directly.*

#### CORE UPSC — THE ARGUMENT IN FULL

1. **Why error is central rather than peripheral.** ✅ Any theory of intrinsic validity must explain
   illusion. If cognition is *prima facie* valid, what exactly goes wrong in false experience? ⚠️ The
   Mīmāṃsā theories of error are therefore not an appendix to the epistemology; they are its
   stress-test.

2. **Prābhākara's non-apprehension of difference, in five steps.**
   (i) In the shell-silver illusion the subject validly perceives *this shining object here* — a
   perception of the shell's present appearance.
   (ii) The subject also recalls previously experienced silver, and that recollection is valid as
   memory.
   (iii) The mistake lies in the failure to distinguish these two cognitions.
   (iv) Because of this non-discrimination, one behaves as if the present object were silver.
   (v) There is no need to posit a separate false cognition over and above these two.

3. **The presupposition and the naming point.** ✅ Perception and memory can operate together in
   confused synthesis, and practical action can arise from such non-discrimination. ⚠️ **Akhyāti does
   not mean total non-apprehension.** It means non-apprehension of the *difference* between what is
   perceived and what is remembered — which is why the label **vivekākhyāti** ("non-apprehension of
   discrimination") is often more precise and is worth writing.

4. **The standing objection to Prābhākara and his reply.**
   ❌ *But the person says "this is silver," which sounds like one positive false judgement.*
   ✅ *Reply:* the utterance and the action can be explained by the fusion of two valid cognitions; a
   further false cognition is unnecessary.
   ❌ *A second objection:* the theory underestimates the assertive force of illusion.
   ✅ *Reply:* non-discrimination can explain assertive behaviour without adding a false cognition.

5. **How this preserves intrinsic validity.** ✅ Every constituent cognition remains valid — the
   presentational cognition of "this" is valid, the memory of silver is valid, and error lies only in
   their conflation. ✅ Therefore no individual cognition needs to be labelled false. ⚠️ This is
   Prābhākara's distinctive way of protecting the thesis: **he never has to admit a false cognition
   at all.**

6. **Bhāṭṭa's contrary apprehension, in seven steps.**
   (i) In the shell-silver illusion the shell is before the subject.
   (ii) Due to bad light, distance, similarity of shine or sensory defect, the shell is miscognised.
   (iii) A single erroneous cognition arises: "this is silver."
   (iv) The cognition is false.
   (v) Its falsity, however, is due to *extrinsic defect*, not to cognition's intrinsic nature.
   (vi) Later correction occurs when the subject approaches and sees the shell clearly.
   (vii) Invalidity is then known extrinsically.

7. **The presupposition and the distinction.** ✅ False cognition is possible, but its cause lies in
   defect, not in the normal operation of cognition. ⚠️ Bhāṭṭa permits false cognition and does *not*
   thereby abandon intrinsic validity: validity belongs to cognition under normal conditions, whereas
   falsity belongs to defect-ridden conditions.

8. **The standing objection to Bhāṭṭa and his reply.**
   ❌ *If false cognition exists, why call validity intrinsic at all?*
   ✅ *Reply:* because falsity is not basic — it arises through abnormal interference and is
   recognised through later defeat.
   ❌ *A second objection:* the theory grants too much to falsity.
   ✅ *Reply:* it grants falsehood only under abnormal conditions, thereby preserving the default
   truth of cognition.

9. **The 2022 puzzle, resolved in four lines.** ✅ Both accept intrinsic validity. ✅ They differ
   because they *locate error differently*. ✅ Prābhākara: no false cognition, only failure of
   discrimination. ✅ Bhāṭṭa: yes, a false cognition can occur, but only through defect and only known
   false extrinsically. ⚠️ **Hence one protects all constituent cognitions; the other protects the
   principle governing normal cognition.**

10. **The rival theories, stated exactly.** ✅ **Nyāya (anyathā-khyāti):** error involves the
    misplacement of a real object known elsewhere — the silver is real but displaced. ✅ **Advaita
    (anirvacanīya-khyāti):** the illusory object is neither fully real nor unreal, and is therefore
    indescribable. ⚠️ Mīmāṃsā remains more epistemologically anchored and realist than either: it
    neither disperses the object elsewhere like Nyāya nor renders it metaphysically indeterminate
    like Advaita.

11. **Why the behavioural fact matters.** ✅ Reaching for the silver is the datum that both Mīmāṃsā
    theories must explain. ✅ Bhāṭṭa thinks a positive false judgement explains it best; Prābhākara
    thinks non-discrimination suffices. ⚠️ The debate therefore concerns philosophical psychology as
    much as epistemology, and saying so converts a descriptive answer into an analytical one.

12. **Presuppositions common to both.** ✅ Error must be explainable without collapsing trust in
    cognition. ✅ Memory and perception can interact. ✅ Defect and correction are intelligible
    features of cognitive life.

13. **The critical verdict.** ❌ *Criticism:* both theories seem strained compared with common sense.
    ✅ *Reply:* they are sophisticated attempts to save default trust in cognition while accounting
    for illusion — Prābhākara by minimising false cognition, Bhāṭṭa by localising it in extrinsic
    defect. ⚠️ Report this as a graded verdict rather than as a knockout for either side.

#### NAMED EVIDENCE YOU CAN CITE

- ✅ The shell-silver (nacre-silver) case as the shared datum of all four theories.
- ✅ Prabhākara's five-step non-discrimination analysis, with **vivekākhyāti** as the more precise
  label.
- ✅ Kumārila's seven-step contrary-apprehension analysis, with defect and later correction.
- ✅ The four-theory contrast table: akhyāti (Prābhākara), viparīta-khyāti (Bhāṭṭa), anyathā-khyāti
  (Nyāya), anirvacanīya-khyāti (Advaita).
- ✅ The 2022 reconciliation: same thesis, two defence strategies.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Prabhākara saves intrinsic validity by refusing to admit a false cognition anywhere, and Kumārila
> saves it by admitting one and then quarantining it in abnormal conditions; the disagreement is not
> about whether cognition may be trusted but about what price is worth paying to keep that trust
> intact.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

| Keyword | Why the examiner wants it |
|---|---|
| **non-apprehension of difference (akhyāti / vivekākhyāti)** | Prābhākara's theory, precisely named |
| **contrary apprehension (viparīta-khyāti)** | Bhāṭṭa's theory |
| **otherwise-apprehension (anyathā-khyāti)** | Nyāya's theory — must not be merged with Bhāṭṭa's |
| **indescribable-apprehension (anirvacanīya-khyāti)** | Advaita's theory |
| **shell-silver (nacre-silver)** | The shared datum |
| **defect (doṣa) and later correction** | How Bhāṭṭa keeps falsity extrinsic |
| **conflation of two valid cognitions** | The exact Prābhākara formula |

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — CLAIM -> NAMED EVIDENCE -> ANALYSIS -> QUALIFICATION

> **Claim.** Prabhākara and Kumārila differ over erroneous cognition not despite their shared thesis
> of intrinsic validity but *because* of it: each is protecting that thesis, and each has chosen a
> different thing to protect.
>
> **Named evidence.** In the shell-silver case Prabhākara analyses the episode into a valid
> perception of "this shining thing" and a valid memory of silver whose difference is not
> apprehended, so that no false cognition is admitted at all; Kumārila admits a single erroneous
> cognition, "this is silver," and attributes it to defect — bad light, distance, similarity of
> lustre — with invalidity later disclosed by approaching and seeing the shell clearly.
>
> **Analysis.** The two strategies trade against each other precisely: Prabhākara keeps every
> cognition valid at the cost of explaining assertive illusion by a mere failure of discrimination,
> while Kumārila explains that assertive force straightforwardly at the cost of admitting a false
> cognition into a system built on default truth — and each cost is exactly what the other refuses to
> pay.
>
> **Qualification.** ⚠️ Neither analysis is uncontested: the non-discrimination account is charged
> with underrating the positive force of "this is silver," and the contrary-apprehension account with
> granting too much to falsity; and both must be kept distinct from Nyāya's displacement theory,
> which admits a false cognition but locates its object elsewhere rather than misidentifying the one
> in front of the subject.

#### EXAM APPLICATION — WHERE THIS HAS BEEN ASKED

- **2022, Q7(a), 20 marks** — "Inspite of accepting the intrinsic validity of knowledge, why and how
  Prabhākara and Kumārila differ in their interpretation of erroneous cognition?" This session is the
  entire answer, and the reconciliation panel is its conclusion.
- Supports **2018, Q8(a)** and **2024, Q8(a)**, since both require a controlled Nyāya–Mīmāṃsā–Advaita
  comparison in which the error-theories often supply the sharpest contrast.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ **Equating Bhāṭṭa's viparīta-khyāti with Nyāya's anyathā-khyāti.** Both admit a false
   cognition, but Bhāṭṭa misidentifies *this* object while Nyāya displaces a *real object from
   elsewhere*. This is the single most valuable distinction in the session.
2. ⚠️ Translating **akhyāti** as "total non-apprehension." It is non-apprehension **of difference**.
3. ⚠️ Reversing the attributions — putting contrary apprehension on Prabhākara.
4. ⚠️ Writing only the two Mīmāṃsā theories when the question invites a four-column comparison.
5. ⚠️ Concluding that Bhāṭṭa abandons intrinsic validity because he admits false cognition.
6. ⚠️ Forgetting to answer the *"in spite of"* clause, which is the 2022 question's real demand.

#### RAPID REVISION

- Datum: shell-silver.
- Prābhākara akhyāti/vivekākhyāti: two valid cognitions, difference not apprehended; **no false
  cognition**.
- Bhāṭṭa viparīta-khyāti: one false cognition, caused by defect, corrected later.
- Nyāya anyathā-khyāti: real silver elsewhere, displaced here.
- Advaita anirvacanīya-khyāti: neither real nor unreal.
- 2022 answer: same thesis, two defence strategies — constituents versus principle.

---

### SESSION 12 — HOW COGNITION IS KNOWN: THREEFOLD AWARENESS, KNOWNNESS, AFTER-COGNITION

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition.** You know the pot. Fine. But how do you know *that you know* the pot?
Is the knowing itself lit up in the act, or must you find it out afterwards — and if afterwards, by
what route?

**Technical definition.** Prābhākara holds **threefold awareness (tripuṭī-saṃvit / tripuṭī-pratyakṣa)**:
every cognition simultaneously reveals knower (**jñātṛ**), known (**jñeya**) and knowing (**jñāna**),
because cognition is **self-luminous (svaprakāśa)**. Bhāṭṭa denies self-luminosity and holds that
cognition is *inferred* from the object's acquired property of **knownness (jñātatā)**. Nyāya holds
that cognition is known by a distinct second-order **after-cognition (anuvyavasāya)**.

#### SIMPLE START — THE GATEWAY IDEA

Three answers to the same question, and one very good analogy each.

*Prābhākara:* cognition is a **lamp**. A lamp does not need a second lamp to be seen; it shows itself
in the very act of showing the room. So knowing shows itself in the very act of showing the pot.

*Bhāṭṭa:* cognition is a **painter**. A painter paints objects but does not paint himself. You do not
see the painter; you see the painted canvas and conclude that a painter was here. Similarly, you find
the pot now bearing the property of *having-been-known*, and from that you infer that a pot-cognition
occurred.

*Nyāya:* cognition is an **event that a later observer reports**. You have the first-order cognition,
and then a distinct inner awareness takes that cognition as its object: "I know the pot."

⚠️ All three are trying to solve the same problem — how to stop an infinite regress of
cognitions-of-cognitions — and each solves it differently. Say that once, and the comparison stops
being a list and becomes an argument.

#### VISUAL — THREE SOLUTIONS TO ONE REGRESS

```text
   THE PROBLEM
     if a cognition is NOT manifest in itself, another cognition must reveal it
     ... and that one needs a third ... and so on  ⇒  INFINITE REGRESS

   ┌─── PRĀBHĀKARA ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ ONE ACT, THREE DISCLOSURES        knower (jñātṛ) ─┐                                 │
   │ cognition is SELF-LUMINOUS        known  (jñeya) ─┼── revealed TOGETHER, at once    │
   │ (svaprakāśa)                      knowing(jñāna) ─┘                                 │
   │ LAMP ANALOGY: a lamp reveals itself and other things at once                        │
   │ REGRESS BLOCKED because no second cognition is ever required                        │
   │ ⇒ NO METACOGNITIVE GAP                                                              │
   └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
   ┌─── BHĀṬṬA ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ cognition is NOT self-luminous                                                      │
   │ step 1: the object, having been cognised, acquires a NEW relational status          │
   │ step 2: that status is KNOWNNESS (jñātatā)                                          │
   │ step 3: observing the object's knownness, we INFER that a cognition occurred        │
   │ ⇒ awareness of cognition is INFERENTIAL, not immediate                              │
   │ PAINTER ANALOGY: it paints the world but not itself                                 │
   │ CONSEQUENCE: a temporal AND logical GAP — "I know" first, "I know that I know" after│
   └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
   ┌─── NYĀYA ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ first-order cognition reveals the object                                            │
   │ a LATER inner awareness takes that cognition as its OBJECT                          │
   │ form: "I know the pot" / "I knew the pot"  =  AFTER-COGNITION (anuvyavasāya)         │
   │ ⇒ neither self-luminosity nor inference from knownness: SECOND-ORDER APPREHENSION   │
   │ ⇒ REFLECTIVE GAP                                                                    │
   └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

   ┌──────────────┬───────────────────────────────┬──────────────┬────────────────────────┐
   │ SCHOOL       │ HOW COGNITION IS KNOWN        │ SELF-LUMINOUS│ MECHANISM              │
   ├──────────────┼───────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼────────────────────────┤
   │ PRĀBHĀKARA   │ simultaneously, in every      │ YES          │ tripuṭī-saṃvit         │
   │              │ cognition                     │              │                        │
   │ BHĀṬṬA       │ inferred after the fact       │ NO           │ jñātatā ⇒ inference    │
   │ NYĀYA        │ by a second-order cognition   │ NO           │ anuvyavasāya           │
   └──────────────┴───────────────────────────────┴──────────────┴────────────────────────┘
              no gap            inferential gap             reflective gap
```

*What the diagram shows: the regress problem at the top, three engineered solutions in the middle,
and the comparison table at the bottom. The three one-word gap-labels — none, inferential,
reflective — are the fastest way to keep the theories apart under exam pressure.*

#### CORE UPSC — THE ARGUMENT IN FULL

1. **Why the issue arises at all.** ✅ Once object-knowledge is admitted, a second question appears:
   how is the cognition itself known? Is knowing self-revealing, inferred, or grasped in a
   second-order act? ⚠️ Mīmāṃsā and Nyāya diverge sharply here — and Mīmāṃsā diverges *internally*,
   which is what makes the topic examinable at twenty marks.

2. **Prābhākara's argument, in six steps.**
   (i) Cognition illuminates its object.
   (ii) If cognition were not itself manifest, another cognition would be required to reveal it.
   (iii) That second cognition would need a third, leading to regress.
   (iv) Therefore cognition must reveal itself in the very act of revealing the object.
   (v) The subject is also disclosed as the one who knows.
   (vi) Thus one act yields a threefold revelation: **jñātṛ, jñeya, jñāna**.

3. **Presupposition and precision.** ✅ Cognition is self-luminous (**svaprakāśa**); awareness need
   not wait for a reflective duplicate in order to be present to itself. ⚠️ This is **not** the claim
   that the self alone shines and the object is projected. It is the claim that cognition is
   immediately present *in* revealing the object — a distinction that separates Prābhākara from
   idealist readings.

4. **The lamp analogy and what it buys.** ✅ A lamp reveals itself and other things at once. ✅ The
   analogy supports epistemic economy and blocks the regress in one stroke.

5. **The standing objection to Prābhākara and his reply.**
   ❌ *Then why do we ever need reflective statements like "I know that I know"?*
   ✅ *Reply:* reflection is a later conceptual articulation of what was already manifest
   pre-reflectively.
   ❌ *A second objection:* simultaneous revelation confuses subject, object and cognition.
   ✅ *Reply:* they are simultaneously *disclosed*, not ontologically *collapsed*.

6. **Bhāṭṭa's argument, in four steps.**
   (i) An object, after being cognised, stands in a new relation: it is now known.
   (ii) This new status is **jñātatā**.
   (iii) Observing the object's knownness, we infer that a cognition has occurred.
   (iv) Therefore awareness of cognition is inferential, not immediate.

7. **Presupposition, analogy and consequence.** ✅ Objects can bear relational properties grounded in
   cognition, and such properties can serve as inferential bases. ✅ Cognition is like a painter who
   paints objects but does not paint himself: it reveals the world but not itself directly, and its
   occurrence is established through what it leaves behind in the object. ✅ The consequence is a
   **temporal and logical gap** between first-order knowing and second-order awareness of that
   knowing: I know first; I know-that-I-know afterward.

8. **The standing objection to Bhāṭṭa and his reply.**
   ❌ *Knownness seems an unnecessary mysterious property.*
   ✅ *Reply:* Bhāṭṭa accepts this cost in order to avoid the stronger thesis of self-luminosity.
   ❌ *A second objection:* ordinary self-awareness feels too immediate for inference.
   ✅ *Reply:* immediacy in experience need not equal non-inferential structure.

9. **Nyāya's after-cognition, in four steps.**
   (i) First-order cognition reveals the object.
   (ii) A later inner awareness takes that cognition as object.
   (iii) It has the form "I know the pot" or "I knew the pot."
   (iv) Therefore knowing-that-I-know is neither self-luminosity nor inference from knownness but
   second-order apprehension.
   ❌ *Objection:* second-order awareness threatens regress.
   ✅ *Reply:* Nyāya treats the higher-order awareness as sufficient without endless repetition.

10. **The three-gap summary that keeps them apart.** ✅ Prābhākara = **no metacognitive gap**. ✅
    Bhāṭṭa = **inferential gap**. ✅ Nyāya = **reflective second-order gap**. ⚠️ These are three
    distinct solutions to the same problem, and naming the problem — regress — is what turns a
    comparison into an argument.

11. **Why this session is worth twenty marks.** ⚠️ The 2024 question asks "How do I know that I
    know?" with all three schools named. The examiner is testing whether you can (a) state the
    regress problem, (b) give three mechanisms with their analogies, and (c) show that they are
    responses to one shared pressure rather than three unrelated opinions.

#### NAMED EVIDENCE YOU CAN CITE

- ✅ Prabhākara's threefold awareness with the technical triad **jñātṛ / jñeya / jñāna**, and the
  lamp analogy.
- ✅ Kumārila's **jñātatā** with the painter analogy and the inferential structure it implies.
- ✅ Nyāya's **anuvyavasāya** in the form "I know the pot," with the reply that higher-order awareness
  need not repeat endlessly.
- ✅ The three-row comparison table of mechanism and self-luminosity.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> "How do I know that I know?" is not three separate doctrines but one regress met three ways: the
> Prābhākara stops it by letting cognition light itself, the Bhāṭṭa stops it by reading cognition off
> the mark it leaves on its object, and the Naiyāyika stops it by permitting exactly one further act
> of awareness and no more.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

| Keyword | Why the examiner wants it |
|---|---|
| **threefold awareness (tripuṭī-saṃvit)** | Prābhākara's mechanism |
| **knower / known / knowing (jñātṛ / jñeya / jñāna)** | The technical triad |
| **self-luminosity (svaprakāśa)** | The property in dispute |
| **knownness (jñātatā)** | Bhāṭṭa's mechanism |
| **after-cognition (anuvyavasāya)** | Nyāya's mechanism |
| **regress (anavasthā)** | The shared problem |
| **lamp / painter analogies** | Two ready-made illustrations |

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — CLAIM -> NAMED EVIDENCE -> ANALYSIS -> QUALIFICATION

> **Claim.** The three classical answers to how a cognition is known are best understood as three
> engineered stopping-points for a single regress, and they differ in where they allow the process to
> halt.
>
> **Named evidence.** Prabhākara argues that if cognition were not self-manifest a second cognition
> would be needed and a third after that, and concludes that one act discloses knower, known and
> knowing together, comparing cognition to a lamp; Kumārila denies self-luminosity and infers the
> cognition from the object's acquired knownness (**jñātatā**), comparing cognition to a painter who
> does not paint himself; Nyāya posits a distinct after-cognition (**anuvyavasāya**) of the form "I
> know the pot."
>
> **Analysis.** The three positions can therefore be ranked by the size of the gap they tolerate
> between knowing and knowing-that-one-knows: none for the Prābhākara, an inferential gap for the
> Bhāṭṭa, a reflective gap for the Naiyāyika — and each gap-size carries a cost, respectively the
> risk of conflating the three disclosed items, the positing of an obscure relational property, and
> the reappearance of the very regress the higher-order act was meant to stop.
>
> **Qualification.** ⚠️ Each school has an answer to its own cost, so the honest verdict is that the
> dispute is unresolved rather than won: what a candidate can assert confidently is only that
> Kumārila's combination of non-self-luminous cognition with intrinsic validity is philosophically
> stable, which is what Session 13 exploits.

#### EXAM APPLICATION — WHERE THIS HAS BEEN ASKED

- **2024, Q8(a), 20 marks** — "How do I know that I know? Answer with reference to the Naiyāyikas,
  the Bhaṭṭa Mīmāṃsakas and the Prābhākaras." Three named columns; this session supplies all three.
- **2018, Q8(a), 20 marks** — the self-luminosity half of that question is established here and
  applied in Session 13.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Swapping the mechanisms: threefold awareness is **Prābhākara**, knownness is **Bhāṭṭa**.
2. ⚠️ Describing Nyāya as accepting self-luminosity. It denies it and supplies a higher-order act.
3. ⚠️ Presenting the three views as a list without naming the regress they are all answering.
4. ⚠️ Reading Prābhākara's threefold awareness as idealism — as if the object were merely projected.
5. ⚠️ Confusing knownness (**jñātatā**) with knownness-as-truth; it is a relational status of the
   object, not a validity-conferring property.

#### RAPID REVISION

- Problem: regress of cognitions-of-cognitions.
- Prābhākara: self-luminous; one act discloses knower, known, knowing; **lamp**; no gap.
- Bhāṭṭa: not self-luminous; object acquires **knownness**; cognition inferred; **painter**;
  inferential gap.
- Nyāya: **after-cognition** "I know the pot"; reflective gap.
- Three gaps: none / inferential / reflective.

---
### SESSION 13 — DOES SELF-LUMINOSITY ENTAIL INTRINSIC VALIDITY?

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition.** Two different questions are constantly run together. The first is
*how does a cognition show up?* The second is *is a cognition entitled to be believed?* Showing up and
being true are not the same thing, and the whole 2018 twenty-marker turns on keeping them apart.

**Technical definition.** **Self-luminosity (svayaṃprakāśatva / svaprakāśatva)** concerns whether
cognition or consciousness is manifest without another cognition. **Intrinsic validity
(svataḥ-prāmāṇya)** concerns whether a cognition is entitled to *prima facie* truth without external
certification. They answer different questions — manifestation and truth — and therefore neither
thesis follows from the other merely by definition.

#### SIMPLE START — THE GATEWAY IDEA

Think of a witness in a courtroom. Two questions can be asked about him.

*Question one:* is he visible? Does he need someone else to point him out, or does he step forward
himself?

*Question two:* is he to be believed? Does the court trust him until he is impeached, or must
corroboration be produced first?

These questions plainly come apart. A witness can be perfectly visible and thoroughly untrustworthy;
another can be difficult to locate and, once located, entirely credible.

⚠️ Now the philosophical payoff. If someone claims that self-manifestation *entails* trustworthiness,
you defeat the claim by producing a single case of one without the other. Indian philosophy supplies
that case from *inside* Mīmāṃsā — and that is why the 2018 question, which looks like a
three-school survey, actually has a decisive answer.

#### VISUAL — THE FOUR-SCHOOL GRID AND THE DECISIVE COUNTEREXAMPLE

```text
                        TWO INDEPENDENT QUESTIONS
        Q1  MANIFESTATION : is cognition/consciousness self-luminous?
        Q2  TRUTH-ENTITLEMENT : is validity intrinsic?

   ┌──────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┬───────────────┬──────────────────┐
   │ SCHOOL               │ Q1 SELF-LUMINOUS?          │ Q2 VALIDITY   │ CONSEQUENCE FOR  │
   │                      │                            │ INTRINSIC?    │ THE 2018 ANSWER  │
   ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────┤
   │ NYĀYA                │ NO — known through a        │ NO — truth is │ accepts NEITHER  │
   │                      │ subsequent after-cognition  │ ascertained   │ thesis           │
   │                      │ (anuvyavasāya)              │ extrinsically │                  │
   ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────┤
   │ PRĀBHĀKARA MĪMĀṂSĀ   │ YES — one cognition         │ YES           │ CONNECTS both    │
   │                      │ manifests knower, known     │               │ theses           │
   │                      │ and knowing                 │               │                  │
   ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────┤
   │ BHĀṬṬA MĪMĀṂSĀ       │ NO — cognition is inferred  │ YES           │ ★ DECISIVE       │
   │                      │ from acquired knownness     │               │ COUNTEREXAMPLE   │
   │                      │ (jñātatā)                   │               │ TO ENTAILMENT    │
   ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────┤
   │ ADVAITA VEDĀNTA      │ YES — fundamentally, of     │ at the        │ CONNECTS them    │
   │                      │ consciousness ITSELF        │ empirical     │ inside a wider   │
   │                      │ (not merely of episodes)    │ level, prima  │ NON-DUAL theory  │
   │                      │                             │ facie until   │                  │
   │                      │                             │ SUBLATED      │                  │
   └──────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┴───────────────┴──────────────────┘

   THE ARGUMENT IN ONE LINE
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ Kumārila = NOT self-luminous  +  intrinsically valid                                │
   │ ⇒ self-luminosity is NOT NECESSARY for intrinsic validity                           │
   │ ⇒ and since the two theses answer different questions, neither entails the other    │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

   ⚠ THE TRAP THE 2018 PAPER IS SETTING
     Nyāya is NOT "self-luminous but extrinsically valid."
     Nyāya DENIES self-luminosity and supplies a higher-order cognition instead.
     Writing Nyāya into the wrong cell destroys the entire comparative grid.
```

*What the diagram shows: a two-question grid, four schools placed in it, and the one cell — Bhāṭṭa —
that settles the question by exhibiting the combination the entailment-thesis says is impossible.*

#### CORE UPSC — THE ARGUMENT IN FULL

1. **State the two theses separately, in one sentence each.** ✅ Self-luminosity concerns how
   cognition manifests itself and/or its object. ✅ Intrinsic validity concerns a cognition's *prima
   facie* truth and action-guiding authority before a defeater appears.

2. **Establish that they are different questions.** ✅ One is about manifestation, the other about
   truth. ⚠️ Because they are different questions, no entailment can be read off from the definitions
