   alone; an entailment would have to be *argued* for, and no school supplies such an argument.

3. **The four placements, stated exactly.**
   ✅ **Nyāya:** cognition is known through a subsequent cognition (**anuvyavasāya**) — *not*
   self-luminous — and truth is ascertained extrinsically (**parataḥ-prāmāṇya**). It accepts neither
   thesis.
   ✅ **Prābhākara Mīmāṃsā:** one cognition manifests knower, known and knowing — self-luminous — and
   validity is intrinsic. It connects both theses.
   ✅ **Bhāṭṭa Mīmāṃsā:** cognition is *not* self-luminous and is inferred from the object's acquired
   knownness (**jñātatā**), yet validity *is* intrinsic. **This is the decisive counterexample.**
   ✅ **Advaita Vedānta:** self-luminosity holds fundamentally of consciousness itself; at the
   empirical level cognition carries *prima facie* authority until sublated. It connects the two
   within a wider non-dual theory.

4. **The decisive move, stated for the examiner.** ✅ Kumārila's combination — non-self-luminous
   cognition together with intrinsic validity — shows most clearly that **self-luminosity does not
   entail intrinsic validity, and intrinsic validity does not require self-luminosity.** ⚠️ A single
   internal counterexample defeats a necessity-claim; you do not need to survey every school to
   settle the question, though the survey is what the question asks you to display.

5. **Why Advaita is included in the question at all.** ⚠️ Advaita extends self-luminosity from
   cognitive *episodes* to consciousness *as such*, which is a stronger and differently grounded
   thesis; and its account of validity is embedded in a doctrine of levels of reality and sublation.
   Including it shows that the two theses can be connected for reasons quite unlike Prābhākara's.

6. **The distinction that must not be lost in the writing.** ⚠️ Mīmāṃsā debates cognition-episodes
   and validity within a theory of instruments; Advaita debates consciousness and levels of reality.
   Using the same English word "self-luminous" for both is legitimate only if the difference of scope
   is stated.

7. **The standing objection and its reply.** ❓ *Objection:* intrinsic validity seems unable to
   explain error. ✅ *Reply:* falsity is extrinsically discovered through sublation or causal defect;
   cognition presents itself as valid until defeated. ⚠️ This links Session 13 back to Sessions 4 and
   11, and a good 20-mark answer makes that link explicitly.

8. **The answer to the question as printed.** ✅ **No** — the admission of self-luminosity does not
   *necessarily* lead to the admission of intrinsic validity. The two doctrines are logically
   independent; they happen to travel together in Prābhākara and in Advaita, and they come apart in
   Bhāṭṭa, while Nyāya rejects both. ⚠️ State the verdict early and then justify it; a survey that
   never returns a verdict loses the "discuss" mark.

#### NAMED EVIDENCE YOU CAN CITE

- ✅ The four-row grid of self-luminosity against intrinsic validity across Nyāya, Prābhākara, Bhāṭṭa
  and Advaita.
- ✅ Kumārila's combination as the counterexample that disproves necessary entailment.
- ✅ Nyāya's **anuvyavasāya** as the reason Nyāya is placed in the "neither" row.
- ⚠️ Advaita's extension of self-luminosity to consciousness as such, with validity holding at the
  empirical level until sublation.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Kumārila is the standing refutation of the entailment: he holds that a cognition cannot show itself
> and yet insists that it may be believed on arrival — which proves that being manifest and being
> credible are two properties, not one property under two names.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

| Keyword | Why the examiner wants it |
|---|---|
| **self-luminosity (svayaṃprakāśatva)** | The first thesis |
| **intrinsic validity (svataḥ-prāmāṇya)** | The second thesis |
| **manifestation versus truth-entitlement** | The distinction that decides the question |
| **knownness (jñātatā)** | Why Bhāṭṭa is the counterexample |
| **after-cognition (anuvyavasāya)** | Why Nyāya is in the "neither" row |
| **sublation (bādha)** | Advaita's qualification of empirical validity |
| **counterexample to necessary entailment** | The verdict, in technical form |

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — CLAIM -> NAMED EVIDENCE -> ANALYSIS -> QUALIFICATION

> **Claim.** The admission of self-luminosity does not necessarily lead to the admission of intrinsic
> validity, because the two doctrines answer different questions and can be held separately.
>
> **Named evidence.** Nyāya denies both, holding that cognition is known by a subsequent
> after-cognition (**anuvyavasāya**) and that validity is ascertained extrinsically; Prābhākara
> affirms both; Advaita affirms both but grounds self-luminosity in consciousness as such and treats
> empirical validity as holding until sublation; and Kumārila affirms intrinsic validity while
> expressly denying self-luminosity, inferring the cognition from the object's acquired knownness
> (**jñātatā**).
>
> **Analysis.** The Bhāṭṭa position is therefore not one opinion among four but the logically
> decisive datum: a single actual case of intrinsic validity without self-luminosity is sufficient to
> refute a claim of necessity, and the survey of the other three schools serves to show that the
> pairing found in Prābhākara and Advaita is a substantive philosophical commitment rather than a
> definitional consequence.
>
> **Qualification.** ⚠️ The independence claim should not be over-read: nothing here shows that the
> two theses are *unrelated*, since a self-luminous cognition plainly makes intrinsic validity easier
> to defend, and Advaita's version connects them within a non-dual framework that Mīmāṃsā would
> reject for entirely separate reasons.

#### EXAM APPLICATION — WHERE THIS HAS BEEN ASKED

- **2018, Q8(a), 20 marks** — "Does the admission of Svayaṃprakāśavāda necessarily lead to the
  admission of Svataḥprāmāṇyavāda? Discuss after the Naiyāyikas, the Mīmāṃsakas and the Advaita
  Vedāntins." The grid and the counterexample are the complete answer.
- Supports **2025, Q5(c)** and **2024, Q8(a)**, both of which reuse the same distinctions.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Placing Nyāya as "self-luminous but extrinsically valid." Nyāya denies self-luminosity.
2. ⚠️ Treating "the Mīmāṃsakas" as one column. The question is decided *inside* Mīmāṃsā.
3. ⚠️ Using "self-luminous" for Advaita and for Prābhākara without noting the difference of scope —
   consciousness as such versus cognitive episodes.
4. ⚠️ Surveying the schools and never answering the "necessarily" in the question.
5. ⚠️ Confusing sublation (Advaita's **bādha**) with Mīmāṃsā's defeater (**bādhaka**) as if the two
   frameworks were identical.

#### RAPID REVISION

- Two questions: manifestation (self-luminosity) versus truth-entitlement (intrinsic validity).
- Nyāya: neither. Prābhākara: both. Advaita: both, at a deeper level. Bhāṭṭa: intrinsic validity
  **without** self-luminosity.
- Bhāṭṭa is the counterexample ⇒ no necessary entailment, in either direction.
- Verdict first, then the grid, then the qualification.

---

### SESSION 14 — SENTENCE-UNITY AND THE TWO RIVAL SEMANTICS

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition.** A sentence is many words, each apparently carrying its own meaning,
and yet it produces *one* understanding. How? Two Mīmāṃsā answers: either the words mean things
first and the meanings are joined afterwards, or the words mean things only *as already joined*.

**Technical definition.** The problem is **sentence-unity (ekavākyatā)**. Bhāṭṭa holds
**connection-of-the-already-denoted (abhihitānvayavāda)**: each word first denotes its isolated
meaning (**padārtha**) through its primary denotative power (**abhidhā**), and sentence-meaning
arises through a secondary connecting operation, often explained as indirect signification
(**lakṣaṇā**) or purport-power (**tātparya-śakti**). Prābhākara holds
**denotation-of-the-connected (anvitābhidhānavāda)**: words denote their meanings only as already
connected with the other meanings in the sentence.

#### SIMPLE START — THE GATEWAY IDEA

Take a set of loose bricks and a wall.

Bhāṭṭa's picture: you have bricks first — "bring," "the," "cow" — each with a fixed shape it carries
into every wall it ever joins. Building the wall is a further operation performed on already-shaped
bricks. This explains beautifully why the same brick can appear in a thousand different walls without
changing shape.

Prābhākara's picture: there are no loose bricks. What you actually encounter, always, is a wall. A
child does not learn "cow" from a dictionary and later assemble it into a command; the child hears
*"bring the cow,"* watches someone bring a cow, and learns the word as *the-thing-to-be-brought*. The
"brick" is an abstraction we make afterwards by comparing many walls.

⚠️ Both are explaining the same phenomenon and both accept the same three conditions of sentence
formation. The disagreement is entirely about **which unit is semantically primary** — and therefore
about whether meaning is compositional or contextual at bottom.

#### VISUAL — THE SHARED CONDITIONS AND THE TWO ROUTES

```text
   ══════════ SHARED BY BOTH SUB-SCHOOLS: THE CONDITIONS OF SENTENCE-UNITY ══════════
   ┌──────────────────┬───────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬────────────────┐
   │ CONDITION        │ MEANING                   │ FAILURE CASE             │ WHY IT MATTERS │
   ├──────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼────────────────┤
   │ ākāṅkṣā          │ words mutually REQUIRE    │ "Bring..." leaves an     │ explains why   │
   │ (mutual          │ completion                │ open expectation of      │ words lean     │
   │  expectancy)     │                           │ WHAT is to be brought    │ toward each    │
   │                  │                           │                          │ other          │
   ├──────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼────────────────┤
   │ yogyatā          │ meanings must be          │ "sprinkle with fire" is  │ blocks absurd  │
   │ (semantic        │ semantically COMPATIBLE   │ unfit in ordinary usage  │ constructions  │
   │  fitness)        │                           │                          │                │
   ├──────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼────────────────┤
   │ sannidhi/āsatti  │ words must occur in       │ words separated by       │ protects Vedic │
   │ (proximity,      │ proper PROXIMITY          │ unrelated discourse fail │ sentence-      │
   │  contiguity)     │                           │ to cohere                │ boundaries     │
   ├──────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼────────────────┤
   │ tātparya         │ ⚠ purport / intended      │ works for HUMAN speech   │ ⚠ PROBLEM FOR  │
   │ (purport)        │ thrust — added by         │                          │ AN AUTHORLESS  │
   │ [Nyāya adds]     │ Naiyāyikas                │                          │ VEDA           │
   └──────────────────┴───────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴────────────────┘
   ⚠ MĪMĀṂSĀ CANNOT ground Vedic meaning in a speaker's intention, because the Veda is
     authorless (apauruṣeya). "Purport" must therefore be located in TEXTUAL FORCE,
     CONTEXTUAL UNITY and INJUNCTIONAL FUNCTION — not in an intending author.

   ══════════════════════ THE TWO ROUTES TO ONE MEANING ══════════════════════
   TEST SENTENCE: "svargakāmo yajeta" — "one desiring heaven should sacrifice"

   BHĀṬṬA — abhihitānvayavāda            │  PRĀBHĀKARA — anvitābhidhānavāda
   "connection of the already denoted"   │  "denotation of the connected"
   ──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────
   step 1  each word denotes its ISOLATED│  there is NO step 1 of this kind
           meaning via abhidhā           │
   step 2  meanings are CONNECTED via    │  words denote meanings ONLY AS CONNECTED
           lakṣaṇā / tātparya-śakti      │  with agent, action, object, context
   ──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────
   PRIMARY UNIT = THE WORD               │  PRIMARY UNIT = THE SENTENCE
   sentence-meaning is DERIVATIVE        │  word-meaning is grasped IN RELATION
   ──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────
   STRENGTH: explains STABLE LEXICAL     │  STRENGTH: explains LANGUAGE-LEARNING
   MEANING across contexts               │  (vyutpatti) and ACTION-GUIDANCE
   ──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────
   ⚠ two stages are LOGICAL, not a slow  │  ⚠ does NOT deny that words are repeatable;
   temporal sequence in consciousness    │  denies that their PRIMARY life is isolated
   ──────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────
   VERDICT LINE: Bhāṭṭa secures stable lexical meaning; Prābhākara secures contextual
                 action-guidance.
```

*What the diagram shows: three shared conditions plus one contested fourth, then the two routes set
side by side with their strengths and their standard clarifications — which is exactly the shape a
10-mark "how do they differ" answer should take.*

#### CORE UPSC — THE ARGUMENT IN FULL

1. **The problem, stated as Mīmāṃsā states it.** ✅ The central semantic problem is how a sentence
   made of many words, each apparently carrying its own meaning, yields one unified cognition called
   **ekavākyatā**. ⚠️ Mīmāṃsā *must* solve this, because Vedic obligation is grasped through
   sentence-meaning and not through isolated word-meanings.

2. **Why the problem is not artificial.** ✅ The Veda works through meaningful sentences —
   injunctions, prohibitions, mantras, names, explanatory passages. ✅ An injunction cannot guide
   action unless its words form one determinate action-guiding meaning. ✅ Mere succession does not
   produce unity, since unrelated words can be uttered side by side. ✅ Therefore the tradition must
   specify the conditions under which a sequence of words becomes a single sentence.
   ❌ *Objection:* ordinary people understand sentences instantly; a technical theory seems
   unnecessary. ✅ *Reply:* Mīmāṃsā is explaining the *logical structure* of comprehension, especially
   for Vedic interpretation — not claiming that every hearer performs scholastic analysis.

3. **The three shared conditions.** ✅ **Mutual expectancy (ākāṅkṣā):** without it, words do not
   demand one another and no single meaning arises. ✅ **Semantic fitness (yogyatā):** without it,
   connected words may still generate an impossible meaning. ✅ **Proximity (sannidhi / āsatti):**
   without it, even fit and expectant words may belong to different sentences.

4. **The fourth condition and the Mīmāṃsā difficulty.** ⚠️ Naiyāyikas often add **purport
   (tātparya)** — the speaker's intention or intended thrust. ⚠️ Mīmāṃsā cannot ground Vedic meaning
   in a speaker's intention because the Veda is authorless, so any "purport" must be located in
   textual force, contextual unity and injunctional function.
   ❌ *Objection:* if purport is needed for human sentences, authorless Vedic sentences become
   unintelligible. ✅ *Reply:* Vedic purport is not psychological intention but sentence-function
   disclosed by textual relations, especially subordination to injunction (**vidhi**).
   ⚠️ This creates a real internal pressure, and a strong answer says so: *the refusal of
   speaker-intention forces Mīmāṃsā to make purport impersonal, textual and rule-governed.*

5. **Bhāṭṭa's connection-of-the-denoted, in six steps.**
   (i) Words such as "cow," "bring" and "heaven-desirer" have repeatable lexical meanings.
   (ii) These meanings are grasped when the corresponding words are heard.
   (iii) The isolated meanings are not yet a full sentence-meaning.
   (iv) They become one meaning only when related through expectancy, fitness and proximity.
   (v) Comprehension therefore has two logical stages: denotation of word-meanings, then connection
   of those meanings.
   (vi) This preserves the independent force of words and explains stable lexical meaning across
   contexts.
   ✅ Presupposition: word-meanings can be grasped independently enough to be available for later
   syntactic connection; sentence meaning is **compositional**.
   ⚠️ Distinction: the two stages are primarily *logical*, not a slow temporal sequence — Bhāṭṭa need
   not claim the hearer pauses after each word.
   ❌ *Objection:* the theory multiplies operations, seeming to require indirect signification or a
   special connecting power in every sentence. ✅ *Reply:* that is the cost of explaining how stable
   word-meanings combine into determinate sentence-meaning; the operation is structural, not an
   arbitrary extra act.

6. **Prābhākara's denotation-of-the-connected, in six steps.**
   (i) In actual learning and use, words are encountered within meaningful action-guiding sentences.
   (ii) The child does not first master isolated dictionary meanings and then build sentences.
   (iii) The standard **learning argument (vyutpatti)** says a child observes an elder command
   "bring the cow" and a younger person comply.
   (iv) From this whole event the child learns the connected sense of the words inside a practical
   sentence.
   (v) Therefore words signify meanings already connected with action, agent, object and context.
   (vi) Sentence-comprehension is not a later synthesis of atomised meanings but the original mode of
   linguistic understanding.
   ✅ Presupposition: no meaning is grasped outside some context of use; language is primarily
   action-guiding, which fits Mīmāṃsā's injunction-centred hermeneutics.
   ⚠️ Distinction: Prabhākara does not deny that words are repeatable; he denies that their primary
   semantic life is an isolated lexical object detached from sentential connection.
   ❌ *Objection:* if a word never means anything in isolation, dictionaries, grammar lessons and
   single-word utterances become inexplicable. ✅ *Reply:* these are secondary abstractions from
   repeated connected uses; primary meaning remains relational.

7. **The four cross-objections, which convert a description into a debate.**
   ❌ *Against Bhāṭṭa:* the isolated-word stage is not phenomenologically observed and makes every
   sentence depend on an extra indirect-signification or purport-operation. ✅ *Reply:* the
   distinction is explanatory, not introspective; stable lexical meaning requires word-priority.
   ❌ *Against Prābhākara:* if words signify only connected meanings, the same word would seem to
   mean differently in every sentence. ✅ *Reply:* recurrent connected uses yield stable practical
   competence without positing context-free meanings.
   ❌ *Against Bhāṭṭa:* language-learning begins in sentences, not in isolated word-lists. ✅ *Reply:*
   learning-history does not settle semantic structure; what is learned in context can still be the
   word's repeatable meaning.
   ❌ *Against Prābhākara:* single-word commands such as "Cow!" or "Fire!" seem meaningful. ✅
   *Reply:* such utterances are elliptical sentences supplied by context, not proof of isolated
   word-meaning.

8. **The verdict line.** ⚠️ **Bhāṭṭa secures stable lexical meaning; Prābhākara secures contextual
   action-guidance.** Neither claim is refuted by the other, and the honest close is that the two
   theories capture different halves of a genuine phenomenon.

#### NAMED EVIDENCE YOU CAN CITE

- ✅ The test sentence **"svargakāmo yajeta"** — "one desiring heaven should sacrifice" — which the
  hearer must grasp as a unified injunction upon a qualified agent, not as "heaven-desirer" plus
  "sacrifice."
- ✅ The three shared conditions with their failure cases: "Bring..." for expectancy, "sprinkle with
  fire" for fitness, separated discourse for proximity.
- ✅ Bhāṭṭa's denotative power (**abhidhā**) plus indirect signification (**lakṣaṇā**) or
  purport-power (**tātparya-śakti**).
- ✅ Prābhākara's learning argument (**vyutpatti**): elder commands, younger complies, child learns.
- ✅ The four-row cross-objection table.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The Bhāṭṭa hears a sentence as words that have been joined; the Prābhākara hears words as a
> sentence that has been analysed — and the whole dispute is about which of those two descriptions
> reports the original event and which reports the later reconstruction.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

| Keyword | Why the examiner wants it |
|---|---|
| **sentence-unity (ekavākyatā)** | The problem, correctly named |
| **connection-of-the-denoted (abhihitānvayavāda)** | Bhāṭṭa's thesis |
| **denotation-of-the-connected (anvitābhidhānavāda)** | Prābhākara's thesis |
| **expectancy (ākāṅkṣā) · fitness (yogyatā) · proximity (sannidhi)** | The three shared conditions |
| **purport (tātparya)** | The contested fourth, and the authorless-Veda problem |
| **denotative power (abhidhā) / indirect signification (lakṣaṇā)** | Bhāṭṭa's machinery |
| **learning argument (vyutpatti)** | Prābhākara's strongest evidence |
| **svargakāmo yajeta** | The standard test sentence |

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — CLAIM -> NAMED EVIDENCE -> ANALYSIS -> QUALIFICATION

> **Claim.** Bhāṭṭa and Prābhākara agree entirely about the conditions of sentence-unity and disagree
> only about which linguistic unit is semantically primary.
>
> **Named evidence.** Both require mutual expectancy (**ākāṅkṣā**), semantic fitness (**yogyatā**)
> and proximity (**sannidhi**) before a word-sequence becomes one sentence; Bhāṭṭa then holds that
> each word denotes its own meaning through **abhidhā** and that these meanings are connected by a
> further operation, while Prabhākara appeals to the learning situation (**vyutpatti**) — an elder
> commanding "bring the cow" and a younger complying — to argue that words are learned, and therefore
> denote, only as already connected.
>
> **Analysis.** The two theories are strong at opposite points: word-priority explains why "cow"
> carries the same meaning into a thousand sentences, whereas sentence-priority explains how the word
> was acquired in the first place and why the Veda's injunctions are grasped as demands rather than as
> assembled descriptions — which is why the Prābhākara account sits more naturally with the school's
> injunction-centred hermeneutics.
>
> **Qualification.** ⚠️ Neither side refutes the other, and both inherit a common difficulty: because
> the Veda has no author, the purport (**tātparya**) that Naiyāyikas locate in a speaker's intention
> must here be relocated into textual function and subordination to injunction, which is an
> intellectually demanding move rather than an obvious one.

#### EXAM APPLICATION — WHERE THIS HAS BEEN ASKED

- **2024, Q5(d), 10 marks** — "How does Bhaṭṭa's view of nature of word-meaning and sentential-
  meaning differ from Prabhākara's view? Critically discuss." Three shared conditions, two theses,
  two cross-objections and the verdict line fill the word limit exactly.
- **2023, Q8(c), 15 marks** — universals or particulars; that answer stands on this session's
  semantic frame (Session 15).

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Reversing the two labels. **Abhihitānvaya = Bhāṭṭa** (denoted-then-connected); **anvitābhidhāna
   = Prābhākara** (connected-then-denoted). Memorise by the first syllable pairing: **A-BHI-hita**
   with **BHĀ-ṭṭa**.
2. ⚠️ Presenting Bhāṭṭa's two stages as a temporal sequence in the hearer's consciousness.
3. ⚠️ Omitting the three shared conditions and writing only the disagreement.
4. ⚠️ Adding **tātparya** to the Mīmāṃsā list without flagging the authorless-Veda problem.
5. ⚠️ Claiming Prabhākara denies that words are repeatable.

#### RAPID REVISION

- Problem: **ekavākyatā** — how many words yield one cognition.
- Shared: expectancy · fitness · proximity. Contested fourth: purport (Nyāya adds it; Mīmāṃsā must
  impersonalise it).
- Bhāṭṭa: word first (**abhidhā**), then connection (**lakṣaṇā / tātparya-śakti**) ⇒ compositional.
- Prābhākara: connected denotation, supported by the learning argument (**vyutpatti**) ⇒ contextual.
- Verdict: stable lexicon versus action-guidance; test sentence **svargakāmo yajeta**.

---
### SESSION 15 — UNIVERSALS OR PARTICULARS, EXCLUSION, AND THE REJECTED SOUND-BURST

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition.** When you say "cow," what does the word actually pick out — the general
character shared by all cows, or the individual animal in front of you? Mīmāṃsā answers "the general
character, primarily"; Nyāya answers "the individual, as qualified"; and a Buddhist answers "neither
— the word merely excludes non-cows."

**Technical definition.** Mīmāṃsā generally holds that words primarily denote the **universal
(jāti)**, while Nyāya gives primacy to the **individual (vyakti)** qualified by universal and form.
The Buddhist alternative is **exclusion (apoha)**. The Grammarians add a third position,
**sound-burst theory (sphoṭa-vāda)**, on which sentence-meaning is grasped in a single indivisible
flash of **vākya-sphoṭa**; both Mīmāṃsā sub-schools reject it.

#### SIMPLE START — THE GATEWAY IDEA

Point at a cow and say the word. Something has to explain two facts at once.

*Fact one:* the same word works for a cow you have never seen, in a country you have never visited.
So the word cannot be tied to *this* animal.

*Fact two:* when the Veda says "bring the cow," an actual animal must be brought. So the word cannot
float free of individuals either.

Mīmāṃsā's solution: the word's *primary* grip is on cow-ness, and the individual is reached *through*
the universal as instantiated in a concrete context. Nyāya reverses the priority. ⚠️ Note that both
sides reach both — the dispute is about **semantic priority**, not about coverage, and caricaturing
either side as knowing only universals or only particulars is the standard way to lose this question.

#### VISUAL — THREE POSITIONS ON REFERENCE, AND THE FOURTH ON SENTENCE-MEANING

```text
   THE QUESTION:  do words refer to universals, particulars, or both?

   ┌──────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┐
   │ SCHOOL       │ PRIMARY DENOTATION               │ HOW THE OTHER IS REACHED          │
   ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
   │ MĪMĀṂSĀ      │ the UNIVERSAL (jāti) — cow-ness  │ the individual is reached THROUGH │
   │              │                                  │ the universal as instantiated in  │
   │              │                                  │ a concrete context                │
   ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
   │ NYĀYA        │ the INDIVIDUAL (vyakti), as      │ the universal is present AS THE   │
   │              │ qualified by universal and form  │ QUALIFIER of the individual       │
   ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
   │ BUDDHIST     │ NEITHER — the word functions by  │ apparent generality is a          │
   │ (apoha)      │ EXCLUDING non-cows               │ constructed negative shadow       │
   └──────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┘
   ⚠ BOTH Mīmāṃsā and Nyāya reach BOTH universal and individual.
     THE DIFFERENCE IS PRIORITY, NOT COVERAGE. Do not caricature either side.

   ═════════════ THE MĪMĀṂSĀ ARGUMENT (five steps) ═════════════
     1. the same word "cow" applies to MANY individual cows
     2. this REPEATABILITY is best explained by the word's relation to cow-ness
     3. the individual is then reached through the universal as instantiated
     4. Nyāya REVERSES the priority, taking the qualified individual as primary
     5. ∴ answers must not caricature either side
     PRESUPPOSITION: linguistic repeatability requires a STABLE SEMANTIC GROUND across
                     changing individuals.

   ═════════════ TWO OBJECTIONS AND TWO REPLIES ═════════════
   ❌ "A universal alone cannot be milked, brought or sacrificed."
   ✅ the universal is primary FOR MEANING, while ACTION reaches the qualified individual
      through contextual application.
   ❌ BUDDHIST EXCLUSION: no real universal is needed; "cow" works by excluding non-cows.
   ✅ pure exclusion cannot explain STABLE POSITIVE RECOGNITION and REPEATABLE APPLICATION
      unless a shared class-character is already available ⇒ word-use is grounded in a
      REAL universal, not only in negation.

   ═════════════ THE GRAMMARIAN THIRD POSITION — AND ITS REJECTION ═════════════
   ⚠ BHARTṚHARI / GRAMMARIANS — sphoṭa-vāda
        individual sounds PERISH and cannot by themselves yield the whole meaning
        ⇒ posit a unitary SOUND-BURST (vākya-sphoṭa) as the real bearer of meaning
                                   │
   ✅ MĪMĀṂSĀ REPLY (both sub-schools reject the extra entity)
        the eternal PHONEMES (varṇa) and their ordered manifestation are SUFFICIENT
        word-meaning + the sentence-conditions already explain comprehension
        ⇒ NO ADDITIONAL SPHOṬA IS REQUIRED
        ❌ "if letters are heard successively, how can they produce one meaning?"
        ✅ MEMORY of the ordered series + ākāṅkṣā + yogyatā + sannidhi is enough
        ⇒ preserves economy AND the theory of eternal word-units WITHOUT Grammarian holism
```

*What the diagram shows: the three-way reference dispute with its priority-not-coverage caution, the
five-step Mīmāṃsā argument, both objections with their replies, and the Grammarian position that a
15-mark answer can add for depth.*

#### CORE UPSC — THE ARGUMENT IN FULL

1. **The thesis, with its exact hedge.** ✅ Mīmāṃsā generally holds that words primarily denote the
   universal; Nyāya gives primacy to the individual qualified by universal and form. ⚠️ **Both
   positions can reach both;** the difference concerns semantic priority.

2. **The five-step argument.** (i) The same word "cow" applies to many individual cows. (ii) This
   repeatability is best explained, for Mīmāṃsā, by the word's relation to cow-ness. (iii) The
   individual is then reached through the universal as instantiated in a concrete context. (iv) Nyāya
   reverses the priority. (v) Therefore exam answers must not caricature either side.

3. **The presupposition.** ✅ Linguistic repeatability requires a *stable semantic ground* across
   changing individuals. ⚠️ This is why the dispute is not merely terminological: it is a dispute
   about what makes language possible at all.

4. **The worked example.** ✅ "Cow" can refer to this cow in front of me because the universal
   cow-ness is recognisable in that individual.

5. **The action objection and its reply.** ❌ A universal alone cannot be milked, brought or
   sacrificed. ✅ The universal is primary *for meaning*, while action reaches the qualified
   individual through contextual application. ⚠️ Notice how neatly this preserves the school's ritual
   commitments: an injunction must reach a real animal, and the theory of meaning does not prevent
   that.

6. **The Buddhist exclusion objection and its reply.** ❌ No real universal is needed; "cow" functions
   by excluding non-cows (**apoha**). ✅ Mīmāṃsā replies that pure exclusion cannot explain stable
   positive recognition and repeatable application unless a shared class-character is already
   available; word-use is therefore grounded in a real universal, not only in negation. ⚠️ This reply
   is also the semantic face of the realism defended in Session 7's two-stage perception, and saying
   so links two answers together.

7. **The Grammarian third position.** ⚠️ Bhartṛhari and the Grammarian tradition defend
   **sphoṭa-vāda**: sentence-meaning is grasped in a single indivisible flash of **vākya-sphoṭa**.
   The argument is that individual sounds perish and cannot by themselves yield the whole meaning, so
   a unitary sound-burst must be posited as the real bearer of meaning. ✅ Both Bhāṭṭa and Prābhākara
   reject the need for such an additional entity.

8. **The Mīmāṃsā reply to the Grammarian, in three steps.** ✅ The eternal phonemes (**varṇa**) and
   their ordered manifestation are sufficient. ✅ Since word-meaning and the sentence-conditions
   already explain comprehension, no additional sound-burst is required. ✅ This preserves Mīmāṃsā's
   economy and its theory of eternal word-units without accepting Grammarian holism.
   ❌ *Objection:* if letters are heard successively, how can they produce one meaning without a
   sound-burst? ✅ *Reply:* memory of the ordered series, together with expectancy, fitness and
   proximity, is enough to yield unity.
   ✅ Presupposition: the phonemes are real and stable enough to carry meaning without a separate
   indivisible language-entity.

9. **How a 15-marker on this question should be built.** ⚠️ Begin from sentence-unity and the shared
   conditions (Session 14); state the Mīmāṃsā universal-priority thesis with the repeatability
   argument; state the Nyāya reversal fairly with its qualified-individual formula; supply the cow /
   cow-ness example on both sides; answer the action objection; add the Buddhist exclusion challenge
   and the reply; and, if space permits, add the Grammarian position and its rejection for depth.
   Close on priority-not-coverage.

10. **The connection back to eternal words.** ⚠️ The rejection of the sound-burst is not an isolated
    polemic: it protects the doctrine that word-units are eternal and merely *manifested* by
    utterance, which Session 16 needs in order to defend authorlessness. The semantics and the
    scriptural defence are one structure.

#### NAMED EVIDENCE YOU CAN CITE

- ✅ The Mīmāṃsā universal-priority thesis and the Nyāya qualified-individual thesis, with the
  explicit "both reach both" caution.
- ✅ The repeatability argument and the cow / cow-ness example.
- ✅ The Buddhist **apoha** objection and the stable-positive-recognition reply.
- ⚠️ Bhartṛhari's **sphoṭa-vāda** and its rejection by both Mīmāṃsā sub-schools, with the eternal
  phonemes (**varṇa**) and ordered manifestation as the sufficient alternative.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Neither Mīmāṃsā nor Nyāya is confined to one side of the universal–particular divide; what is
> genuinely at stake is which of the two the word *reaches first*, and Mīmāṃsā answers "the
> universal" because nothing else explains how a word learned on one animal can be spent on a
> thousand.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

| Keyword | Why the examiner wants it |
|---|---|
| **universal (jāti)** | Mīmāṃsā's primary denotatum |
| **individual (vyakti), qualified** | Nyāya's primary denotatum |
| **semantic priority, not coverage** | The anti-caricature sentence |
| **repeatability** | The argument's engine |
| **exclusion (apoha)** | The Buddhist alternative |
| **sound-burst (sphoṭa) / sentence-sound-burst (vākya-sphoṭa)** | The Grammarian position, for depth |
| **phonemes (varṇa) and ordered manifestation** | Mīmāṃsā's economical alternative |

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — CLAIM -> NAMED EVIDENCE -> ANALYSIS -> QUALIFICATION

> **Claim.** Mīmāṃsā holds that words denote the universal primarily and the individual derivatively,
> while Nyāya reverses that order — so the dispute concerns semantic priority rather than the range
> of what language can reach.
>
> **Named evidence.** Mīmāṃsā argues from repeatability: the same word "cow" applies to indefinitely
> many individuals, which is explained by its relation to cow-ness, the individual being reached
> through the universal as instantiated; Nyāya takes the individual qualified by universal and form
> as primary; and the Buddhist **apoha** theory denies real universals altogether, treating "cow" as
> the exclusion of non-cows.
>
> **Analysis.** The Mīmāṃsā reply to exclusion is the decisive move for a realist semantics: pure
> negation cannot account for the stable positive recognition and repeatable application that
> language actually exhibits, since to exclude non-cows one must already be able to identify what
> counts as a cow — and the same realism reappears in the school's insistence that determinate
> perception interprets rather than falsifies the given.
>
> **Qualification.** ⚠️ The Mīmāṃsā position must still explain how a universal is milked or
> sacrificed, and its answer — that action reaches the qualified individual through contextual
> application — concedes that the individual is indispensable in practice even if secondary in
> meaning; the Grammarian sound-burst theory, though rejected here, shows that even within the
> orthodox camp the sufficiency of phonemes plus sentence-conditions is contested.

#### EXAM APPLICATION — WHERE THIS HAS BEEN ASKED

- **2023, Q8(c), 15 marks** — "Do words refer to universals or particulars or both? Present an
  exposition of Nyāya and Mīmāṃsā position ... along with suitable examples." The "or both" in the
  stem is precisely the priority-not-coverage point, and examples are explicitly demanded.
- Supports **2024, Q5(d)**, since the semantics of Session 14 and this session are one continuous
  argument.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Writing that Mīmāṃsā knows *only* universals and Nyāya *only* particulars. The stem's "or
   both" is a warning, not a decoration.
2. ⚠️ Omitting examples in a question that asks for them by name.
3. ⚠️ Attributing **sphoṭa-vāda** to Mīmāṃsā. Both sub-schools **reject** it.
4. ⚠️ Treating **apoha** as a minor curiosity; it is the strongest challenge to the Mīmāṃsā position
   and its refutation is worth marks.
5. ⚠️ Confusing the Grammarian eternal *sound-burst* with the Mīmāṃsā eternal *phoneme*; Mīmāṃsā's
   eternity claim is about word-units, not about an indivisible meaning-bearer.

#### RAPID REVISION

- Mīmāṃsā: universal (**jāti**) primary; individual reached through it.
- Nyāya: individual (**vyakti**) qualified by universal and form, primary.
- Both reach both — priority, not coverage.
- Engine: repeatability requires a stable semantic ground.
- Buddhist **apoha** answered by stable positive recognition.
- Grammarian **sphoṭa** rejected: phonemes + ordered manifestation + the three conditions suffice.

---

### SESSION 16 — THE AUTHORLESS VEDA AND THE CIRCULARITY CHARGE

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition.** The Veda is authoritative, says Mīmāṃsā, precisely because nobody
wrote it. A text with an author has somewhere for error, deceit, partiality and forgetfulness to
enter. A text with no author has no such place.

**Technical definition.** **Authorlessness (apauruṣeyatva)** means that the Veda is not the
composition of any human or divine person. It is supported by the **eternality of the word
(śabda-nityatva)** and by the doctrine that the relation between word (**śabda**) and meaning
(**artha**) is **natural or inherent (autpattika)** rather than a convention (**saṅketa**) instituted
by a person. Utterance therefore *manifests* rather than *produces* the word.

#### SIMPLE START — THE GATEWAY IDEA

Every other route to scriptural authority runs through a person: this prophet was inspired, this
teacher was omniscient, this God does not lie. And every such route has the same weak joint — you
must first establish the person's credentials, and you must establish them without using the
scripture whose authority is in question.

Mīmāṃsā takes the opposite road. It removes the person entirely.

Ask the Mīmāṃsaka *why* the Veda is reliable and he will not praise anyone. He will say: reliability
fails when a speaker is ignorant, deceitful, partial or forgetful; there is no speaker here; so that
entire class of failure does not arise.

⚠️ It is a defence by *subtraction*, and it is why Session 18's atheism is not an embarrassment to
this school but a requirement of it. Add God as author and you have re-installed exactly the weak
joint you removed.

#### VISUAL — THE ARGUMENT, THE CIRCULARITY CHARGE, AND SIX REPLIES

```text
   ══════════ THE EIGHT-STEP ARGUMENT FOR AUTHORLESSNESS ══════════
   1. no author of the Veda is REMEMBERED in the tradition
   2. an author of so momentous a text would NORMALLY have been remembered
   3. the word–meaning relation is NATURAL (autpattika), not newly instituted by a person
   4. word-units (varṇa) have ETERNALITY (śabda-nityatva) ⇒ utterance MANIFESTS, not produces
   5. transmission has been maintained by disciplined recitation; no event of composition is
      located
   6. ∴ the Veda need not be treated as a composed book with a fallible author
   7. authorial defects explain unreliability in human testimony
   8. ∴ an authorless Veda is free of that class of defect ⇒ SELF-VALID FOR DHARMA

   WHY AUTHORLESSNESS MATTERS — the four-defect argument
     a personal author MAY ERR · MAY DECEIVE · MAY LACK COMPLETE KNOWLEDGE
     ⇒ authority would depend on PROVING competence and sincerity
     ⇒ authorlessness removes the dependence ⇒ validity is intrinsic

   AGAINST NYĀYA'S GOD-GUARANTEE — the three-stage burden
     first prove God exists → then prove God is omniscient → then prove God intends truth
     each stage needs INDEPENDENT support ⇒ cumbersome ⇒ authorlessness avoids it

   ══════════ THE CIRCULARITY CHARGE, IN TWO FORMS ══════════
   ❌ FORM 1: the Veda is authoritative because authorless — but authorlessness and the claim
              that dharma is unknowable otherwise seem accepted BECAUSE THE TRADITION SAYS SO.
   ❌ FORM 2 (sharper): intrinsic validity PROVES TOO MUCH — if every cognition is
              intrinsically valid, RIVAL SCRIPTURES become self-valid too.
                                        │
   ══════════ SIX REPLIES ══════════    ▼
   ✅ R1  authorlessness rests on ORDINARY REASONING + the absence of any remembered author,
          not on a Vedic quotation
   ✅ R2  the reasoning has a POSTULATION (arthāpatti) FORM: extraordinary authority + no
          remembered authorial event ⇒ authorless transmission is the required postulate
   ✅ R3  intrinsic validity ≠ indefeasibility; invalidity is known through defect (doṣa) or
          a sublating cognition (bādhaka)
   ✅ R4  rival scriptures WITH known or claimed human authors possess a POSSIBLE LOCUS OF
          DEFECT; an authorless Veda lacks that locus
   ✅ R5  the Veda is authoritative ONLY FOR DHARMA, where no rival instrument operates; it
          does not compete with perception about colour or inference about fire
   ✅ R6  the DIVINE-AUTHOR alternative is WORSE: a person, even a divine person, becomes a
          further object needing proof, and his omniscience and disinterest would themselves
          require support

   ══════════ RESIDUAL FORCE — state this in a critical answer ══════════
   ⚠ the reply loads great weight on "no remembered author" and on the NATURALNESS of the
     word–meaning relation
   ⚠ Nyāya (Jayanta, Udayana) deny naturalness: the relation is CONVENTION (saṅketa),
     ultimately instituted by Īśvara, and the Veda is the testimony of a trustworthy
     authority (āpta)
   ⚠ Buddhists in the Dharmakīrti line reject eternal words and ask how an eternal,
     changeless word could produce a cognition AT A PARTICULAR TIME
     ✅ Mīmāṃsā answer: the eternal word is not newly produced; it is MANIFESTED by utterance
        under auditory conditions, so the timed cognition depends on manifestation and
        hearer-conditions, not on creation of the word
```

*What the diagram shows: the positive argument, the two supporting arguments, the charge in both its
forms, six numbered replies, and the residual pressure — which together constitute a complete
answer to the 2019 question at any length.*

#### CORE UPSC — THE ARGUMENT IN FULL

1. **The thesis.** ✅ The Veda is authoritative because it is authorless, eternal and self-valid.

2. **Why authorlessness is the right ground.** ✅ Mīmāṃsā grounds Vedic authority in authorlessness
   because a text without an author has no locus for ignorance, deceit, partiality or
   forgetfulness. ✅ The doctrine is central because the Veda reveals duty, a supersensible object
   unavailable to perception, inference or ordinary testimony. ⚠️ The Veda is therefore authoritative
   **not because a perfect speaker guarantees it, but because it is impersonal, eternal and free from
   authorial defect.**

3. **The eight-step argument.** (i) No author is remembered in the tradition. (ii) A human author of
   so momentous a text would normally have been remembered. (iii) The word–meaning relation is
   natural (**autpattika**), not a convention newly instituted by a person. (iv) For Bhāṭṭa Mīmāṃsā,
   words as sound-burst-independent phonemes and the relevant universals of wordhood have eternality
   (**śabda-nityatva**), so utterance manifests rather than produces the word. (v) Vedic transmission
   has been maintained through disciplined recitation, and no original event of composition is
   located. (vi) Therefore the Veda need not be treated as a composed book with a fallible author.
   (vii) Since authorial defects explain unreliability in human testimony, (viii) an authorless Veda
   is free from that class of defects — hence Vedic testimony is self-valid in its proper domain,
   namely duty.

4. **The presuppositions.** ✅ A text can be meaningful without a historical author if word-meaning
   relations are eternal and impersonal. ✅ The absence of a remembered author is philosophically
   relevant, because an author of the Veda would not be a trivial forgotten artisan. ✅ Duty is
   supersensible, so the Veda's authority is restricted to a domain not otherwise known.

5. **The distinction that prevents an obvious misreading.** ⚠️ Authorlessness is **not** the claim
   that no one ever recited, taught or arranged Vedic branches. It is the claim that transmission and
   recension are not original authorship. ✅ Names such as **Kaṭha** and **Kauṣītaki** can mark
   schools, transmitters or branches without proving personal composition of Vedic content. ✅
   Example: a teacher who recites a mantra is a transmitter; Mīmāṃsā denies that such transmission
   makes the teacher the author of the mantra's Vedic authority.

6. **The four-defect argument for why authorlessness matters.** ✅ A personal author may err; may
   deceive; may lack complete knowledge. ✅ If the Veda had an author, its authority would depend on
   proving that author's competence and sincerity. ✅ By being authorless it is free from these
   defects, and hence its validity is intrinsic.

7. **Against Nyāya's God-guarantee.** ✅ Mīmāṃsā objects that Nyāya's route is cumbersome: first
   prove God exists, then prove God is omniscient, then prove God intends truth — each stage needing
   independent support. ✅ Authorlessness avoids the dependence entirely.

8. **The circularity charge, in both forms, and the six replies.** ❌ *Form 1:* the defence looks
   circular, since authorlessness and the unknowability of duty by other means seem accepted because
   the tradition says so. ❌ *Form 2, sharper:* intrinsic validity proves too much — if every
   cognition is intrinsically valid, rival scriptures also become self-valid unless an external
   criterion is smuggled in.
   ✅ *Reply 1:* authorlessness is presented as supported by ordinary reasoning plus the absence of
   any remembered author, not as a conclusion quoted from the Veda.
   ✅ *Reply 2:* the reasoning has a **postulation (arthāpatti)** form — given a tradition of
   extraordinary authority and no remembered authorial event, authorless transmission is posited as
   the required explanation.
   ✅ *Reply 3:* intrinsic validity does not mean every cognition is finally indefeasible; it means
   validity needs no positive external certificate, while invalidity is known through defect
   (**doṣa**) or a sublating cognition (**bādhaka**).
   ✅ *Reply 4:* rival scriptures with known or claimed human authors possess a possible locus of
   defect, whereas an authorless Veda lacks that locus.
   ✅ *Reply 5:* the Veda is authoritative only for **duty**, where no rival instrument operates; it
   does not compete with perception on colour or with inference on smoke and fire.
   ✅ *Reply 6:* the divine-author alternative is worse for Mīmāṃsā, because a person — even a divine
   person — becomes a further object needing proof, and the alleged omniscience and freedom from
   self-interest of that person would themselves require support from some instrument.

9. **The residual force, which a critical answer must state.** ⚠️ The reply pushes significant weight
   onto "no remembered author" and onto the naturalness of the word–meaning relation. ⚠️ Nyāya
   thinkers such as **Jayanta** and **Udayana** deny naturalness, treating the relation as convention
   (**saṅketa**) ultimately grounded in Īśvara's institution, and reading the Veda as the testimony
   of a trustworthy authority (**āpta**). ⚠️ Buddhist critics in the **Dharmakīrti** line reject
   eternal words and ask how an eternal, changeless word could produce a cognition at a particular
   time.

10. **The four rejoinders to those attacks.**
    ❌ *Nyāya:* word-meaning is conventional; God institutes the convention and guarantees the Veda
    as its trustworthy author. ✅ *Reply:* conventionalism makes meaning depend on an institutor whose
    existence and reliability must first be proved; the natural relation explains linguistic
    stability without that extra theological burden.
    ❌ *Nyāya:* branch-names such as Kaṭha and Kauṣītaki show human authorship. ✅ *Reply:* such names
    can indicate reciters, teachers or schools of transmission, not composers of Vedic sentences.
    ❌ *Buddhist:* an eternal word cannot cause a timed cognition, because what is changeless cannot
    suddenly produce an effect. ✅ *Reply:* the eternal word is not newly produced but *manifested* by
    utterance under appropriate auditory conditions, so the timed cognition depends on manifestation
    and hearer-conditions, not on the creation of the word.
    ❌ *Buddhist:* if all authority is defended through intrinsic validity, sectarian texts can make
    the same claim. ✅ *Reply:* intrinsic validity is defeasible; a text with an author, a
    contradiction or a detectable defect can be defeated, whereas the Veda's domain is precisely the
    supersensible domain in which perception and inference supply no defeater.

11. **The eternality of the word, stated compactly.** ✅ Words are eternal in the relevant Mīmāṃsā
    sense; utterance manifests them rather than creates them; Vedic revelation is therefore not
    dependent on temporal composition.

12. **The final formula.** ✅ Presupposition: duty is supersensible. ✅ Distinction: Vedic authority
    rests on authorlessness, not on speaker intention. ✅ Example: ritual obligations and unseen
    fruits lie beyond perception. ❌ *Objection:* communication requires a speaker's intention. ✅
    *Reply:* Mīmāṃsā grounds meaning in the eternal relation of word and meaning, not in a historical
    author's psychology. ✅ **Conclusion: the Veda is self-valid because it is eternal, authorless and
    uniquely revelatory of duty.**

#### NAMED EVIDENCE YOU CAN CITE

- ✅ The eight-step argument for authorlessness and the four-defect argument for why it matters.
- ✅ The **Kaṭha** and **Kauṣītaki** branch-names, used as the standard reply to the human-authorship
  inference.
- ✅ The three-stage burden Mīmāṃsā attributes to the Nyāya God-guarantee.
- ✅ The six numbered replies to the circularity charge, including the postulation-form of Reply 2.
- ⚠️ **Jayanta** and **Udayana** on convention (**saṅketa**) and on the Veda as an
  **āpta**-testimony; the **Dharmakīrti** line on timed cognition from an eternal word.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Every rival grounds a scripture by praising somebody; Mīmāṃsā grounds it by finding nobody — and
> its wager is that an absent author is a safer foundation than a perfect one, because perfection has
> to be proved and absence only has to be undisturbed.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

| Keyword | Why the examiner wants it |
|---|---|
| **authorlessness (apauruṣeyatva)** | The thesis |
| **eternality of the word (śabda-nityatva)** | The supporting doctrine |
| **natural relation (autpattika)** | Why meaning needs no institutor |
| **convention (saṅketa)** | The Nyāya alternative, correctly named |
| **trustworthy authority (āpta)** | Nyāya's route to Vedic reliability |
| **defect (doṣa) / defeater (bādhaka)** | How invalidity is detected |
| **manifested, not produced** | The reply to the Buddhist timing objection |
| **domain restriction to dharma** | Reply 5, the strongest anti-proliferation move |

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — CLAIM -> NAMED EVIDENCE -> ANALYSIS -> QUALIFICATION

> **Claim.** Mīmāṃsā establishes Vedic authority by removing its author, on the ground that
> unreliability in testimony is traceable to defects that can exist only in a person.
>
> **Named evidence.** The school argues that no author of the Veda is remembered although such an
> author would have been; that the word–meaning relation is natural (**autpattika**) rather than an
> instituted convention; that word-units possess eternality (**śabda-nityatva**) so that utterance
> merely manifests them; and that a personal author may err, deceive or lack complete knowledge, so
> that Nyāya's route must first prove God's existence, then his omniscience, then his truthfulness.
>
> **Analysis.** The defence is stronger than it first appears because it is restricted: the Veda is
> claimed to be self-valid only for duty, where no other instrument operates and therefore no
> defeater can arise, which simultaneously blocks the charge that intrinsic validity would license
> every rival scripture — those rivals have authors, and an author is precisely a locus of defect
> and a target for defeat.
>
> **Qualification.** ⚠️ The argument nevertheless rests heavily on two contested props — the absence
> of a remembered author and the naturalness of the word–meaning relation — and both are denied by
> Nyāya, which treats the relation as divinely instituted convention, and by the Dharmakīrti line,
> which rejects eternal words altogether; the honest verdict is that Mīmāṃsā's position is coherent
> within its own presuppositions rather than compelling against those who reject them.

#### EXAM APPLICATION — WHERE THIS HAS BEEN ASKED

- **2019, Q5(e), 10 marks** — "How does Mīmāṃsā establish the authority of Vedic knowledge?" A
  ten-mark answer needs the eight-step argument compressed to four, the four-defect argument, and one
  line of residual pressure.
- Supports **2018, Q8(a)** and the whole of Session 18, since the atheism follows from this defence
  rather than sitting beside it.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Writing that the Veda is "God-given" or "revealed by God." For Mīmāṃsā it is authorless — this
   is the single most damaging error available on this topic.
2. ⚠️ Treating authorlessness as denying that anyone recited or transmitted the Veda.
3. ⚠️ Omitting the domain restriction, which is what stops the doctrine from validating every
   scripture.
4. ⚠️ Attributing the convention theory (**saṅketa**) to Mīmāṃsā; it is the Nyāya position.
5. ⚠️ Forgetting that Reply 2 is itself an application of postulation — a link worth a mark.
6. ⚠️ Presenting the Buddhist timing objection without the "manifested, not produced" reply.

#### RAPID REVISION

- Thesis: authorless (**apauruṣeyatva**) ⇒ no locus of defect ⇒ self-valid for duty.
- Supports: eternal word (**śabda-nityatva**), natural relation (**autpattika**), no remembered
  author, unlocated composition.
- Against Nyāya: three-stage burden — exists, omniscient, truthful.
- Circularity: two forms; six replies; strongest are the postulation-form (R2) and the domain
  restriction (R5).
- Residual: Jayanta/Udayana on convention; Dharmakīrti on timed cognition — answered by
  "manifested, not produced."

---
### SESSION 17 — INJUNCTION, IMPELLING FORCE, OBLIGATION, UNSEEN POTENCY AND DUTY

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition.** The Veda's characteristic sentence is not "this is the case" but "do
this." Mīmāṃsā therefore needs an account of what makes a sentence a *command* rather than a report,
what makes a command *bind* someone who has no commander in front of him, and how an act performed
today can pay out a fruit long after the act has ended.

**Technical definition.** The Veda's primary import is **injunction (vidhi / codanā)**. Its
meaning-content, on the Bhāṭṭa analysis, is **impelling force (bhāvanā)** — the efficient force of
bringing something into being, expressed by the verbal ending — which divides into **verbal impelling
force (śābdī bhāvanā)** and **objective productive force (ārthī bhāvanā)**, each requiring the three
factors (**aṃśa-traya**). Prabhākara replaces this doubling with a directly apprehended **to-be-done
(kārya) / obligation (niyoga)**. The bridge from a perishing act to a distant fruit is the **unseen
potency (apūrva)**.

#### SIMPLE START — THE GATEWAY IDEA

Read the school's standard test sentence aloud: **"svargakāmo yajeta"** — *"one desiring heaven
should sacrifice."*

Three puzzles are packed into those two words.

*First:* what turns this into a command? No one is commanding. There is no speaker to be obeyed.
Bhāṭṭa's answer: the imperative force sits in the verbal form itself, whose relation to its meaning
is natural rather than instituted. The sentence impels because of what it *is*, not because of who
said it.

*Second:* why would anyone obey? Bhāṭṭa answers: because you want heaven, and the sentence tells you
that sacrifice produces it. Prabhākara answers: because it is enjoined, full stop — the desire is not
the ground of the obligation.

*Third:* how can a rite performed on a Tuesday produce heaven decades later? The flames do not travel.
Mīmāṃsā answers with **unseen potency (apūrva)**: the act generates a persisting potency that matures
into the fruit. ⚠️ And notice the method — that potency is not asserted, it is *postulated*, by the
very instrument defended in Session 9.

#### VISUAL — FROM SENTENCE TO FRUIT, WITH THE TWO RIVAL ACCOUNTS

```text
   ══════════ THE VEDIC SENTENCE TAXONOMY (five functions) ══════════
   ┌────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────┐
   │ injunction (vidhi)         │ enjoins a duty or rite not other-   │ PRIMARY REVEALER  │
   │                            │ wise known                          │ OF DHARMA         │
   │ prohibition (niṣedha)      │ forbids an act, grounds avoidance   │ negative duty     │
   │ mantra (mantra)            │ recalls deity, material, detail     │ assists a rite    │
   │                            │ during performance                  │                   │
   │ name (nāmadheya)           │ names a rite, substance, component  │ fixes ritual      │
   │                            │                                     │ identification    │
   │ praise/explanation         │ praises, condemns, narrates or      │ force through     │
   │ (arthavāda)                │ explains in support of a vidhi      │ relation to vidhi │
   └────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────┘
   ✅ Do NOT dismiss mantra, name and arthavāda as meaningless: their Vedic function is
      secured by SENTENCE-UNITY (ekavākyatā) with an operative injunction.

   SECOND CLASSIFICATION — three logical kinds of injunction
     apūrva-vidhi      (novel)       reveals an otherwise UNKNOWN duty
     niyama-vidhi      (restrictive) SELECTS one eligible means where alternatives appear
     parisaṃkhyā-vidhi (exclusionary) EXCLUDES alternatives
   ⚠ THIRD, SEPARATE GRID — ritual coordination of a complex rite; do NOT conflate with the above
     utpatti (the act) · viniyoga (subsidiary application) · prayoga (performance-order)
     · adhikāra (qualified agent)

   ══════════ BHĀṬṬA: THE DOUBLE FORCE ══════════
   "svargakāmo yajeta"
        │
        ├── ŚĀBDĪ BHĀVANĀ  (locus: the Vedic sentence / injunction)
        │      word-force IMPELS the hearer ⇒ immediate result: PRAVṚTTI (impulse to act)
        │      aṃśa-traya:  kim? → the agent's pravṛtti      (what is to be brought about)
        │                   kena? → the injunctive suffix + knowledge of the injunction
        │                   katham? → arthavāda and eulogistic supports strengthen it
        │
        └── ĀRTHĪ BHĀVANĀ  (locus: the agent's own effort and ritual performance)
               action BRINGS ABOUT the result through the proper means
               ⇒ immediate result: PHALA (e.g. svarga), mediated by apūrva where needed
               aṃśa-traya:  kim? → SVARGA          (what is to be brought about)
                            kena? → YĀGA, sacrifice (the means)
                            katham? → procedure, materials, sequence, subsidiary acts
   ⇒ WORD-FORCE PRODUCES UNDERTAKING; ACTION-FORCE PRODUCES FRUIT.

   ══════════ PRĀBHĀKARA: THE SINGLE BINDING ══════════
   the sentence directly presents an act as TO-BE-DONE (kārya) ⇒ OBLIGATION (niyoga)
   bindingness does NOT wait upon attraction toward svarga
   ⇒ PRĀBHĀKARA = NIYOGA-VĀDA (duty for duty's sake)
     BHĀṬṬA      = BHĀVANĀ-VĀDA (injunction operates through desire for a result)
   ⚠ Western comparison flag: niyoga may be compared CAUTIOUSLY with Kant's categorical
     imperative — both separate duty from desired consequences — but niyoga is grounded in an
     impersonal eternal Veda and applies only to the ritually qualified agent (adhikārin).

   ══════════ THE BRIDGE: UNSEEN POTENCY (apūrva) ══════════
   ritual act is TEMPORALLY LIMITED  →  promised fruit is TEMPORALLY REMOTE
   if the act simply perished without remainder, the Vedic connection would be unintelligible
   ⇒ POSTULATE apūrva as the unseen link  ⇒ established by ARTHĀPATTI (Session 9)
   ⇒ THE FLAMES DO NOT TRAVEL TO HEAVEN; THE POTENCY PERSISTS UNTIL THE FRUIT MATURES.

   ══════════ HERMENEUTIC CANONS (comparative note) ══════════
   ⚠ The tradition's rule-governed interpretation is standardly presented through the
     strength-ordering śruti–liṅga–vākya–prakaraṇa–sthāna–samākhyā and the general/exception
     pair utsarga–apavāda. In this repository these canons are held as a CONTROLLED COMPARISON
     with rule-governed legal interpretation, with the express disanalogy that Mīmāṃsā
     presupposes an INFALLIBLE AUTHORLESS text and admits NO amendment by legislative intention.
     Use them as a named comparison, not as a doctrinal list to be expanded.
```

*What the diagram shows: three separate classifications kept apart, the two rival theories of
injunctive force with their three-factor analyses, the postulated bridge to the fruit, and the
hermeneutic canons flagged at exactly the level of support the sources give them.*

#### CORE UPSC — THE ARGUMENT IN FULL

1. **Why injunction is the semantic heart.** ✅ For Mīmāṃsā the primary import of the Veda is not
   description but injunction. ✅ Because the Veda's whole purpose is **codanā** and its subject is
   **dharma**, impelling force is the semantic heart of the system. ⚠️ An injunction is therefore not
   merely a sentence *about* an action; it is a sentence that makes an action *obligatory for a
   qualified agent*.

2. **The six-step argument.** (i) Duty is supersensible and cannot be known by perception or ordinary
   inference. (ii) The Veda reveals duty through action-guiding sentences. (iii) A merely descriptive
   sentence would tell what is already the case, not what ought to be done. (iv) Therefore the
   decisive Vedic sentence-type is the injunction. (v) The force that converts a sentence into an
   action-guiding demand is **bhāvanā**. (vi) Hence Mīmāṃsā semantics, ritual theory and epistemology
   converge in the analysis of injunction.

3. **The presupposition that makes an authorless command possible.** ✅ Vedic meaning is primarily
   prescriptive, and an authorless text can bind because obligation flows from the *injunctive
   structure*, not from a personal commander.
   ❌ *Objection:* a sentence without a personal speaker cannot command anyone. ✅ *Reply:* imperative
   force is carried by the optative/injunctive verbal form (**liṅ / vidhi**) itself, whose relation to
   its meaning is natural (**autpattika**); the obligation is impersonal and structural, not the will
   of a person.

4. **Bhāṭṭa's two-fold analysis.** ✅ **Verbal impelling force (śābdī bhāvanā)** has the Vedic
   sentence as its locus; the word-force impels the hearer to act, and its immediate result is
   **pravṛtti**, the volitional impulse to act in the qualified agent. ✅ **Objective productive force
   (ārthī bhāvanā)** has the agent's own effort and ritual performance as its locus; the action brings
   about the desired result through the proper means, and its immediate result is **phala** such as
   heaven, mediated by unseen potency where needed.

5. **The seven-step justification for doubling the force.** (i) An injunction has a linguistic side:
   the sentence must be understood as *requiring* action. (ii) This side belongs to the word or verbal
   ending and is therefore called verbal impelling force. (iii) Understanding the injunction produces
   the impulse in the qualified agent. (iv) But mere linguistic impulse does not itself produce heaven
   or any ritual result. (v) The agent must perform the act with the prescribed materials, sequence
   and subsidiaries. (vi) This real activity is objective productive force. (vii) Therefore the
   analysis doubles the productive force: word-force produces undertaking; action-force produces the
   fruit.
   ❌ *Objection:* the two-fold structure is artificial, since one injunction seems to convey one act.
   ✅ *Reply:* the distinction is needed because linguistic impelling and real production are not the
   same function.

6. **The three factors (aṃśa-traya).** ✅ Every impelling force requires **what is to be brought about
   (sādhya / bhāvya)**, the **means (karaṇa / sādhana)**, and **how it is to be done
   (itikartavyatā)** — answering *kim?*, *kena?* and *katham?* ✅ For objective force in "svargakāmo
   yajeta": heaven is the result, sacrifice is the means, and ritual procedure, materials, sequence
   and subsidiary acts supply the "how." ✅ For verbal force in the same injunction: the agent's
   impulse is to be brought about, the injunctive suffix and the knowledge of the injunction are the
   means, and explanatory/eulogistic support strengthens and specifies the undertaking.

7. **Why the three factors matter beyond grammar.** ✅ No action-guiding sentence is complete unless
   it indicates what is to be achieved, by what means, and by what procedure. ✅ The frame lets
   Mīmāṃsā connect grammar, ritual detail and obligation in a single hermeneutic scheme.
   ❌ *Objection:* praise passages seem meaningful on their own as praise, story or explanation. ✅
   *Reply:* their Vedic relevance is secured through sentence-unity with an injunction; they are
   meaningful *as subordinate to* injunction. ✅ Example: a passage glorifying a rite does not
   independently create duty; it supports the relevant injunction by generating confidence, attention
   or motivation.

8. **Prabhākara's alternative, in five steps.** ✅ He rejects the Bhāṭṭa doubling and holds that the
   injunctive content is **to-be-done / obligation (kārya / niyoga)**, directly apprehended from the
   injunction and binding on the qualified agent. (i) The qualified agent hears the injunction. (ii)
   The sentence directly presents an act as to-be-done. (iii) The bindingness does not wait upon
   attraction toward heaven or any other fruit. (iv) Therefore obligation arises from **niyoga**
   itself. (v) This gives Prabhākara a rigorously deontological structure within ritual hermeneutics.
   ❌ *Objection:* if the fruit does not motivate, why would the agent act at all? ✅ *Reply:* the
   cognition of the act as enjoined is itself action-guiding for the qualified agent.

9. **The doctrinal contrast to write out.** ⚠️ **Prābhākara = niyoga-vāda**, duty for duty's sake;
   **Bhāṭṭa = bhāvanā-vāda**, in which the injunction operates through desire for a result. ⚠️ This
   is a genuine Indian anticipation of the deontology/consequentialism contrast, and the Kantian
   comparison is legitimate *only* with its disanalogy attached: **niyoga** is grounded in an
   impersonal eternal Veda and applies to ritually qualified agents, whereas Kant grounds obligation
   in rational autonomy and universalisability.

10. **The subordination of non-injunctive material.** ✅ Praise (**arthavāda**), mantra and name
    (**nāmadheya**) are subordinate to injunctions, gaining Vedic function through sentence-unity with
    a **vidhi**. ✅ The argument: the Veda's central purpose is to reveal duty; duty is action-guiding;
    therefore sentences that do not directly enjoin must be related to sentences that do.
    ❌ *Objection:* this reduces rich Vedic discourse to ritual command. ✅ *Reply:* Mīmāṃsā accepts
    the cost, because its project is not literary appreciation but the discovery of duty.

11. **The Vedānta seam — where the subordination is challenged.** ✅ The sharp doctrinal seam appears
    over Upaniṣadic sentences such as **"tat tvam asi."** ✅ *Mīmāṃsā's prior position:* the Veda's
    purpose is duty; duty is known through injunction; a sentence that does not guide action seems
    purposeless in the Vedic economy; therefore Upaniṣadic statements must either enjoin
    meditation/knowledge-related discipline or serve an injunctional purpose. ✅ *Advaita's
    counter-position:* knowledge is not an action and cannot be produced by command as sacrifice can;
    Brahman is not a future product but an accomplished reality; therefore sentences revealing Brahman
    can be independently valid as disclosure of an **accomplished fact (siddha-vastu)**, and the
    Upaniṣads need not be subordinated to ritual injunction. ✅ *Mīmāṃsā's reply back:* Vedic
    purposiveness is unintelligible unless scriptural sentences are action-guiding. ⚠️ *Advaita's
    rejoinder:* revelation of an accomplished reality is itself a valid scriptural purpose. ⚠️ The
    disagreement turns on whether Vedic meaningfulness requires action-production or can include
    direct metaphysical disclosure — and this is the doctrinal seam between the two Mīmāṃsās.

12. **Ethics in the narrow sense.** ✅ Mīmāṃsā epistemology culminates in duty: the point of knowing
    is to know what ought to be done. ✅ **Injunction (vidhi)** gives positive command and
    **prohibition (niṣedha)** gives negative command; together they define the normative shape of
    duty. ✅ On motivation, Bhāṭṭa foregrounds the desired fruit while Prābhākara stresses obligation.
    ❌ *Objection:* without God there is no moral authority. ✅ *Reply:* for Mīmāṃsā, Vedic injunction
    and unseen potency are enough to secure binding duty and future fruit.

13. **Unseen potency, argued rather than asserted.** ✅ **Apūrva** is the unseen potency generated by
    ritual action that links a perishing act to a distant fruit; it makes objective productive force
    intelligible across time, because the act ends but its potency persists until the fruit matures.
    ✅ The five-step argument: (i) a ritual act is temporally limited; (ii) its promised fruit may be
    temporally remote and not visibly produced at the moment of action; (iii) if the act simply
    perished without remainder, the Vedic connection between rite and fruit would be unintelligible;
    (iv) therefore Mīmāṃsā posits apūrva as the unseen link; (v) and this postulation is defended
    through **arthāpatti**, because otherwise the scripturally guaranteed connection between a
    perishing act and a later fruit remains inexplicable.
    ✅ Example: a sacrifice performed now can produce a later fruit because the act generates unseen
    potency, not because the visible flames physically travel to heaven.
    ❌ *Objection:* apūrva is unverifiable and looks like an ad hoc posit. ✅ *Reply:* it is not
    arbitrary; it is established by postulation as the necessary link between a transitory ritual act
    and a promised later fruit.

14. **The critical residue.** ⚠️ *Criticism:* impersonal injunction without a commander is
    unintelligible. ✅ *Reply:* Bhāṭṭa locates imperative force in verbal impelling force and the
    injunctive suffix; Prābhākara locates bindingness in the directly apprehended to-be-done. ⚠️ The
    pressure is strongest exactly where Mīmāṃsā tries to subordinate non-injunctive Upaniṣadic
    sentences to injunction — which is why Session 19's verdict treats this as a live weakness rather
    than a settled victory.

#### NAMED EVIDENCE YOU CAN CITE

- ✅ **"svargakāmo yajeta"** as the standard test injunction, worked through both three-factor grids.
- ✅ The five-function Vedic sentence taxonomy and the three logical kinds of injunction
  (**apūrva-vidhi**, **niyama-vidhi**, **parisaṃkhyā-vidhi**), kept separate from the ritual
  coordination grid (**utpatti, viniyoga, prayoga, adhikāra**).
- ✅ The Bhāṭṭa two-fold force with **pravṛtti** and **phala** as their respective immediate results.
- ✅ Prabhākara's **kārya / niyoga** and the **niyoga-vāda** versus **bhāvanā-vāda** contrast.
- ✅ The Advaita objection over **"tat tvam asi"** and **siddha-vastu**, with the Mīmāṃsā reply.
- ✅ Unseen potency established by postulation, with the flames-do-not-travel illustration.
- ⚠️ The hermeneutic canons **śruti–liṅga–vākya–prakaraṇa–sthāna–samākhyā** and **utsarga–apavāda**
  are held in the sources as a controlled comparison with rule-governed legal interpretation, with
  the disanalogy that Mīmāṃsā presupposes an infallible authorless text admitting no amendment by
  legislative intention. Name them at that level of support and do not expand them into a doctrinal
  syllabus of their own.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Mīmāṃsā's boldest claim is that a sentence can obligate without anyone meaning it and an act can
> pay without anyone paying: the imperative force sits in the verbal form and the reward sits in an
> unseen potency, so the entire moral machinery runs with the commander and the paymaster both
> removed.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

| Keyword | Why the examiner wants it |
|---|---|
| **injunction (vidhi / codanā)** | The Veda's primary sentence-type |
| **impelling force (bhāvanā)** | Bhāṭṭa's analysis of injunctive content |
| **verbal (śābdī) / objective (ārthī) impelling force** | The two-fold division |
| **impulse (pravṛtti) / fruit (phala)** | Their two immediate results |
| **three factors (aṃśa-traya): sādhya · karaṇa · itikartavyatā** | The *kim / kena / katham* grid |
| **obligation (niyoga) / to-be-done (kārya)** | Prabhākara's alternative |
| **niyoga-vāda versus bhāvanā-vāda** | The deontology/consequentialism contrast |
| **unseen potency (apūrva)** | The bridge, established by postulation |
| **praise (arthavāda) subordinated by sentence-unity** | Prevents treating the Veda as literature |
| **qualified agent (adhikārin)** | The addressee of the command |

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — CLAIM -> NAMED EVIDENCE -> ANALYSIS -> QUALIFICATION

> **Claim.** Mīmāṃsā converts its epistemology into an ethics by locating obligation in the
> injunctive structure of the sentence rather than in the will of any commander.
>
> **Named evidence.** In "svargakāmo yajeta" Kumārila distinguishes a verbal impelling force whose
> result is the agent's impulse (**pravṛtti**) from an objective productive force whose result is the
> fruit (**phala**), each analysed by the three factors of what, by what means and how; Prabhākara
> replaces the doubling with a directly apprehended to-be-done (**kārya / niyoga**) that binds the
> qualified agent independently of desire; and both accept unseen potency (**apūrva**) as the link
> between a perishing rite and a remote fruit, established by postulation.
>
> **Analysis.** The two accounts amount to an internal Indian version of the consequentialist and
> deontological options: Kumārila keeps the desired fruit inside the mechanism of obligation, while
> Prabhākara makes the command self-sufficient — and the difference has practical exegetical
> consequences, since it determines whether a praise passage motivates the act or merely specifies
> it.
>
> **Qualification.** ⚠️ The scheme purchases its independence from a commander at the price of
> subordinating everything non-injunctive to injunction, and that price becomes visible precisely
> where Advaita resists it: if knowledge is not an action, an identity-sentence such as "tat tvam
> asi" cannot be governed by injunctive hermeneutics, and the seam between the two Mīmāṃsās opens
> exactly there.

#### EXAM APPLICATION — WHERE THIS HAS BEEN ASKED

- **2019, Q5(e), 10 marks** — Vedic authority; the injunction-centred reading is why authority
  matters at all.
- Provides the essential background paragraph for any question on duty, ritual efficacy or the
  Mīmāṃsā–Vedānta relation, and supplies the applied case of postulation used in **2022, Q6(c)**.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Conflating the three classifications: five sentence-functions, three logical kinds of
   injunction, and the four-part ritual coordination grid are **three separate schemes**.
2. ⚠️ Reversing the two impelling forces. Verbal force produces the *impulse*; objective force
   produces the *fruit*.
3. ⚠️ Equating **bhāvanā-vāda** with **niyoga-vāda**. They are the two rival accounts, not synonyms.
4. ⚠️ Running the Kant comparison without the disanalogy about heteronomy and ritual qualification.
5. ⚠️ Calling **apūrva** an unargued posit. It is established by **arthāpatti**.
6. ⚠️ Writing that praise passages are meaningless in Mīmāṃsā. They are *subordinate*, not
   meaningless.
7. ⚠️ Expanding the hermeneutic canons into a long doctrinal list; the sources hold them as a
   controlled comparison.

#### RAPID REVISION

- Veda enjoins: five functions; three kinds of injunction; separate four-part coordination grid.
- Bhāṭṭa: **śābdī** ⇒ impulse; **ārthī** ⇒ fruit; both need what/means/how.
- Prābhākara: **kārya / niyoga** binds directly; duty for duty's sake.
- Contrast: **bhāvanā-vāda** (fruit-mediated) versus **niyoga-vāda** (categorical); Kant comparison
  with disanalogy.
- **Apūrva** = unseen link, established by postulation; flames do not travel.
- Seam with Advaita: "tat tvam asi" and accomplished fact (**siddha-vastu**).

---

### SESSION 18 — MĪMĀṂSĀ WITHOUT GOD

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition.** The school that exists to defend the Veda also denies that there is a
creator, and denies that any deity hands out the rewards of ritual. This is not an inconsistency. It
is the same argument as authorlessness, extended one step further.

**Technical definition.** **No-God doctrine (nirīśvaravāda)** is the classical Pūrva-Mīmāṃsā —
especially Kumārila's — rejection of a creator God and of a divine dispenser of ritual fruits
(**karmaphaladātṛ**). ⚠️ The rejection is compatible with Vedic orthodoxy because Mīmāṃsā grounds
scriptural authority in **impersonality**, not in divine authorship — and it does not simply erase
the ritual **deities (devatā)**, which are retained as mantra-denoted entities subordinate to the
sacrifice.

#### SIMPLE START — THE GATEWAY IDEA

Here is the sentence that surprises every candidate the first time: **an orthodox, Veda-affirming,
ritual-performing school is among the most rigorously anti-theistic systems in Indian philosophy.**
